The nonsymmetric shuffle theorem Thanks: Authors were supported by NSF Grants DMS-2154282 and 2452208 (J.B.), DMS-2154281 and 2452209 (J.M.), DMS-2303175 (G.S.) and DMS-1929284 (all authors, in residence at MathematicsCollaborate@ICERM)
J. Blasiak, M. Haiman, J. Morse, A. Pun, G. H. Seelinger

Abstract
. The shuffle conjecture of Haglund et al. expresses the symmetric function ∇ e n \nabla e_{n} as a sum over labeled Dyck paths. Here ∇ \nabla is an operator on symmetric functions defined in terms of its diagonal action on the basis of modified Macdonald polynomials. The shuffle conjecture was later refined by Haglund-Morse-Zabrocki to the compositional shuffle conjecture, expressing ∇ C α \nabla C_{\alpha} as a sum over labeled Dyck paths with touchpoints specified by α \alpha , where C α C_{\alpha} is a compositional Hall-Littlewood polynomial. Carlsson-Mellit settled both versions by developing the theory of a variant of the DAHA called the double Dyck path algebra. In a recent paper, we discovered a notion of nonsymmetric plethsym which led us to a construction of modified nonsymmetric Macdonald polynomials 𝖧 η | λ ​ ( 𝐱 , q , t ) \mathsf{H}_{\eta|\lambda}(\mathbf{x};q,t) . These polynomials Weyl symmetrize to their symmetric counterparts and are conjecturally atom positive. Here we introduce a nonsymmetric version ∇ \boldsymbol{\nabla} of ∇ \nabla , now acting diagonally on the basis given by the functions 𝖧 η | λ ​ ( 𝐱 , q , t ) \mathsf{H}_{\eta|\lambda}(\mathbf{x};q,t) . Weaving together our theory with results of Carlsson-Mellit and Mellit, we establish a nonsymmetric version of the compositional shuffle theorem, which equates ∇ − 1 \boldsymbol{\nabla}^{-1} applied to a nonsymmetric version 𝖢 α \mathsf{C}_{\alpha} of C α C_{\alpha} with a sum over flagged labeled Dyck paths with touchpoints given by α \alpha . This combinatorial sum is conjecturally atom positive, refining the known Schur positivity of its symmetric counterpart.



1.
Introduction

The introduction of Macdonald’s symmetric functions in the 1980s sparked investigations in
many directions and ultimately gave rise to several robust theories. Our focus is on the
unification of two of the major theories.
The first of these emerged from efforts to prove Macdonald’s positivity conjecture, which asserted
the Schur positivity of the plethystically modified
Macdonald polynomials
\tilde{H}_{\mu}({\mathbf{x}};q,t)
.
Garsia and Haiman
[
13
]
approached this problem by constructing modules
\mathcal{M}_{\mu}

whose Frobenius series are the
\tilde{H}_{\mu}
. Their work launched a broad program centered around
the space of diagonal harmonics
{\rm DH}_{n}
,
an
S_{n}
-module containing all the
\mathcal{M}_{\mu}
.

Studies of the Frobenius series
\mathcal{F}({\rm DH}_{n})

drew
on both algebraic and combinatorial
methods. Haiman
[
19
]
proved
that
\mathcal{F}({\rm DH}_{n})=\nabla e_{n}
, where

\nabla
is an operator defined by a diagonal action on

\tilde{H}_{\mu}
. It was later discovered that
\nabla
yields an automorphism of the elliptic Hall algebra
of Burban and Schiffmann
[
9
]
.
Meanwhile,
Haglund et al.
[
15
]

formulated the
shuffle conjecture
, an identity giving a positive expansion of

\nabla e_{n}

in terms of
the combinatorial
LLT basis.
The
shuffle conjecture
took over a decade to prove, during which time a wealth of new conjectures and ideas emerged, significantly broadening the scope of the field. Ultimately, Carlsson and Mellit
[
10
]
established
the
compositional shuffle conjecture
,
a more general formula for
\nabla
applied to any compositional Hall–Littlewood polynomial
conjectured by Haglund-Morse-Zabrocki
[
16
]
.

In parallel with the developments
around modified Macdonald polynomials and diagonal harmonics,
a separate study of nonsymmetric Macdonald polynomials
E_{\alpha}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n};q,t)

(\alpha\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{n})
was initiated by Opdam
[
30
]
, Macdonald
[
27
]
, and Cherednik
[
11
]
.
This nonsymmetric framework drew on techniques from representation theory and topology, and quickly developed into a rich subject in its own right. Notably, it also shed new light on the symmetric theory.
For example, the polynomials
E_{\alpha}
satisfy
a recurrence due to Knop and Sahi
[
24
,
32
]
,
which has no symmetric analog.
Knop
[
24
]
also constructed integral forms
{\mathcal{E}}_{\alpha}
of the nonsymmetric Macdonald polynomials
E_{\alpha}
from which the
symmetric integral forms
can be recovered by symmetrization using Hecke algebra operators.

It was long unclear whether
the theory of plethystically modified Macdonald polynomials
\tilde{H}_{\mu}
could be enriched by lifting it to the nonsymmetric setting.
In
[
7
]
, we constructed such a lift by defining a
nonsymmetric plethysm operator
{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{r}
.
Applying
{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{r}
to a stable version of
the
{\mathcal{E}}_{\alpha}
’s, we constructed
a family of modified
r
-nonsymmetric Macdonald polynomials,

\mathsf{H}_{\eta|\lambda}({\mathbf{x}};q,t)
,
which satisfy properties bearing a striking resemblance to
those
of the symmetric polynomials
\tilde{H}_{\mu}
.
In particular,
the
\mathsf{H}_{\eta|\lambda}

are monomial positive and are conjecturally positive in the Demazure atom basis
1
1

1

More precisely, we conjecture that the
\mathsf{H}_{\eta|\lambda}(x_{1},\dots,x_{N},0,\dots;q,t)
are atom positive. See §
8.5
. The same comment applies to other atom positivity conjectures mentioned before §
1.2
.
.
Moreover,
\mathsf{H}_{\eta|\lambda}
admits a combinatorial expansion as a positive sum of
flagged LLT polynomials
,
a nonsymmetric variant of LLT polynomials.

Here we develop
a nonsymmetric diagonal harmonics program
by reinterpreting key ideas from Carlsson-Mellit
[
10
]

in the new framework of modified polynomials
\mathsf{H}_{\eta|\lambda}
from
[
7
]
.
Carlsson and Mellit introduce an algebra
{\mathbb{A}}_{t,q}
, called the double Dyck path algebra.
They
deduce the compositional shuffle theorem by applying an operator

d_{-}\in{\mathbb{A}}_{t,q}
to
an identity which is in effect a nonsymmetric lift of this theorem.
A key ingredient in
their setup
is a family of
nonsymmetric polynomials
that are used as intermediate steps in building LLT polynomials. One of our main insights
here
is that
the images
of these intermediate objects
under the nonsymmetric plethysm
{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{r}

are precisely flagged LLT polynomials.
This perspective leads us to a nonsymmetric
\nabla
operator that acts diagonally on the modified polynomials
\mathsf{H}_{\eta|\lambda}({\mathbf{x}};q^{-1},t)
.
Pulling these ideas together, we
obtain a nonsymmetric compositional shuffle theorem fully parallel to the one in the symmetric case, expressed in terms of combinatorial objects
with conjectural atom positivity,
refining the known Schur positivity of their symmetric counterparts.

1.1.
Main theorems

We now give an account of our main results, deferring a few definitions to later in the paper.

The compositional shuffle theorem,
conjectured by Haglund-Morse-Zabrocki
[
16
]
and proved by Carlsson-Mellit
[
10
]
,
states that for any strict composition
\alpha=(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{r})
of size
k
(i.e.,
\alpha\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{+}^{r}
and
\sum_{i=1}^{r}\alpha_{i}=k
),

(1)

\displaystyle\nabla C_{\alpha}({\mathbf{x}};t)=\sum_{\pi\in{\mathbb{D}}^{\alpha}_{k,k}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{word parking}\\
\text{functions $w$ on $\pi$}\end{subarray}}q^{\area(\pi)}t^{\dinv(\pi,w)}{\mathbf{x}}^{\content(w)}.

On the algebraic
left hand
side,
C_{\alpha}({\mathbf{x}};t)
is the compositional Hall-Littlewood polynomial from
[
16
]
.
On the combinatorial
right hand side,

{\mathbb{D}}^{\alpha}_{k,k}
denotes the set of Dyck paths from
(0,0)
to
(k,k)

which touch the diagonal exactly at the points

(k_{j},k_{j})
, where
k_{j}=\sum_{i<j}\alpha_{i}
for
j=1,\ldots,r+1
;
a
word parking function
on
\pi
is a map from the north steps of

\pi
to
{\mathbb{Z}}_{+}
which is strictly increasing going north along vertical runs;

\area(\pi)
is the number of full lattice squares between
\pi
and the diagonal, and
\dinv
is another integer statistic on Dyck paths
(see §
5.1
and (
192
)).

The right hand side of (
1
) can be packaged as a sum of LLT polynomials for columns, and these are known to be Schur positive. As such,

(2)

\displaystyle\nabla C_{\alpha}\text{ is Schur positive.}

Our nonsymmetric compositional shuffle theorem takes place in the space of
almost symmetric polynomials

\mathcal{P}_{\rm as}=\bigcup_{r\geq 0}\mathcal{P}(r)
, where

\mathcal{P}(r)={\mathbb{Q}}(q,t)[x_{1},\dots,x_{r}]\otimes\Lambda_{{\mathbb{Q}}(q,t)}(x_{r+1},\dots)
.
We work with a
variant of the modified
r
-nonsymmetric Macdonald polynomials introduced in
[
7
]
, namely,

\check{\mathsf{H}}_{\eta|\lambda}({\mathbf{x}};q,t)=q^{\mathsf{n}(\mu^{*})}\mathsf{H}_{\eta|\lambda}({\mathbf{x}};q^{-1},t)\in\mathcal{P}_{\rm as}
,
where
\mu=(\eta;\lambda)_{+}
is the partition rearrangement of the concatenation of
\eta
and
\lambda
.
The
\check{\mathsf{H}}_{\eta|\lambda}
are indexed by pairs
(\eta|\lambda)
, where
\eta\in{\mathbb{N}}^{r}
with

\eta_{r}>0
, and
\lambda
a partition.
We show that
\check{\mathsf{H}}_{\eta|\lambda}
Weyl symmetrizes to

\omega\tilde{H}_{(\eta;\lambda)_{+}}({\mathbf{x}};q,t)
.

Here, we define a nonsymmetric version
\boldsymbol{\nabla}
of
\omega\nabla\omega
whose eigenvectors are the

\check{\mathsf{H}}_{\eta|\lambda}({\mathbf{x}};q,t)
. Specifically,
\boldsymbol{\nabla}\colon\mathcal{P}_{\rm as}\to\mathcal{P}_{\rm as}
is the
{\mathbb{Q}}(q,t)
-linear map determined by

(3)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\nabla}\check{\mathsf{H}}_{\eta|\lambda}({\mathbf{x}};q,t)=q^{\mathsf{n}(\mu^{*})}t^{\mathsf{n}(\mu)}\check{\mathsf{H}}_{\eta|\lambda}({\mathbf{x}};q,t),\quad\text{for $\mu=(\eta;\lambda)_{+}$}.

In our nonsymmetric compositional shuffle theorem, the
C_{\alpha}
are replaced by
nonsymmetric compositional Hall-Littlewood polynomials

\mathsf{C}_{\alpha}
, which Weyl symmetrize to
C_{\alpha}({\mathbf{x}};t^{-1})
. They are given by

(4)

\displaystyle\mathsf{C}_{\alpha}=(-t)^{|\alpha|-r}\pol\Big(\frac{x_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}\cdots x_{r}^{\alpha_{r}}}{\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq r}(1-tx_{i}/x_{j})}\Big),

where
\pol
is the linear
projection from Laurent polynomials onto ordinary polynomials
determined by its action on Demazure characters, as follows:

\pol({\mathcal{D}}_{\alpha})={\mathcal{D}}_{\alpha}
for
\alpha\in{\mathbb{N}}^{r}
and
\pol({\mathcal{D}}_{\alpha})=0
for
\alpha\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{r}\setminus{\mathbb{N}}^{r}
.
On symmetric functions,
\pol
is the polynomial truncation operator which truncates
\GL_{r}
-characters (indexed by
\lambda_{1}\geq\cdots\geq\lambda_{r}
) to polynomial ones (indexed by
\lambda_{1}\geq\cdots\geq\lambda_{r}\geq 0
).

Theorem 1.1.1
.

For every strict composition
\alpha=(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{r})
of size
k
,

(5)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\nabla}^{-1}\mathsf{C}_{\alpha}=\sum_{\pi\in{\mathbb{D}}^{\alpha}_{k,k}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{flagged word parking}\\
\text{functions $w$ on $\pi$ }\end{subarray}}q^{-\area(\pi)}t^{-\dinv(\pi,w)}{\mathbf{x}}^{\content(w)},

where the sum is now over word parking functions
w
satisfying the following flagging condition:
for each
j=1,\dots,r
, the entry of
w
on the north step from the
touchpoint
(k_{j},k_{j})
is at most
j
, where
k_{j}=\sum_{i<j}\alpha_{i}
.

See §
8.6
for examples illustrating this theorem.
We prove that (
1
) can be recovered by Weyl symmetrizing (
5
)
(see §
8.4
for why
\boldsymbol{\nabla},q,t
are inverted in (
5
) but not (
1
)).

The right hand side of (
5
) can be packaged as a sum of the flagged LLT polynomials introduced in
[
7
]
.
We showed there that these Weyl symmetrize to ordinary LLT polynomials, and conjectured that they are atom positive.
This would imply that
\boldsymbol{\nabla}^{-1}\mathsf{C}_{\alpha}
is atom positive, thereby giving a nonsymmetric strengthening of (
2
).
This also raises the question of whether
\boldsymbol{\nabla}^{-1}\mathsf{C}_{\alpha}

has a natural representation-theoretic interpretation,
which we discuss further in §
1.2
.

In
[
4
]
, Bergeron et al. proposed the
(km,kn)
-compositional shuffle conjecture,
where the combinatorial side is now replaced with a sum over Dyck paths
{\mathbb{D}}^{\alpha}_{km,kn}
from
(0,0)
to
(km,kn)
with touchpoints given by
\alpha
.
This was proved by Mellit
[
29
]
, building on the methods in
[
10
]
. Our methods
yield a nonsymmetric counterpart for this version as well. We now describe this result and
along the way outline its proof and that of Theorem
1.1.1
.

Our approach builds on a framework around signed and unsigned flagged variants of LLT polynomials introduced in

[
7
]
. A key discovery there was the
r
-nonsymmetric plethysm map

{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{r}
, a nonsymmetric variant of the plethystic transformation
g[{\mathbf{x}}]\mapsto g[{\mathbf{x}}/(1-t)]
.
It is the
{\mathbb{Q}}(q,t)
-linear map
{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{r}\colon\mathcal{P}(r)\to\mathcal{P}(r)

determined by its action on elements

f(x_{1},\dots,x_{r})g({\mathbf{x}})
by

(6)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{r}(f(x_{1},\dots,x_{r})g({\mathbf{x}}))=g\Big[\frac{{\mathbf{x}}}{(1-t)}\Big]\pol\Big(\frac{f(x_{1},\dots,x_{r})}{\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq r}(1-t\hskip 0.85358ptx_{i}/x_{j})}\Big),

where
g
is symmetric in
{\mathbf{x}}=x_{1},x_{2},\dots
.
Remarkably,
{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{r}
maps signed flagged LLT polynomials to unsigned ones (see Theorem
5.4.1
).

Here we capitalize on this
result by showing that it meshes beautifully with objects
introduced in Carlsson and Mellit’s proof of the shuffle theorem
[
10
]
. More specifically,
we show that certain nonsymmetric polynomials used in their proof to
construct symmetric LLT polynomials inductively are,
after an (ordinary) plethystic transformation
{\mathsf{P}}_{r}^{-1}
, equal to our signed flagged LLT polynomials
for a sequence of
single-row skew shapes (see Theorem
5.5.4
).
This enables us to interpret an intermediate step in Mellit’s proof of the
(km,kn)
-compositional shuffle conjecture as
saying that, for a certain
operator
\boldsymbol{\rho}_{m,n}^{\alpha}
in the double Dyck path algebra

{\mathbb{A}}_{t,q}
, the expression
{\mathsf{P}}_{r}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\rho}_{m,n}^{\alpha}\cdot 1
is equal to a sum of signed flagged LLT
polynomials.
Applying
{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{r}
then yields our nonsymmetric
(km,kn)
-shuffle theorem, which expresses
{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{r}\,{\mathsf{P}}_{r}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\rho}_{m,n}^{\alpha}\cdot 1

as a sum of (unsigned) flagged LLT polynomials
\mathsf{G}_{r}(\pi^{\prime},\Sigma_{\pi})
:

(7)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{r}\,{\mathsf{P}}_{r}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\rho}_{m,n}^{\alpha}\cdot 1=\sum_{\pi\in{\mathbb{D}}^{\alpha}_{km,kn}}q^{\area(\pi)}t^{\dinv(\pi)-\maxtdinv(\pi)}\,\mathsf{G}_{r}(\pi^{\prime},\Sigma_{\pi})\,,

with the complete statement and notation explained in Theorem
6.3.1
.

The advantage of this framing is that we now obtain a statement that Weyl symmetrizes to the original symmetric
(km,kn)
-shuffle theorem, and
where the flagged LLT polynomials
\mathsf{G}_{r}(\pi^{\prime},\Sigma_{\pi})
are conjecturally atom positive.
Hence, this gives a conjectural strengthening of
the Schur positivity of the symmetric
(km,kn)
-shuffle theorem.

Theorem
1.1.1
can then be deduced from (
7
)
by relating
\boldsymbol{\nabla}
to the endomorphism
N
of
{\mathbb{A}}_{t,q}
introduced in
[
29
]
;
this, in turn, is proved using results of Bechtloff Weising
[
2
]
and Ion-Wu
[
21
]

as well as
a Monk’s rule for nonsymmetric Macdonald polynomials due to
Halverson-Ram
[
20
]
.

1.2.
Atom positivity and representation theory

A guiding principle in our work is that atom positivity in a polynomial signals the presence of underlying representation-theoretic structure. Here, we sketch ideas for a representation-theoretic interpretation of
\boldsymbol{\nabla}^{-1}\mathsf{C}_{\alpha}
, following some preliminaries on Demazure characters and atoms.

The
Demazure operator
or
isobaric divided difference operator
\pi_{i}
is given by

(8)

\displaystyle\pi_{i}(f)

\displaystyle=(x_{i}f-s_{i}(x_{i}f))/(x_{i}-x_{i+1}).

For
w\in S_{N}
, define the operator
\pi_{w}:=\pi_{s_{j_{1}}}\cdots\pi_{s_{j_{d}}}
, where

w=s_{j_{1}}s_{j_{2}}\cdots s_{j_{d}}
is any reduced expression. This does not depend on the choice of reduced expression since the
\pi_{i}
satisfy the braid relations.
For
\alpha\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{N}
, the
Demazure character

{\mathcal{D}}_{\alpha}
is

(9)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{D}}_{\alpha}=\pi_{w}(\mathbf{x}^{\alpha_{+}}),

where
w
is the shortest permutation such that
w(\alpha_{+})=\alpha
.
The
Demazure atom

{\mathcal{A}}_{\alpha}
is defined similarly but with
\hat{\pi}_{w}
in
place of
\pi_{w}
, defined from
\hat{\pi}_{i}=\pi_{i}-1
.

Demazure characters
\{{\mathcal{D}}_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{N}}
and atoms
\{{\mathcal{A}}_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{N}}

are each a basis of
{\mathbb{Q}}(q,t)[x_{1}^{\pm 1},\dots,x_{N}^{\pm 1}]
.
Demazure characters are positive sums of Demazure atoms:

(10)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{D}}_{\beta}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\,\in\,S_{N}\beta,\\[2.27621pt]
w_{\alpha}\leq w_{\beta}\end{subarray}}{\mathcal{A}}_{\alpha},

where
S_{N}\beta
denotes the
S_{N}
orbit of
\beta
,
w_{\alpha}
is the shortest permutation such that
w_{\alpha}(\alpha_{+})=\alpha
,
and
\leq
is Bruhat order on
S_{N}
.
For the longest permutation
w_{0}
of
S_{N}
,
\pi_{w_{0}}
is the
Weyl symmetrization operator
.
It takes the monomial
{\mathbf{x}}^{\lambda}
indexed by a weakly decreasing weight
\lambda\in{\mathbb{N}}^{N}
to the
Schur function
s_{\lambda}(x_{1},\dots,x_{N})
.

Consider the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{gl}_{N}
of
N\times N
matrices and its Lie subalgebra
\mathfrak{b}\subset\mathfrak{gl}_{N}
of upper triangular matrices.
For
\nu=(\nu_{1}\geq\cdots\geq\nu_{N})\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{N}
,
let
V(\nu)
be the irreducible
\mathfrak{gl}_{N}
-module of highest weight
\nu
.
The
Demazure module

D(\alpha)\subset V(\alpha_{+})

is the
\mathfrak{b}
-module
U(\mathfrak{b})\hskip 0.85358ptu_{\alpha}
, where
u_{\alpha}
is an element of the (one-dimensional)
\alpha
-weight space of
V(\alpha_{+})
.
The
Demazure atom module

A(\alpha)

is the quotient of
D(\alpha)
by the sum of all Demazure modules properly contained in
D(\alpha)
.
The characters
\chr(D(\alpha))
and
\chr(A(\alpha))
are
{\mathcal{D}}_{\alpha}
and
{\mathcal{A}}_{\alpha}
, respectively.

Just as a symmetric polynomial being Schur positive suggests it is the character of a natural
\mathfrak{gl}_{N}
-module,
a nonsymmetric polynomial being Demazure character (resp. Demazure atom) positive suggests it is the character of a
\mathfrak{b}
-module with a filtration
whose subquotients are isomorphic to
Demazure modules (resp. Demazure atom modules).
Such filtrations have been
extensively
studied from the geometric perspective—see, e.g.,

[
23
,
28
,
31
,
34
]
.

For any polynomial
f
, we have

(11)

\displaystyle\text{$f$ is Demazure character positive}\implies\text{$f$ is atom positive}\implies\text{$\pi_{w_{0}}(f)$ is Schur positive}.

Thus, Demazure character and atom positivity are natural strengthenings of Schur positivity to the nonsymmetric setting.

For
f\in\mathcal{P}(r)
, write
f[{\mathbf{x}}_{N}]
as shorthand for
f(x_{1},\dots,x_{N},0,\dots)
.
Any
f\in\mathcal{P}(r)
of degree
d
is determined by

f[{\mathbf{x}}_{r+d}]
, so it is mostly harmless to work with
f({\mathbf{x}})
and
f[{\mathbf{x}}_{r+d}]
interchangeably.
See also Remark
8.5.2
.

The conjectured atom positivity of
(\boldsymbol{\nabla}^{-1}\mathsf{C}_{\alpha})[{\mathbf{x}}_{N}]
then suggests the following problem:

Problem 1.2.1
.

Find a natural representation-theoretic interpretation of

\boldsymbol{\nabla}^{-1}\mathsf{C}_{\alpha}
. More precisely, find a (natural)
\mathfrak{b}
-module whose character is
(\boldsymbol{\nabla}^{-1}\mathsf{C}_{\alpha})[{\mathbf{x}}_{N}]
, with a
filtration whose subquotients are Demazure atom modules.

A possible starting point for this problem is a construction of Feigin-Loktev
[
12
]

of a
\mathfrak{gl}_{N}
-module
{\rm W}_{n}^{N}
which is Schur-Weyl dual to the diagonal harmonics module
{\rm DH}_{n}
. Using the
\mathcal{F}({\rm DH}_{n})=\nabla e_{n}
theorem, they showed that
{\rm W}_{n}^{N}
has character
(\nabla e_{n})[{\mathbf{x}}_{N}]
.
One might then expect to find the
\mathfrak{b}
-module in Problem
1.2.1
as a subquotient of
{\rm Res}_{\hskip 0.85358pt\mathfrak{b}}{\rm W}_{n}^{N}
.
Also note that the symmetric version of this problem—find a representation-theoretic interpretation for
\nabla C_{\alpha}
—is still open;
some partial progress has been made by Bergeron-Descouens-Zabrocki
[
5
]
.

1.3.
Organization

To prove Theorem
1.1.1
and its

(km,kn)
-generalization, Theorem
6.3.1
, we need to
provide the language necessary to state both the algebraic and
combinatorial sides, along with their connections to previously known
objects.
To establish this groundwork,
we summarize definitions from
[
10
,
21
,
29
]
in §
3

and establish properties of the
r
-nonsymmetric plethysm map (§
4
), flagged LLT
polynomials (§
5
), and modified
r
-nonsymmetric Macdonald polynomials (§
7
).
In Section
6
, we prove
Theorem
6.3.1
using the
machinery from
[
29
]
and results from §
5
.
In Section
8
, we establish results about
\boldsymbol{\nabla}
that we use
to deduce Theorem
1.1.1
from the
m=n=1
case of Theorem
6.3.1
.
Additionally, we provide examples of Theorem
1.1.1
in §
8.6
and a
summary of notation in
Figure
2
.

2.
Preliminaries

We fix some notation used throughout the paper.

We use the shorthand
[n]=\{1,2,\dots,n\}
.

Integer partitions are written
\lambda=(\lambda_{1}\geq\cdots\geq\lambda_{l})
, possibly with trailing zeroes.
We set
|\lambda|=\lambda_{1}+\cdots+\lambda_{l}
, let
\ell(\lambda)
be the
number of nonzero parts, and define the integer statistic
\mathsf{n}(\lambda)=\sum_{i}(i-1)\lambda_{i}
.
The conjugate partition of
\lambda
is denoted
\lambda^{*}
.
Let
\Par
denote the set of partitions.

We use the notation
{\mathbf{x}}_{N}=x_{1},\dots,x_{N}
,
{\mathbf{x}}=x_{1},x_{2},\dots
,
and for
f({\mathbf{x}})\in\mathcal{P}(r)
, we write

f[{\mathbf{x}}_{N}]
for
f({\mathbf{x}})|_{x_{N+1}=x_{N+2}=\cdots=0}
.
For
\alpha\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{N}
, set
{\mathbf{x}}^{\alpha}=x_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}\cdots x_{N}^{\alpha_{N}}
.

2.1.
Symmetric functions

Let
\Lambda({\mathbf{x}})=\Lambda_{{\mathbb{Q}}(q,t)}({\mathbf{x}})
be the algebra of symmetric
functions in the infinite alphabet
{\mathbf{x}}
, with coefficients in the field
{\mathbb{Q}}(q,t)
.
We follow the notation of Macdonald
[
26
]
for the graded
bases of
\Lambda({\mathbf{x}})
. We write
\omega\colon\Lambda({\mathbf{x}})\rightarrow\Lambda({\mathbf{x}})
for the involutory
{\mathbb{Q}}(q,t)
-algebra automorphism determined by

\omega s_{\lambda}=s_{\lambda^{*}}
for Schur functions
s_{\lambda}
.

Given
f\in\Lambda({\mathbf{x}})

and any expression
A

involving indeterminates, the plethystic evaluation
f[A]
is defined
by writing
f
as a polynomial
in the power-sums
p_{k}
and evaluating with
p_{k}\mapsto p_{k}[A]
,
where
p_{k}[A]
is the result of substituting
a^{k}
for every
indeterminate
a
occurring in
A
. The variables
q,t
from our
ground field count as indeterminates.
When
{\mathbf{x}}_{r}
and
{\mathbf{x}}
are used in a plethystic formula, we interpret these as
{\mathbf{x}}_{r}=x_{1}+\cdots+x_{r}
and
{\mathbf{x}}=x_{1}+x_{2}+\cdots
.
For
example, the evaluation
f[{\mathbf{x}}/(1-t)]
is the image of
f({\mathbf{x}})
under
the
{\mathbb{Q}}(q,t)
-algebra automorphism of
\Lambda({\mathbf{x}})
that sends
p_{k}
to

p_{k}/(1-t^{k})
.

We fix notation for the series

(12)

\Omega=1+\sum_{k>0}h_{k}=\exp\sum_{k>0}\frac{p_{k}}{k},\quad\text{or}\quad\Omega[a_{1}+a_{2}+\cdots-b_{1}-b_{2}-\cdots]=\frac{\prod_{i}(1-b_{i})}{\prod_{i}(1-a_{i})}.

2.2.
Strong and
t
-adic convergence

For
a\in{\mathbb{Q}}(q,t)
, write
a=b/c
with
b,c\in{\mathbb{Q}}[q,t]
. The order of vanishing at
t=0

of
a
, denoted
\ord(a)
, is the order of vanishing of
b
minus the order of vanishing of
c
.
For
e\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, let
({\mathbb{Q}}(q,t)[{\mathbf{x}}_{N}])_{e}
denote the subset of
{\mathbb{Q}}(q,t)[{\mathbf{x}}_{N}]
consisting of linear combinations
of monomials
{\mathbf{x}}^{\lambda}
with coefficients
a_{\lambda}
satisfying
\ord(a_{\lambda})\geq e
.
By convention,
\ord(0)=\infty
and
0\in({\mathbb{Q}}(q,t)[{\mathbf{x}}_{N}])_{e}
for all
e
.

We say that a sequence of polynomials
g_{N_{0}},g_{N_{0}+1},\dots
, with

g_{N}({\mathbf{x}}_{N})\in{\mathbb{Q}}(q,t)[{\mathbf{x}}_{N}]
,
converges strongly to
f({\mathbf{x}})\in\mathcal{P}(r)
if

(13)

g_{N}({\mathbf{x}}_{N})=f[{\mathbf{x}}_{N}]

for all
N\geq N_{0}
.

We say that a sequence of polynomials

g_{N}({\mathbf{x}}_{N})\in{\mathbb{Q}}(q,t)[{\mathbf{x}}_{N}]

converges
t
-adically to
f({\mathbf{x}})\in\mathcal{P}(r)
if for all
e>0
, we have

(14)

\big(g_{N}({\mathbf{x}}_{N})-f[{\mathbf{x}}_{N}]\big)\in({\mathbb{Q}}(q,t)[{\mathbf{x}}_{N}])_{e}

for sufficiently large
N
.

Remark 2.2.1
.

These are the same notions of convergence as in
[
7
, §2.6]
. The definition of
t
-adic convergence here is closely related to
that in
[
21
,
2
]
.
In particular, if
g_{N}
converges
t
-adically to
f
in the sense of

[
2
,
21
]
, then it also does in the sense above.

3.
The double Dyck path algebra

Much of this paper involves stable versions of the double affine Hecke algebra and nonsymmetric Macdonald polynomials.

The double affine Hecke algebra (DAHA) or Cherednik algebra
\mathcal{H}_{N}
is a
{\mathbb{Q}}(q,t)
-algebra
generated by
X_{1}^{\pm 1},\dots,X_{N}^{\pm 1}
,
Y_{1}^{\pm 1},\dots,Y_{N}^{\pm 1}
, and
T_{1},\dots,T_{N-1}
.
It contains two copies of the type
\tilde{A}_{N-1}
extended affine Hecke algebra, and
reduces to
the group algebra
{\mathbb{Q}}[({\mathbb{Z}}^{N}\times{\mathbb{Z}}^{N})\rtimes S_{N}]

when the parameters
q
and
t
are set to 1.
There is an action of
\mathcal{H}_{N}
on
{\mathbb{Q}}(q,t)[x_{1}^{\pm 1},\dots,x_{N}^{\pm 1}]
in which
X_{i}
acts by multiplication by
x_{i}
, the
T_{i}
act by Demazure-Lusztig operators,
and the
Y_{i}
act by Cherednik operators (further discussed in §
3.3
).
The nonsymmetric Macdonald polynomials
E_{\alpha}
can then be constructed as the joint eigenfunctions of
Y_{1},\dots,Y_{N}\in\mathcal{H}_{N}
.

The double Dyck path algebra
{\mathbb{A}}_{t,q}
of Carlsson-Mellit
[
10
]
and the
+
stable limit DAHA
\mathcal{H}^{+}
of Ion-Wu
[
21
]
are
two algebras which both aim to capture the idea of a limiting object for the double affine Hecke algebras
\mathcal{H}_{N}
as
N\to\infty
. The latter accomplishes this through a certain
t
-adic limit, whereas the former avoids directly defining any kind of limit and instead contains a copy of something that is close to
\mathcal{H}_{N}
for each
N
together with extra elements to go between these copies. Because of these extra elements,
{\mathbb{A}}_{t,q}
should be thought of as being bigger than
\mathcal{H}^{+}
(see Theorem
3.3.2
for the precise relationship).

In this section we describe the algebras
{\mathbb{A}}_{t,q}
and
\mathcal{H}^{+}
and some of their natural representations. In §
7
we will introduce stable versions of the
E_{\alpha}
’s and their relation to
\mathcal{H}^{+}
.

3.1.
The Dyck path algebra
{\mathbb{A}}_{t}
and double Dyck path algebra
{\mathbb{A}}_{t,q}

In this paper, we use the same notation for the (double) Dyck path algebra and its natural representation as Mellit
[
29
]
except that we swap
the role of
q
and
t
to match the traditional DAHA notation, and we use
W
in place of
X
to avoid confusion with our notation
{\mathbf{x}}=x_{1},x_{2},\dots
.

Definition 3.1.1
.

Let
\dot{\mathbf{Q}}
be the quiver with vertex set
{\mathbb{N}}
, with arrows
labeled
d_{+}
from
r\to r+1
, arrows labeled
d_{-}
from

r+1\to r
, and with
r-1
loops from
r
to
r
, labeled

T_{1},\ldots,T_{r-1}
.
The
Dyck path algebra

{\mathbb{A}}_{t}
is the path algebra of the quiver
\dot{\mathbf{Q}}
,
over the field
{\mathbb{Q}}(q,t)
, with the following relations:

\displaystyle\begin{gathered}(T_{i}-1)(T_{i}+t)=0\,,\ 1\leq i\leq r-1\,,\\
T_{i}T_{i+1}T_{i}=T_{i+1}T_{i}T_{i+1}\,,\ 1\leq i\leq r-2\,,\\
T_{i}T_{j}=T_{j}T_{i}\,,\ |i-j|>1\,,\end{gathered}

(18)

\displaystyle d_{-}^{2}T_{r-1}=d_{-}^{2}\,,\ T_{i}d_{-}=d_{-}T_{i}\,,\ 1\leq i\leq r-2\,,

(19)

\displaystyle T_{1}d_{+}^{2}=d_{+}^{2}\,,\ d_{+}T_{i}=T_{i+1}d_{+}\,,\ 1\leq i\leq r-1\,,

(20)

\displaystyle d_{-}[d_{+},d_{-}]T_{r-1}=t\,[d_{+},d_{-}]d_{-}\,,\ r\geq 2\,,

(21)

\displaystyle T_{1}[d_{+},d_{-}]d_{+}=t\hskip 0.85358ptd_{+}[d_{+},d_{-}]\,,\ r\geq 1\,,

where each line is an identity of paths starting at vertex
r
and we impose these relations for all
r\in{\mathbb{N}}
. We write
e_{r}
for the idempotent at vertex
r
.

Note that to simplify notation, many arrows have the same name, so to resolve potential ambiguity,
we adopt the convention that whenever a product of elements of
{\mathbb{A}}_{t}
is specified to start at vertex
r
, we interpret this as the unique sequence of arrows which concatenate head to tail.
For example, in
(
19
),
T_{1}d_{+}^{2}
is the directed path which begins with the arrow from
r
to
r+1
labeled
d_{+}
, followed by the arrow from

r+1
to
r+2
labeled
d_{+}
, and then the loop
T_{1}
from
r+2
to
r+2
.

Definition 3.1.2
.

Let
\ddot{\mathbf{Q}}
be the quiver formed from
\dot{\mathbf{Q}}
by adding additional arrows labeled

d_{+}^{*}
from
r\to r+1
.
The
double Dyck path algebra

{\mathbb{A}}_{t,q}
is the path
algebra of the quiver
\ddot{\mathbf{Q}}
,
over the field
{\mathbb{Q}}(q,t)
, with relations (
3.1.1
)–(
21
) and the following additional relations, each regarded as a path starting at
vertex
r
:

(22)

\displaystyle T_{1}(d_{+}^{*})^{2}=(d_{+}^{*})^{2}\,,\ d_{+}^{*}T_{i}=T_{i+1}d_{+}^{*}\,,1\leq i\leq r-1\,,

(23)

\displaystyle td_{-}[d_{+}^{*},d_{-}]=[d_{+}^{*},d_{-}]d_{-}T_{r-1}\,,\ r\geq 2\,,

(24)

\displaystyle t[d_{+}^{*},d_{-}]d_{+}^{*}=T_{1}d_{+}^{*}[d_{+}^{*},d_{-}]\,,r\geq 1\,,

(25)

\displaystyle d_{+}z_{i}=z_{i+1}d_{+}\,,\ d_{+}^{*}y_{i}=y_{i+1}d_{+}^{*}\,,\ 1\leq i\leq r\,,

(26)

\displaystyle z_{1}d_{+}=-qt^{r+1}y_{1}d_{+}^{*}\,,

where the elements
y_{i},z_{i}
, for
1\leq i\leq r
, are the following loops at vertex
r
:

(27)

\displaystyle y_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{t^{r-1}(t-1)}[d_{+},d_{-}]T_{r-1}\cdots T_{1}\,,

(28)

\displaystyle y_{i+1}

\displaystyle=t\,T_{i}^{-1}y_{i}T_{i}^{-1}\,,\ 1\leq i\leq r-1\,,

(29)

\displaystyle z_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{t^{r}}{1-t}[d_{+}^{*},d_{-}]T_{r-1}^{-1}\cdots T_{1}^{-1}\,,

(30)

\displaystyle z_{i+1}

\displaystyle=t^{-1}T_{i}z_{i}T_{i}\,,\ 1\leq i\leq r-1\,.

We write
e_{r}
for the idempotent at vertex
r
.

Remark 3.1.3
.

There is a
{\mathbb{Q}}(q,t)
-algebra homomorphism
\rho\colon{\mathbb{A}}_{t}\to{\mathbb{A}}_{t,q}
determined uniquely by

(31)

\displaystyle e_{r}\mapsto e_{r},\ \ T_{i}\mapsto T_{i},\ \ d_{-}\mapsto d_{-},\ \ d_{+}\mapsto d_{+},

and a
{\mathbb{Q}}
-algebra homomorphism
\rho^{*}\colon{\mathbb{A}}_{t}\to{\mathbb{A}}_{t,q}
determined uniquely by

(32)

\displaystyle q\mapsto q^{-1},\ \ t\mapsto t^{-1},\ \ e_{r}\mapsto e_{r},\ \ T_{i}\mapsto T_{i}^{-1},\ \ d_{-}\mapsto d_{-},\ \ d_{+}\mapsto d_{+}^{*}.

Thus,
{\mathbb{A}}_{t,q}
contains the copy
\rho({\mathbb{A}}_{t})
of
{\mathbb{A}}_{t}
and the twisted copy
\rho^{*}({\mathbb{A}}_{t})
of
{\mathbb{A}}_{t}
.
In
[
10
]
, the authors introduce
{\mathbb{A}}_{t,q}
as the free
product of these two copies with
additional relations between them.

3.2.
Three actions of
{\mathbb{A}}_{t,q}

Let

V_{r}={\mathbb{Q}}(q,t)[y_{1},\ldots,y_{r}]\otimes\Lambda_{{\mathbb{Q}}(q,t)}(W)

and
V_{*}=(V_{r})_{r\geq 0}
.
Mellit
[
29
]
and Carlsson-Mellit
[
10
]

each define an action of
{\mathbb{A}}_{t,q}
on
V_{*}
; we write
d_{+},d^{*}_{+},d_{-},T_{i}
for the generators of
{\mathbb{A}}_{t,q}
with Mellit’s action and
d^{CM}_{+},d^{*CM}_{+},d^{CM}_{-},T^{CM}_{i}
for the action defined by Carlsson-Mellit
(they both use the same conventions for the generators and relations of the algebra
{\mathbb{A}}_{t,q}
).
We will also work with
an action obtained from Mellit’s version by twisting by a plethystic transformation.
We first describe Mellit’s version.

Proposition 3.2.1
.

The following formulas define a quiver representation of

{\mathbb{A}}_{t,q}
on
V_{*}
.
The action of the arrows originating at quiver vertex
r
are given by

(33)

\displaystyle T_{i}F

\displaystyle=s_{i}F+(1-t)y_{i}\frac{F-s_{i}F}{y_{i}-y_{i+1}},

(34)

\displaystyle d_{-}F

\displaystyle=\langle y_{r}^{0}\rangle F[y_{1},\ldots,y_{r};W-(t-1)y_{r}]\Omega[-y_{r}^{-1}W],

(35)

\displaystyle d_{+}F

\displaystyle=-T_{1}\cdots T_{r}(y_{r+1}F[y_{1},\ldots,y_{r};W+(t-1)y_{r+1}]),

(36)

\displaystyle d_{+}^{*}F

\displaystyle=\gamma\hskip 0.85358pt(F[y_{1},\ldots,y_{r};W+(t-1)y_{r+1}]).

where
F[y_{1},\ldots,y_{r};W]\in V_{r}
,
i\in[r-1]
, and

\gamma\colon V_{r+1}\to V_{r+1}
is the operator given by
\gamma\hskip 0.85358pt(G[y_{1},\ldots,y_{r+1};W])=G[y_{2},\ldots,y_{r+1},qy_{1};W]
.

In addition, (
33
)–(
35
) imply that the loop
y_{i}\in{\mathbb{A}}_{t,q}
at vertex
r
acts on
V_{r}
as multiplication by
y_{i}
, for any
i\leq r
.

For the action defined by Carlsson-Mellit, we only need the following result relating the two actions.
Let
{\mathsf{Q}}_{r}
denote the
{\mathbb{Q}}(q,t)
-linear isomorphism between the subspace
y_{1}\cdots y_{r}V_{r}\subseteq V_{r}
and
V_{r}
given by

(37)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{Q}}_{r}\colon y_{1}\cdots y_{r}V_{r}\to V_{r},\quad\quad y_{1}\cdots y_{r}v\mapsto v.

Proposition 3.2.2
.

The spaces
(y_{1}\cdots y_{r}V_{r})_{r\geq 0}
form a submodule for Mellit’s action of
{\mathbb{A}}_{t,q}
on
V_{*}
, and we have the following identities of operators on
V_{*}
:

(38)

\displaystyle T_{i}^{CM}

\displaystyle={\mathsf{Q}}_{r}\hskip 0.85358ptT_{i}\hskip 0.85358pt{\mathsf{Q}}_{r}^{-1},

(39)

\displaystyle d_{-}^{CM}

\displaystyle={\mathsf{Q}}_{r-1}\hskip 0.85358pt(-d_{-})\hskip 0.85358pt{\mathsf{Q}}_{r}^{-1},

(40)

\displaystyle d_{+}^{CM}

\displaystyle={\mathsf{Q}}_{r+1}\hskip 0.85358pt(-d_{+})\hskip 0.85358pt{\mathsf{Q}}_{r}^{-1},

(41)

\displaystyle\hskip 0.85358ptd_{+}^{*CM}

\displaystyle={\mathsf{Q}}_{r+1}\hskip 0.85358pty_{1}d_{+}^{*}\hskip 0.85358pt{\mathsf{Q}}_{r}^{-1},

where
i<r
, and
T_{i}^{CM}
and
T_{i}
are loops at quiver vertex
r
.

Proof.

Identities (
38
)–(
40
) are immediate
from
[
29
, Remark 3.11]
.
The action of
d_{+}^{*CM}
, as defined in
[
10
, Theorem 6.1]
, is the same as
d_{+}^{*}
given in (
36
).
Then (
41
) holds by the following direct computation. For any

F[y_{1},\ldots,y_{r};W]\in V_{r}
,

y_{1}d_{+}^{*}{\mathsf{Q}}_{r}^{-1}F=y_{1}d_{+}^{*}(y_{1}\cdots y_{r}F)=y_{1}\hskip 0.85358pt\gamma\hskip 0.85358pt(y_{1}\cdots y_{r}F[y_{1},\ldots,y_{r};W+(t-1)y_{r+1}])\\
=y_{1}y_{2}\cdots y_{r+1}\hskip 0.85358pt\gamma\hskip 0.85358pt(F[y_{1},\ldots,y_{r};W+(t-1)y_{r+1}])={\mathsf{Q}}_{r+1}^{-1}\hskip 0.85358ptd_{+}^{*CM}F.\qed

The third action is the main one we will use.
This version was studied by Ion-Wu
[
21
]
who connected it to a representation
of the
+
stable limit DAHA
\mathcal{H}^{+}
(see §
3.3
),
and it is the action we will use to connect to signed flagged LLT polynomials.

As in §
1
, let
\mathcal{P}(r)={\mathbb{Q}}(q,t)[x_{1},\dots,x_{r}]\otimes\Lambda_{{\mathbb{Q}}(q,t)}(x_{r+1},\dots)
.
Define the isomorphism
{\mathsf{P}}_{r}
to be the
{\mathbb{Q}}(q,t)
-linear map determined by

(42)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} {\mathsf{P}}_{r}\colon\mathcal{P}(r)\xrightarrow{\ \cong\ }V_{r},\hskip 99.58464pt\\
x_{1}^{\eta_{1}}\cdots x_{r}^{\eta_{r}}g[x_{r+1}+\cdots]\mapsto y_{1}^{\eta_{1}}\cdots y_{r}^{\eta_{r}}g[W/(t-1)],\end{aligned}

where
\eta_{1},\dots,\eta_{r}\in{\mathbb{N}}
, and
g
is an arbitrary symmetric function.
Thus, we have an action of
{\mathbb{A}}_{t,q}
on
(\mathcal{P}(r))_{r\geq 0}

induced by Mellit’s action on
(V_{r})_{r\geq 0}
via the
isomorphisms
{\mathsf{P}}_{r}
.
We write
d_{+}^{\mathsf{P}},d^{*{\mathsf{P}}}_{+},d_{-}^{\mathsf{P}},T_{i}
for the generators of
{\mathbb{A}}_{t,q}
when using this action.
For example,
d_{-}^{\mathsf{P}}\colon\mathcal{P}(r)\to\mathcal{P}(r-1)
and
d_{+}^{\mathsf{P}}\colon\mathcal{P}(r)\to\mathcal{P}(r+1)
are defined by

(43)

\displaystyle d_{-}^{\mathsf{P}}={\mathsf{P}}_{r-1}^{-1}\,d_{-}\,{\mathsf{P}}_{r},\quad\quad d_{+}^{\mathsf{P}}={\mathsf{P}}_{r+1}^{-1}\,d_{+}\,{\mathsf{P}}_{r}.

Explicit formulas for these operators are readily calculated from Proposition
3.2.1
,
and we record them in the proposition below (see
[
21
, §7.5]
).
Let
\iota_{r}\colon\mathcal{P}(r)\to\mathcal{P}(r+1)
denote the inclusion map.
Let
\mathcal{B}_{n}
denote Jing’s Hall-Littlewood vertex operator
[
22
]
,
which acts on a
symmetric function
f(Z)\in\Lambda(Z)
by

(44)

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{n}\hskip 0.85358ptf(Z)=\langle u^{0}\rangle u^{n}f[Z-u]\Omega[(1-t)u^{-1}Z].

Proposition 3.2.3
.

The following formulas define a quiver representation of

{\mathbb{A}}_{t,q}
on
(\mathcal{P}(r))_{r\geq 0}
.
The action of

d_{-}^{\mathsf{P}}
at quiver vertex
r
is the
unique
{\mathbb{Q}}(q,t)
-linear map
\mathcal{P}(r)\to\mathcal{P}(r-1)
determined by

(45)

\displaystyle d_{-}^{\mathsf{P}}(x_{1}^{\eta_{1}}\cdots x_{r}^{\eta_{r}}g(x_{r+1},\dots))=x_{1}^{\eta_{1}}\cdots x_{r-1}^{\eta_{r-1}}\mathcal{B}_{\eta_{r}}(g[x_{r}+x_{r+1}+\cdots]),

where
g
is any symmetric function and
\mathcal{B}_{\eta_{r}}
is as in (
44
) with
Z=x_{r}+x_{r+1}+\cdots
.
The action of the other arrows originating at quiver vertex
r
are given by

(46)

\displaystyle T_{i}\hskip 0.85358ptf=s_{i}f+(1-t)x_{i}\frac{f-s_{i}f}{x_{i}-x_{i+1}},

(47)

\displaystyle d_{+}^{\mathsf{P}}\hskip 0.85358ptf=-T_{1}\cdots T_{r}\,(x_{r+1}\,\iota_{r}(f)),

(48)

\displaystyle d_{+}^{*{\mathsf{P}}}f=\tilde{\gamma}\,\iota_{r}(f),

where
f=f({\mathbf{x}})=f(x_{1},x_{2},\dots)\in\mathcal{P}(r)
,
i\in[r-1]
, and

\tilde{\gamma}\colon\mathcal{P}(r+1)\to\mathcal{P}(r+1)
is the operator given by
\tilde{\gamma}(g({\mathbf{x}}))=g(x_{2},\ldots,x_{r+1},qx_{1},x_{r+2},x_{r+3},\dots)
.

3.3.
Ion-Wu’s
+
stable limit DAHA

In
[
21
]
, Ion and Wu propose two ways to make precise the idea of a limiting object of the
\mathcal{H}_{n}
as
n\to\infty
.
The version needed for this paper is a kind of
t
-adic limit of the positive subalgebras
\mathcal{H}_{n}^{+}\overset{\text{{def}}}{=}\langle X_{1},\dots,X_{n},Y_{1},\dots,Y_{n},T_{1},\dots,T_{n-1}\rangle\subseteq\mathcal{H}_{n}
.
Its presentation is given as follows.

Definition 3.3.1

(
[
21
, Definition 4.1]
)
.

The
+
stable limit DAHA

\mathcal{H}^{+}
is the
{\mathbb{Q}}(q,t)
-algebra with generators
T_{i},X_{i}
,
and
Y_{i}
for all
i\geq 1
, satisfying the following relations:

\displaystyle T_{i}T_{j}=T_{j}T_{i}\,,\ |i-j|>1\,,

\displaystyle T_{i}T_{i+1}T_{i}=T_{i+1}T_{i}T_{i+1}\,,\ i\geq 1\,,

\displaystyle(T_{i}-1)(T_{i}+t)=0\,,\ i\geq 1\,,

\displaystyle t\,T_{i}^{-1}X_{i}T_{i}^{-1}=X_{i+1}\,,\ i\geq 1\,,

\displaystyle T_{i}X_{j}=X_{j}T_{i}\,,\ j\neq i,i+1\,,

\displaystyle X_{i}X_{j}=X_{j}X_{i}\,,\ i,j\geq 1\,,

\displaystyle t^{-1}T_{i}Y_{i}T_{i}=Y_{i+1}\,,\ i\geq 1\,,

\displaystyle T_{i}Y_{j}=Y_{j}T_{i}\,,\ j\neq i,i+1\,,

\displaystyle Y_{i}Y_{j}=Y_{j}Y_{i}\,,\ i,j\geq 1\,,

\displaystyle Y_{1}T_{1}X_{1}=X_{2}Y_{1}T_{1}\,.

As in §
1
, the space of
almost symmetric functions

\mathcal{P}_{\rm as}
is

(49)

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{\rm as}=\bigcup_{r\geq 0}\mathcal{P}(r)\ \subseteq\,{\mathbb{Q}}(q,t)[[x_{1},\dots]].

It is shown in
[
21
]
that
there is an action of
\mathcal{H}^{+}
on
\mathcal{P}_{\rm as}

in which

X_{i}
acts by multiplication by
x_{i}
,

T_{i}
acts on
f({\mathbf{x}})\in\mathcal{P}_{\rm as}
by
2
2

2

The same formula as in (
46
), but there the meaning is slightly different since
T_{i}\in{\mathbb{A}}_{t,q}
stands for a different loop at each quiver vertex
r>i
.

(50)

\displaystyle T_{i}f

\displaystyle=s_{i}f+(1-t)\hskip 0.85358ptx_{i}\,\frac{f-s_{i}f}{x_{i}-x_{i+1}}\,,

and the action of
Y_{i}
is defined as follows: first define operators on
{\mathbb{Q}}(q,t)[{\mathbf{x}}_{N}]
by

(51)

\displaystyle\Phi\hskip 0.85358ptg

\displaystyle=g(x_{2},\dots,x_{N},q\hskip 0.85358ptx_{1}),

(52)

\displaystyle\widehat{x}_{1}\hskip 0.85358ptg

\displaystyle=g(0,x_{2},\dots,x_{n}).

Then the action of
Y_{i}
on
f({\mathbf{x}})\in\mathcal{P}_{\rm as}
is given by

(53)

\displaystyle Y_{i}\hskip 0.85358ptf({\mathbf{x}})=\lim_{N\to\infty}t^{N-i+1}\hskip 0.85358ptT_{i-1}\cdots T_{1}\hskip 0.85358pt(1-\widehat{x}_{1})\hskip 0.85358pt\Phi\hskip 0.85358ptT_{N-1}^{-1}\cdots T_{i}^{-1}f[{\mathbf{x}}_{N}],

where the sequence converges
t
-adically to the limit.

For context, this action can be viewed as a limit of the action of
\mathcal{H}_{n}^{+}
on
{\mathbb{Q}}(q,t)[{\mathbf{x}}_{N}]
,
which, in the conventions of
[
21
]
, is given as follows:
X_{i}
acts
by multiplication by
x_{i}
,

T_{i}
by (
50
), and
Y_{i}\in\mathcal{H}_{n}^{+}
by
t^{-i+1}T_{i-1}\cdots T_{1}\hskip 0.85358ptX_{1}\hskip 0.85358pt\Phi\hskip 0.85358ptT_{N-1}^{-1}\cdots T_{i}^{-1}
.

Recall from (
42
) the isomorphism

{\mathsf{P}}_{r}\colon\mathcal{P}(r)\xrightarrow{\ \cong\ }V_{r}
given by

x_{1}^{\eta_{1}}\cdots x_{r}^{\eta_{r}}g[x_{r+1}+\cdots]\mapsto y_{1}^{\eta_{1}}\cdots y_{r}^{\eta_{r}}g[W/(t-1)]
.

Theorem 3.3.2

(
[
21
, Theorems 7.13 and 7.14]
)
.

The action of
X_{i},Y_{i},T_{j}\in\mathcal{H}^{+}
on
\mathcal{P}(r)
and
y_{i},z_{i},T_{j}\in{\mathbb{A}}_{t,q}
on
V_{r}
, for
1\leq i\leq r
,
1\leq j<r
,
are related via
{\mathsf{P}}_{r}
as follows:

(54)

\displaystyle X_{i}={\mathsf{P}}^{-1}_{r}y_{i}{\mathsf{P}}_{r},\ \ Y_{i}={\mathsf{P}}^{-1}_{r}z_{i}{\mathsf{P}}_{r},\ \ T_{j}={\mathsf{P}}^{-1}_{r}T_{j}{\mathsf{P}}_{r}.

In other words, the following diagrams commute:

Remark 3.3.3
.

(i)
The chief advantage of the
\mathcal{H}^{+}
action over that of
{\mathbb{A}}_{t,q}
(in Proposition
3.2.3
) is that

z_{i}\in{\mathbb{A}}_{t,q}
stands for many elements, one for each quiver
vertex
r
with
r\geq i
, each of which acts separately on the different spaces
(V_{r})_{r\geq i}
,
whereas their counterpart
Y_{i}
in
\mathcal{H}^{+}
is a single element which acts on all of
\mathcal{P}_{\rm as}
uniformly (and similarly for other generators).
In particular, this means that

Y_{i}\iota_{r}=\iota_{r}Y_{i}
for the inclusion
\iota_{r}\colon\mathcal{P}(r)\to\mathcal{P}(r+1)
, whereas the corresponding property

{\mathsf{P}}_{r+1}^{-1}z_{i}{\mathsf{P}}_{r+1}\iota_{r}=\iota_{r}{\mathsf{P}}_{r}^{-1}z_{i}{\mathsf{P}}_{r}
is not immediate
from the definition (
29
)–(
30
).

(ii) Theorem
3.3.2
can be thought of informally as saying that

\mathcal{H}^{+}
is a subalgebra of
{\mathbb{A}}_{t,q}
, but this is not technically true because of the inherent difference between these actions just discussed in (i).

4.
Nonsymmetric plethysm, Weyl and Hecke symmetrization, and
d_{-}

In this section we define stable versions,
\widehat{{\mathcal{S}}}_{r}
and
{\boldsymbol{\pi}}_{r}
, of Hecke and Weyl symmetrization,
recall a result of Bechtloff Weising
[
2
]
that
\widehat{{\mathcal{S}}}_{r}=d_{-}^{\mathsf{P}}
, and prove that
d_{-}^{\mathsf{P}}
conjugated by nonsymmetric plethysm is equal to
{\boldsymbol{\pi}}_{r}
.
The latter was proved in
[
7
, §7.8]
, but the proof goes through several
results involving flagged LLT polynomials, and
it seems worthwhile to give a short self-contained proof.

4.1.
Weyl and Hecke symmetrization

Following
[
7
, §7.8]
,
define a stable version of Weyl symmetrization
{\boldsymbol{\pi}}_{r}:\mathcal{P}(r)\to\mathcal{P}(r-1)
by

(55)

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\pi}}_{r}f({\mathbf{x}})=\lim_{N\to\infty}\pi_{x_{r},x_{r+1},\dots,x_{N}}f[{\mathbf{x}}_{N}],

where
\pi_{x_{r},\dots,x_{N}}
is the operator
\pi_{w}
(see §
1.2
)
for
w
the longest permutation in
the Young subgroup
S_{(1^{r-1};N-r+1)}
of the symmetric group
S_{N}
which permutes the last
N-r+1
indices.
By
[
7
, §7.7]
, the sequence on the right hand side converges strongly to a
well-defined limit.

Following
[
2
]
, define the partial Hecke symmetrizer
\widehat{{\mathcal{S}}}_{r+1}^{N}\colon{\mathbb{Q}}(q,t)[{\mathbf{x}}_{N}]\to{\mathbb{Q}}(q,t)[{\mathbf{x}}_{N}]
by

(56)

\widehat{{\mathcal{S}}}_{r+1}^{N}=\frac{1}{[N-r]_{t}!}\sum_{w\in S_{(1^{r};N-r)}}t^{{\binom{N-r}{2}}-\ell(w)}T_{w},

where
T_{w}=T_{i_{1}}T_{i_{2}}\cdots T_{i_{k}}

for any reduced expression
w=s_{i_{1}}s_{i_{2}}\cdots s_{i_{k}}
with
T_{i}
acting as in (
50
),
and
[N-r]_{t}!=\prod_{k=1}^{N-r}[k]_{t}
for
[k]_{t}=\frac{1-t^{k}}{1-t}
.
The operator
\widehat{{\mathcal{S}}}_{r+1}^{N}
is normalized so that
\widehat{{\mathcal{S}}}_{r+1}^{N}\,g({\mathbf{x}}_{N})=g({\mathbf{x}}_{N})
for any polynomial
g({\mathbf{x}}_{N})
symmetric in
x_{r+1},\dots,x_{N}
.

Partial Hecke symmetrization gives rise to an operator
\widehat{{\mathcal{S}}}_{r}\colon\mathcal{P}(r)\to\mathcal{P}(r-1)
defined on any
f({\mathbf{x}})\in\mathcal{P}(r)
by

(57)

\displaystyle\widehat{{\mathcal{S}}}_{r}f({\mathbf{x}})=\lim_{N\to\infty}\widehat{{\mathcal{S}}}_{r}^{N}f[{\mathbf{x}}_{N}],

where, as shown in
[
2
, §4.3]
, the right
hand side converges
t
-adically to a well-defined limit.

Proposition 4.1.1

(
[
2
, Proposition 4.25]
)
.

The operator
\widehat{{\mathcal{S}}}_{r}\colon\mathcal{P}(r)\to\mathcal{P}(r-1)
is the same as
d_{-}^{\mathsf{P}}\colon\mathcal{P}(r)\to\mathcal{P}(r-1)
defined in §
3.2
.

Remark 4.1.2
.

We will also make use of Proposition
4.1.1
as a notational tool:
since
d_{-}^{\mathsf{P}}\in{\mathbb{A}}_{t,q}
actually stands for the different operators
d_{-}^{\mathsf{P}}:\mathcal{P}(r)\to\mathcal{P}(r-1)
for each
r
,
it is sometimes useful to specify the
r
in the notation. We adopt the convention of using
\widehat{{\mathcal{S}}}_{r}

when this is the case.

Remark 4.1.3
.

(i) The version of the Demazure-Lusztig operators
T_{i}
used here agrees
with
[
2
,
10
,
21
,
29
]
. In terms of the
notation here, the Demazure-Lusztig operators in
[
7
,
14
]
are
t\,T_{i}^{-1}
.

(ii) The Hecke symmetrizer
\widehat{{\mathcal{S}}}_{r+1}^{N}
and its stable version
\widehat{{\mathcal{S}}}_{r}\colon\mathcal{P}(r)\to\mathcal{P}(r-1)
agree with those in
[
7
, §5.1 and §7.8]
and
[
2
, §4.3]
, except that the latter reference defines a version of
\widehat{{\mathcal{S}}}_{r}
on a larger domain, from

\mathcal{P}_{\rm as}
to
\mathcal{P}(r-1)
.

4.2.
The nonsymmetric plethysm conjugate of
d_{-}^{\mathsf{P}}

In this subsection, we
prove the operator identity
{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{r-1}\,d_{-}^{\mathsf{P}}\,{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{r}^{-1}={\boldsymbol{\pi}}_{r}
.
The proof uses an alternative description—Theorem
4.2.1
, below—of the
r
-nonsymmetric plethysm map
{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{r}
defined
in (
6
), in terms of flagged versions of the complete
homogeneous symmetric functions
h_{\lambda}({\mathbf{x}})
.

We continue to use the notation
{\mathbf{x}}_{r}=x_{1},\dots,x_{r}
and
{\mathbf{x}}=x_{1},x_{2},\cdots
;
recall that we interpret these as
{\mathbf{x}}_{r}=x_{1}+\cdots+x_{r}
and
{\mathbf{x}}=x_{1}+x_{2}+\cdots

when they are used in a plethystic formula.
For
\eta\in{\mathbb{N}}^{r}
, set

(58)

\displaystyle\mathsf{h}_{\eta}({\mathbf{x}}_{r})=h_{\eta_{1}}(x_{1})h_{\eta_{2}}(x_{1},x_{2})\cdots h_{\eta_{r}}(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{r}).

As
(\eta|\lambda)
ranges over
{\mathbb{N}}^{r}\times\Par
, the elements

(59)

\displaystyle\mathsf{h}_{\eta}({\mathbf{x}}_{r})h_{\lambda}({\mathbf{x}})

form a basis for
\mathcal{P}(r)
.
Additionally, using plethystic notation, set

(60)

\mathsf{h}^{\pm}_{\eta}({\mathbf{x}}_{r})=h_{\eta_{1}}(x_{1})h_{\eta_{2}}[x_{2}+(1-t)x_{1}]h_{\eta_{2}}[x_{3}+(1-t)(x_{1}+x_{2})]\cdots h_{\eta_{r}}[x_{r}+(1-t)({\mathbf{x}}_{r-1})]\,.

Then, as
(\eta|\lambda)
ranges over
{\mathbb{N}}^{r}\times\Par
, the elements

(61)

\displaystyle\mathsf{h}^{\pm}_{\eta}({\mathbf{x}}_{r})h_{\lambda}[(1-t){\mathbf{x}}]

also form a basis for
\mathcal{P}(r)
.

Theorem 4.2.1

(
[
7
]
)
.

The
r
-nonsymmetric plethysm map
{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{r}\colon\mathcal{P}(r)\to\mathcal{P}(r)

acts on the basis in (
61
) as follows:

(62)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{r}\big(\hskip 0.85358pt\mathsf{h}^{\pm}_{\eta}({\mathbf{x}}_{r})h_{\lambda}[(1-t){\mathbf{x}}]\hskip 0.85358pt\big)=\mathsf{h}_{\eta}({\mathbf{x}}_{r})h_{\lambda}({\mathbf{x}}).

Proof.

This follows from the definition (
6
) of
{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{r}

and
[
7
, Definition 4.1.1 and Proposition 4.3.5]
.
∎

Lemma 4.2.2
.

For any
\eta\in{\mathbb{N}}^{r}
and partition
\lambda
, we have

(63)

d_{-}^{\mathsf{P}}\big(\hskip 0.85358pt\mathsf{h}_{\eta}^{\pm}({\mathbf{x}}_{r})h_{\lambda}[(1-t){\mathbf{x}}]\hskip 0.85358pt\big)=\mathsf{h}_{(\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{r-1})}^{\pm}({\mathbf{x}}_{r-1})h_{(\eta_{r},\lambda)_{+}}[(1-t){\mathbf{x}}],

where
(\eta_{r},\lambda)_{+}
denotes the partition rearrangement of the concatenation of
\eta_{r}
and
\lambda
.

Proof.

We use the description of
d_{-}^{\mathsf{P}}
in Proposition
3.2.3
.
Since the factor
\mathsf{h}_{(\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{r-1})}^{\pm}({\mathbf{x}}_{r-1})

of
\mathsf{h}_{\eta}^{\pm}({\mathbf{x}}_{r})
does not depend on
x_{r}
,
(
63
) reduces to the case
\eta_{1}=\dots=\eta_{r-1}=0
.
For this case, we let

Z=x_{r}+x_{r+1}+\cdots
and compute

\displaystyle d_{-}^{\mathsf{P}}\,h_{\eta_{r}}[x_{r}+(1-t)({\mathbf{x}}_{r-1})]\,h_{\lambda}[(1-t){\mathbf{x}}]

\displaystyle=d_{-}^{\mathsf{P}}\,h_{\eta_{r}}[x_{r}+(1-t)({\mathbf{x}}_{r-1})]\,h_{\lambda}[(1-t)({\mathbf{x}}_{r-1}+x_{r}+(x_{r+1}+\cdots))]

\displaystyle=\langle u^{0}\rangle h_{\eta_{r}}[u+(1-t)({\mathbf{x}}_{r-1})]\,h_{\lambda}[(1-t)({\mathbf{x}}_{r-1}+u+(Z-u))]\,\Omega[(1-t)u^{-1}Z]

\displaystyle=h_{\lambda}[(1-t)({\mathbf{x}}_{r-1}+Z)]\ \langle u^{0}\rangle h_{\eta_{r}}[u+(1-t)({\mathbf{x}}_{r-1})]\Omega[(1-t)u^{-1}Z]

\displaystyle=h_{\lambda}[(1-t)({\mathbf{x}}_{r-1}+Z)]\,h_{\eta_{r}}[(1-t)Z+(1-t)({\mathbf{x}}_{r-1})]

\displaystyle=h_{(\eta_{r},\lambda)_{+}}[(1-t){\mathbf{x}}],

where the second to last equality follows from a general plethystic
identity that we now verify:

\langle u^{0}\rangle h_{\eta_{r}}[u+A]\Omega[u^{-1}B]=\langle u^{0}\rangle\sum_{d\geq 0}u^{d}h_{\eta_{r}-d}[A]\Omega[u^{-1}B]=\sum_{d\geq 0}h_{d}[B]h_{\eta_{r}-d}[A]=h_{\eta_{r}}[B+A].\qed

Proposition 4.2.3
.

We have the following identity of operators
\mathcal{P}(r)\to\mathcal{P}(r-1)
,

(64)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{r-1}\,d_{-}^{\mathsf{P}}\,{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{r}^{-1}={\boldsymbol{\pi}}_{r}\,.

Proof.

We compute both sides on the basis of elements
\mathsf{h}_{\eta}({\mathbf{x}}_{r})h_{\lambda}({\mathbf{x}})
.
Since
h_{\eta_{r}}({\mathbf{x}}_{r})
is the Demazure character
{\mathcal{D}}_{(0^{r-1},\eta_{r})}
, we have

\pi_{x_{r},\dots,x_{N}}h_{\eta_{r}}({\mathbf{x}}_{r})=h_{\eta_{r}}({\mathbf{x}}_{N})
. Hence,

(65)

{\boldsymbol{\pi}}_{r}\,\big(\hskip 0.85358pt\mathsf{h}_{\eta}({\mathbf{x}}_{r})h_{\lambda}({\mathbf{x}})\hskip 0.85358pt\big)=\mathsf{h}_{(\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{r-1})}({\mathbf{x}}_{r-1})h_{(\eta_{r},\lambda)_{+}}({\mathbf{x}})\,.

On the other hand, Theorem
4.2.1
and Lemma
4.2.2
give
the matching identity

(66)

{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{r-1}\,d_{-}^{\mathsf{P}}\,{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{r}^{-1}\big(\hskip 0.85358pt\mathsf{h}_{\eta}({\mathbf{x}}_{r})h_{\lambda}({\mathbf{x}})\hskip 0.85358pt\big)=\mathsf{h}_{(\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{r-1})}({\mathbf{x}}_{r-1})h_{(\eta_{r},\lambda)_{+}}({\mathbf{x}})\,.\qed

5.
Flagged LLT polynomials

In
[
7
]
we
defined flagged versions of signed and unsigned LLT polynomials
and showed that the nonsymmetric plethysm map
takes signed flagged LLT polynomials to unsigned ones.
We begin this section
by stating a version of this result in a special case, using notation for LLT polynomials
in terms of a Dyck path which encodes the attacking pairs,
in line with the treatment of LLT polynomials
by Carlsson-Mellit
[
10
]
.
We then show (§
5.5
)
how the methods of
[
10
]
yield a formula for signed flagged LLT polynomials (in the above special case)
in terms of the Dyck path algebra.

5.1.
Dyck paths

We recall some definitions related to Dyck paths, following the
conventions in
[
29
]
. We also use terminology for partial Dyck paths
close to that in
[
10
]
.

For Dyck paths and skew shapes, we work with Cartesian coordinates
and refer to the lattice square with northeast corner
(c,r)
as the
box

(c,r)
,
and we say it is in row
r
and column
c
.

Let
a,b\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{+}
.
An
(a,b)
-Dyck path
is a sequence of north and east lattice steps going from

(0,0)
to
(a,b)
which stays weakly above the
diagonal
, the
line segment from
(0,0)
to
(a,b)
.
For an
(a,b)
-Dyck path
\pi
,
let
\Area(\pi)

denote the set of full boxes below
\pi
and above the diagonal.
Set
\area(\pi)=|\Area(\pi)|
.

An
r
-partial
d
-Dyck path

\pi^{\prime}
is a sequence of north and east lattice steps going from

(0,r)
to
(d,d)
which stays weakly above the diagonal from
(0,0)
to
(d,d)
.
Let
{\mathbb{D}}_{r}(d)
denote the set of
r
-partial
d
-Dyck paths.
Let
\pi^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{D}}_{r}(d)
.
We set
\Area(\pi^{\prime})=\Area(\mathsf{N}^{r}\pi^{\prime})
, where
\mathsf{N}^{r}\pi^{\prime}
is the
(d,d)
-Dyck path
obtained by adding
r
north steps to the southwest end of
\pi^{\prime}
.
A
corner
of
\pi^{\prime}
is a box above
\pi^{\prime}
such that the boxes immediately south and immediately east of it are below
\pi^{\prime}
;
a
marking

\Sigma
of
\pi^{\prime}
is a subset of the corners of
\pi^{\prime}
and its elements are
marked corners
.

Example 5.1.1
.

Shown below is a partial Dyck path
\pi^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{D}}_{2}(6)
,
with
|\Area(\pi^{\prime})|=7
, corners
\{(1,4),(3,5),(4,6)\}
, and a marking
\Sigma=\{(1,4),(4,6)\}
indicated by
\star
’s.

\star
\star

Returning to the case of any
a,b\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{+}
,
let
s_{-}=\frac{b}{a}-\epsilon
for small
\epsilon>0
.
We order north steps by the distance between their southern ends and the line
y=s_{-}x
.
Let
\pi
be an
(a,b)
-Dyck path.
The
sweeping reading order
is this order restricted to the north steps of
\pi
.
We say a north step
z
of
\pi

attacks
all the north steps of
\pi
which are greater than
z

in the sweeping reading order,
but less than the north step just above
z
(which need not be a north step of
\pi
).
Numbering the north steps of
\pi
in sweeping reading order,
the
attacking Dyck path
of
\pi
is the unique
(b,b)
-Dyck
path
\pi^{\prime}
such that a box
(i,j)
with
i<j
belongs to

\Area(\pi^{\prime})
if and only if the north steps numbered
i
and
j
form
an attacking pair in
\pi
. This is illustrated in

[
29
, Figure 1 (pg. 22)]
and
Figure
1
(a, b) below.

5.2.
(Signed) flagged row LLT polynomials

Given
\pi\in{\mathbb{D}}_{r}(d)
and a marking
\Sigma
of
\pi
,
a
(\pi,\Sigma)
-row semistandard word
is a map

T\colon[d]\rightarrow{\mathbb{Z}}_{+}
such that
T(j)\leq T(i)
for every
(i,j)\in\Sigma
.
We say
T
is
flagged
if
T(1)\leq 1,\hskip 0.85358pt\dots,\,T(r)\leq r
. Denote the set of
flagged
(\pi,\Sigma)
-row semistandard words by

\FSSYT(\pi,\Sigma)
.
An
attacking inversion
in
T
is
an ordered pair
(i,j)\in\Area(\pi)
such that
T(j)\leq T(i)
.
The number of attacking inversions in
T
is denoted
\inv(T)
.
(Note: this notion of attacking inversion will actually line up with
attacking non-inversions via a bijection with tableaux in §
5.3
.)

Definition 5.2.1
.

For
\pi\in{\mathbb{D}}_{r}(d)
and a marking
\Sigma
of
\pi
,
the associated
flagged row LLT polynomial
is given by

(67)

\displaystyle\mathsf{G}_{r}(\pi,\Sigma)({\mathbf{x}};t)=\sum_{T\in\FSSYT(\pi,\Sigma)}t^{\inv(T)}{\mathbf{x}}^{T},

where
{\mathbf{x}}^{T}=\prod_{i\in[d]}x_{T(i)}
.

Example 5.2.2
.

We illustrate
\FSSYT(\pi,\Sigma)
for the partial
Dyck path
\pi\in{\mathbb{D}}_{2}(4)
shown below and marking

\Sigma=\{(2,4)\}
, indicated with the
\star
.
The words
T\in\FSSYT(\pi,\Sigma)
are drawn with letters

T(1)T(2)T(3)T(4)
written along the diagonal from southwest to
northeast and
t
’s labeling the boxes of
\Area(\pi)
which are
attacking inversions.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t^{2}x_{1}^{3}x_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t^{2}x_{1}^{2}x_{2}^{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t\hskip 0.85358ptx_{1}^{2}x_{2}x_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t\hskip 0.85358ptx_{1}^{2}x_{2}^{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t^{2}x_{1}x_{2}^{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t\hskip 0.85358ptx_{1}x_{2}^{2}x_{3}}

Thus,

\mathsf{G}_{2}(\pi;\Sigma)({\mathbf{x}};t)=t^{3}x_{1}^{4}+2t^{2}x_{1}^{3}x_{2}+(t^{2}+t)x_{1}^{2}x_{2}^{2}+t^{2}x_{1}x_{2}^{3}+(t^{2}x_{1}^{3}+tx_{1}^{2}x_{2}+tx_{1}x_{2}^{2})e_{1}(x_{3},x_{4},\ldots)
.

We now let
{\mathcal{A}}
denote the totally ordered alphabet
1<\bar{1}<2<\bar{2}\cdots

consisting of a
positive letter

v
for each
v\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{+}

and a
negative letter

\bar{v}

for each
v\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{+}
.
Let
|\bar{v}|=v
for a negative letter
\bar{v}
and
|v|=v
for a positive letter
v
.
For letters
a,b\in{\mathcal{A}}
, we say that
ab
is
row-increasing

if
a<b
or
a
and
b
are equal positive letters;
we say that
ab
is
column-decreasing

if
a>b
or
a
and
b
are equal negative letters.

A
(\pi,\Sigma)
-row super word
is a map

T\colon[d]\rightarrow{\mathcal{A}}
such that for every
(i,j)\in\Sigma
,

T(j)T(i)
is row-increasing.
We say
T
is
flagged
if
T(1)\leq 1,\hskip 0.85358pt\dots,\,T(r)\leq r
. Denote the set of
flagged
(\pi,\Sigma)
-row super words by

\FSSYT^{\pm}(\pi,\Sigma)
.
An
attacking inversion
in
T
is an
ordered pair
(i,j)\in\Area(\pi)
such that

T(j)T(i)
is row-increasing.
The number of attacking inversions in
T
is denoted
\inv(T)
.

Note that a flagged
(\pi,\Sigma)
-row semistandard word is the same as
a flagged
(\pi,\Sigma)
-row super word with all its letters positive,
and the two notions of attacking inversions are compatible.

Definition 5.2.3
.

For
\pi\in{\mathbb{D}}_{r}(d)
and a marking
\Sigma
of
\pi
,
the associated
signed flagged row LLT polynomial
is given by

(68)

\displaystyle\mathsf{G}^{\pm}_{r}(\pi,\Sigma)({\mathbf{x}};t)=\sum_{T\in\FSSYT^{\pm}(\pi,\Sigma)}t^{\inv(T)}(-t)^{m(T)}{\mathbf{x}}^{|T|},

where
m(T)
denotes the number of negative letters in
T
,
and
{\mathbf{x}}^{|T|}=\prod_{i\in[d]}x_{|T(i)|}
.

5.3.
Comparison to the flagged LLT polynomials of
[
7
]

In
[
7
]
, we defined (signed) flagged LLT polynomials
in a general setting, where they are indexed by a permutation
\sigma
and a tuple

{\boldsymbol{\nu}}=(\nu^{(1)},\dots,\nu^{(p)})
in which each
\nu^{(i)}
is a generalization of skew shape called a ragged-right skew shape.
The (signed) flagged row LLT polynomials defined above are essentially a special case of these, as we now make precise.
To avoid some subtleties arising in the fully general setup of
[
7
]
,
we will only review this version in a special case, but one which is more than broad enough to include all the flagged LLT polynomials arising in this paper (see Remark
5.3.1
).

For
\alpha,\beta\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{l}
with
\beta_{1}\geq\cdots\geq\beta_{l}
,
\alpha_{1}\geq\cdots\geq\alpha_{l}
, and
\alpha_{j}\leq\beta_{j}
for all
j
, the (French style)
skew shape

\beta/\alpha
is the array of
boxes whose northeast corners have coordinates
(i,j)
for
j=1,\ldots,l
, and
\alpha_{j}<i\leq\beta_{j}
(if
\alpha_{j}=\beta_{j}
, then the
j
-th row of
\beta/\alpha
is empty).

Let
{\boldsymbol{\nu}}=(\nu^{(1)},\dots,\nu^{(p)})
be a tuple of skew shapes.
We consider the set of boxes of
{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
to be the disjoint union of the boxes
of each
\nu^{(k)}
, and define
the
adjusted content
of a box
b

in row
j
, column
i
of the skew shape
\nu^{(k)}

to be
\tilde{c}(b)=i-j+k\,\epsilon
,
where
\epsilon
is a fixed positive number such that
p\,\epsilon<1
.
The
reading order
on
{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
is the total order
<
on the
boxes of
{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
such that
a<b\Rightarrow\tilde{c}(a)\leq\tilde{c}(b)
and
it increases from southwest to northeast on boxes of fixed adjusted content.
Let

{\boldsymbol{{\boldsymbol{\nu}}}[1]},\dots,{\boldsymbol{{\boldsymbol{\nu}}}[d]}

denote the boxes of

{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
in increasing reading order.

Let
b_{1},\dots,b_{k}
be the boxes of
{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
which are on the eastern end of their row and ordered so that

b_{1}>\dots>b_{k}
in reading order. Let
r\in\{0,1,\dots,k\}
.
A super tableau on
{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
is a map

T^{\prime}\colon{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{A}}
,
weakly increasing along rows and
columns, with positive letters increasing strictly on columns and
negative letters increasing strictly on rows.
We say
T^{\prime}
is
flagged
if

T^{\prime}(b_{1})\leq 1
,
T^{\prime}(b_{2})\leq 2
,
\ldots,

T^{\prime}(b_{r})\leq r
(when
r=0
, this is no extra condition).

An ordered pair
of boxes
(a,b)
in
{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
is an
attacking pair
if
0<\tilde{c}(b)-\tilde{c}(a)<1
.
An
attacking inversion
in
T^{\prime}
is
an attacking pair
(a,b)
such that
T^{\prime}(a)T^{\prime}(b)
is column-decreasing
(as defined before Definition
5.2.3
).
We define
\inv(T^{\prime})
to be the number of
attacking inversions in
T^{\prime}
.

Define

(69)

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{r,{\boldsymbol{\nu}}}({\mathbf{x}};t)

\displaystyle=\sum_{T^{\prime}}t^{\inv(T^{\prime})}{\mathbf{x}}^{T^{\prime}},

(70)

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}^{\pm}_{r,{\boldsymbol{\nu}}}({\mathbf{x}};t)

\displaystyle=\sum_{T^{\prime}}t^{\inv(T^{\prime})}(-t)^{-m(T^{\prime})}{\mathbf{x}}^{|T^{\prime}|},

where the sum in (
70
) is over flagged super tableaux
T^{\prime}
on
{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
, the sum in (
69
) is over the subset of these
with all positive entries, and
{\mathbf{x}}^{T^{\prime}}
,
{\mathbf{x}}^{|T^{\prime}|}
, and
m(T^{\prime})
are as in §
5.2
.

Remark 5.3.1
.

(i) In the language of
[
7
, §§6.1–6.4 and eq. (302)]
, assuming that
{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
has no empty rows,

\mathcal{G}_{r,{\boldsymbol{\nu}}}({\mathbf{x}};t)
and
\mathcal{G}^{\pm}_{r,{\boldsymbol{\nu}}}({\mathbf{x}};t)
are equal to

{\mathcal{G}}_{{\boldsymbol{\nu}},\sigma}[x_{1},\dots,x_{r},Y,0,\dots,0;t]
and
{\mathcal{G}}^{-}_{{\boldsymbol{\nu}},\sigma}(x_{1},\dots,x_{r},Y,0,\dots,0)
, respectively,
where
Y=x_{r+1}+x_{r+2}+\cdots
, and
\sigma
is the standard compatible permutation.
To see that these definitions match, one can use

[
7
, Theorem 6.4.10 and Proposition 6.7.2]
as well as
[
7
, Remark 6.4.9(i)]
,
which shows that certain extra attacking pairs present in the definition
in
[
7
]
, but not here, do not actually contribute to

\inv
in this case.

(ii) The no empty rows assumption in (i) is necessary because empty rows play a significant bookkeeping role in
[
7
]
, which we have not used here.
This is harmless, however, since any
{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
can be replaced by a tuple
\hat{{\boldsymbol{\nu}}}
without empty rows for which the super tableaux on
{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
and
\hat{{\boldsymbol{\nu}}}

are in a natural bijection that lines up attacking pairs.

To realize the flagged LLT polynomials of §
5.2
as a special case of those just defined, we need the following lemma.

Lemma 5.3.2
.

Let
\pi^{\prime}
be a
(d,d)
-Dyck path and
\Sigma
be a marking of
\pi^{\prime}
. Then
there exists a tuple of single-row skew shapes
{\boldsymbol{\mu}}=(\mu^{(1)},\dots,\mu^{(p)})
such that, for all
i<j
,

(71)

\displaystyle\text{$(i,j)\in\Area(\pi^{\prime})$ }\ \ \iff\ \ \text{${\boldsymbol{\mu}}[i],{\boldsymbol{\mu}}[j]$ forms an attacking pair of ${\boldsymbol{\mu}}$, and }

\displaystyle\text{$(i,j)\in\Sigma$ }\ \ \iff\ \ {\boldsymbol{\mu}}[i],{\boldsymbol{\mu}}[j]\text{ forms a horizontal domino in some }\mu^{(k)}.

Proof.

Let
C
denote the set of all corners of
\pi^{\prime}
.

The case
\Sigma=C
is implicit in
[
15
]
, and further explained in
[
10
, §2.4]
.
Note that there are typically many
{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
satisfying (
71
) for a given
\pi^{\prime},\Sigma
, but for
\Sigma=C
there is a
convenient choice arising from the combinatorics of the shuffle theorem.
In the notation of §
5.1
, the correspondence
\pi\mapsto\pi^{\prime}
is bijective for
a=b=d
by
[
17
]
;
then let
\pi
be the unique
(d,d)
-Dyck path such that its attacking Dyck path is
\pi^{\prime}
,
so that
\Area(\pi^{\prime})
coincides with the attacking pairs of north steps of
\pi
.
Then the tuple
{\boldsymbol{\mu}}=(\mu^{(1)},\dots,\mu^{(p)})
of single-row skew shapes given by the runs of consecutive north steps in
\pi

satisfies (
71
).
More precisely, if
R_{1},\dots,R_{p}
are the runs of north steps from south to north, with their southern ends at
(x_{1},y_{1}),\dots,(x_{p},y_{p})
and with lengths
l_{1},\dots,l_{p}
, then
\mu^{(i)}=(l_{i}+y_{i}-x_{i})/(y_{i}-x_{i})
. See Figure
1
(a)–(c).

In the general case, we obtain a desired tuple of single-row skew shapes by modifying the
{\boldsymbol{\mu}}

from the previous paragraph.
Let
(i,j)\in C
; this is also a pair such that

{\boldsymbol{\mu}}[i],{\boldsymbol{\mu}}[j]
forms a horizontal domino.
Let
\tilde{{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}=(\mu^{(1)},\dots,\mu^{(k-1)},\theta,\tau,\mu^{(k+1)},\dots,\mu^{(p)})
be the tuple of single-row skew shapes obtained by
replacing
\mu^{(k)}
with the two single-row skew shapes
\theta
and
\tau
formed from breaking
\mu^{(k)}

between
{\boldsymbol{\mu}}[i]
and
{\boldsymbol{\mu}}[j]
; there are two ways to do this, and the one we want is where
\tilde{{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}[i]\in\theta
and

\tilde{{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}[j]\in\tau
so that
the attacking pairs of
\tilde{{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}
and
{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
are the same. Then the tuple
\tilde{{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}
and Dyck path
\pi^{\prime}
with marking
C\setminus\{(i,j)\}
satisfy (
71
).
This row breaking can be done independently for each
(i,j)\in C
,
so we can handle the case of an arbitrary marking by breaking rows for each pair deleted from
C
.
∎

Now let
\hat{\pi}^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{D}}_{r}(d)
and
\Sigma
be a marking of
\hat{\pi}^{\prime}
.
Let
\pi^{\prime}=\mathsf{N}^{r}\hat{\pi}^{\prime}
be the corresponding
(d,d)
-Dyck path
and choose a tuple
{\boldsymbol{\mu}}=((\beta_{1})/(\alpha_{1}),\dots,(\beta_{p})/(\alpha_{p}))
so that (
71
) holds.
Form

(72)

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\nu}}

\displaystyle=((-\alpha_{p})/(-\beta_{p}),\dots,(-\alpha_{1})/(-\beta_{1})),

which satisfies

(73)

\displaystyle\text{$(d-j,d-i)\in\Area(\pi^{\prime})$ }\ \ \iff\ \ \text{${\boldsymbol{\nu}}[i],{\boldsymbol{\nu}}[j]$ forms an attacking pair of ${\boldsymbol{\nu}}$, and }

\displaystyle\text{$(d-j,d-i)\in\Sigma$ }\ \ \iff\ \ {\boldsymbol{\nu}}[i],{\boldsymbol{\nu}}[j]\text{ forms a horizontal domino in some }\nu^{(k)}.

See Figure
1
(d).

There is a bijection taking a flagged
(\hat{\pi}^{\prime},\Sigma)
-row super word
T
to a flagged super tableau
T^{\prime}
on
{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
given by
setting
T^{\prime}({\boldsymbol{{\boldsymbol{\nu}}}[i]})=T(d-i)
. To see that the flagging conditions match, note that every
(i,j)
with
1\leq i<j\leq r
lies in
\Area(\hat{\pi}^{\prime})

and correspondingly the last
r
boxes of
{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
in reading order are mutually attacking; moreover, these boxes are in distinct rows and all lie on the eastern end of their row. Hence, the flagging condition on
T^{\prime}
is
T^{\prime}({\boldsymbol{{\boldsymbol{\nu}}}[d]})\leq 1,\dots,T^{\prime}({\boldsymbol{{\boldsymbol{\nu}}}[d-r]})\leq r
, which matches the flagging condition

T(1)\leq 1,\dots,T(r)\leq r
on
T
.
The attacking inversions in
T
exactly line up with attacking noninversions in
T^{\prime}
.
So the number of attacking inversions in
T^{\prime}
is equal to
|\Area(\hat{\pi}^{\prime})|-\inv(T)
, and thus

(74)

\displaystyle\mathsf{G}^{\pm}_{r}(\hat{\pi}^{\prime},\Sigma)({\mathbf{x}};t)=t^{|\Area(\hat{\pi}^{\prime})|}\mathcal{G}^{\pm}_{r,{\boldsymbol{\nu}}}({\mathbf{x}};t^{-1}).

By considering only the super words with positive letters and super tableaux with positive entries, we also have

(75)

\displaystyle\mathsf{G}_{r}(\hat{\pi}^{\prime},\Sigma)({\mathbf{x}};t)=t^{|\Area(\hat{\pi}^{\prime})|}\mathcal{G}_{r,{\boldsymbol{\nu}}}({\mathbf{x}};t^{-1}).

Figure 1.

In (a) and (b),
\pi^{\prime}
is the attacking Dyck path of
\pi
.
For the given

\pi^{\prime}
and marking
\Sigma
,
(c) gives the tuple
{\boldsymbol{\mu}}=((\beta_{1})/(\alpha_{1}),\dots,(\beta_{p})/(\alpha_{p}))
from the proof of Lemma
5.3.2

such that (
71
) holds. Reversing and negating
\beta
and
\alpha
gives the
tuple
{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
in (d), for which the flagged row LLT polynomial
\mathsf{G}_{r}(\hat{\pi}^{\prime},\Sigma)
equals

t^{|\Area(\hat{\pi}^{\prime})|}\mathcal{G}_{r,{\boldsymbol{\nu}}}({\mathbf{x}};t^{-1})
.
See §
5.3
.

5.4.
Nonsymmetric plethysm and flagged row LLT polynomials

Theorem 5.4.1

(
[
7
]
)
.

The
r
-nonsymmetric plethysm map takes signed flagged LLT polynomials to flagged LLT polynomials:
for
r\in{\mathbb{N}}
and any tuple of skew shapes
{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
with at least
r
boxes,

(76)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{r}(\mathcal{G}^{\pm}_{r,{\boldsymbol{\nu}}}({\mathbf{x}};t^{-1}))=\mathcal{G}_{r,{\boldsymbol{\nu}}}({\mathbf{x}};t^{-1}).

In particular, it takes signed flagged row LLT polynomials to flagged row LLT polynomials:
for
\pi\in{\mathbb{D}}_{r}(d)
and a marking
\Sigma
of
\pi
,

(77)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{r}(\mathsf{G}^{\pm}_{r}(\pi,\Sigma))=\mathsf{G}_{r}(\pi,\Sigma).

Proof.

The first identity follows from
[
7
, Lemma 7.6.4]

and Remark
5.3.1
.
The second identity is then obtained from (
74
) and (
75
).
∎

.

77
) for the partial Dyck path
\pi\in{\mathbb{D}}_{2}(3)
shown below and marking
\Sigma=\{(1,3)\}
(indicated with the
\star
).

(\pi,\Sigma)

.

t^{\inv(T)}&t&t^{2}&t\\[1.70717pt]
(-t)^{m(T)}&1&1&-t\end{array}

By Definition
5.2.3
, we have

(78)

\displaystyle\mathsf{G}^{\pm}_{2}(\pi,\Sigma)({\mathbf{x}};t)=t\hskip 0.85358ptx_{1}^{2}x_{2}+t^{2}x_{1}^{3}+t(-t)x_{1}^{3}=t\hskip 0.85358ptx_{1}^{2}x_{2},

while
\mathsf{G}_{2}(\pi,\Sigma)
is the sum of
t^{\inv(T)}{\mathbf{x}}^{T}
over the first two words, giving

(79)

\displaystyle\mathsf{G}_{2}(\pi,\Sigma)({\mathbf{x}};t)=t^{2}x_{1}^{3}+t\hskip 0.85358ptx_{1}^{2}x_{2}.

By the definition (
6
) of
{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{r}
, we have

(80)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{2}(t\,x_{1}^{2}x_{2})=t\pol(x_{1}^{2}x_{2}+t\hskip 0.85358ptx_{1}^{3}+t^{2}x_{1}^{4}x_{2}^{-1}+t^{3}x_{1}^{5}x_{2}^{-2}+\cdots)=t\hskip 0.85358pt(x_{1}^{2}x_{2}+t\,x_{1}^{3}),

where to compute the polynomial truncation we have used that all monomials appearing here are Demazure characters.
This verifies (
77
) in this case.

We will also need the following lemma which gives another combinatorial description of the signed flagged row LLT polynomials.
We say that a word
T\in\FSSYT^{\pm}(\pi,\Sigma)

is
non-attacking
if

|T(i)|\neq|T(j)|
for every
(i,j)\in\Area(\pi)
.

Lemma 5.4.3

(
[
7
]
)
.

For any
\pi\in{\mathbb{D}}_{r}(d)
and marking
\Sigma
of
\pi
,

(81)

\displaystyle\mathsf{G}^{\pm}_{r}(\pi,\Sigma)({\mathbf{x}};t)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}T\in\FSSYT^{\pm}(\pi,\Sigma)\\
T\text{ non-attacking}\end{subarray}}t^{\inv(T)}(-t)^{m(T)}{\mathbf{x}}^{|T|}.

Proof.

The analogous result for the
\mathcal{G}^{\pm}_{r,{\boldsymbol{\nu}}}({\mathbf{x}};t^{-1})
follows from
[
7
, Proposition 6.7.9]
,
and then (
74
) gives (
81
).
∎

Example 5.4.4
.

In Example
5.4.2
, only the first word is non-attacking. As we saw in (
78
), the terms for the other two words cancel, illustrating Lemma
5.4.3
.

5.5.
Signed flagged row LLT polynomials and the Dyck path algebra

Definition 5.5.1
.

For
\pi\in{\mathbb{D}}_{r}(d)
and a marking
\Sigma
of
\pi
,
let
\chi^{\mathsf{P}}_{r}(\pi,\Sigma)
be the result of applying the following
sequence of operators to
1\in\mathcal{P}(0)
, determined
by traveling along
\pi
and
\Sigma

starting from the northeast endpoint of
\pi
:

(i)

\frac{1}{t-1}[d_{-}^{\mathsf{P}},-d_{+}^{\mathsf{P}}]
for each marked corner in
\Sigma
,

(ii)

d_{-}^{\mathsf{P}}
for each vertical step not part of a marked corner,

(iii)

-d_{+}^{\mathsf{P}}
for each horizontal step not part of a marked corner.

Example 5.5.2
.

For the partial Dyck path
\pi^{\prime}
and marking
\Sigma
in Example
5.1.1
,

(82)

\displaystyle\chi^{\mathsf{P}}_{2}(\pi^{\prime},\Sigma)=\frac{(-1)^{6}}{(t-1)^{2}}\,d_{-}^{\mathsf{P}}\,[d_{-}^{\mathsf{P}},d_{+}^{\mathsf{P}}]\,d_{+}^{\mathsf{P}}\,d_{+}^{\mathsf{P}}\,d_{-}^{\mathsf{P}}\,[d_{-}^{\mathsf{P}},d_{+}^{\mathsf{P}}]\,d_{+}^{\mathsf{P}}\,d_{+}^{\mathsf{P}}\cdot 1

Our next result provides a combinatorial expression for
\chi_{r}^{P}(\pi,\Sigma)
. Our proof builds on a fundamental theorem from

[
10
]
, which we now recall.

The objects
\chi_{r}(\pi)
and
\chi^{\prime}_{r}(\pi)
defined in
[
10
, eq. (4.5)]
and after
[
10
, [eq. (4.3)]
are readily written in our notation as

(83)

\displaystyle\chi_{r}(\pi)=(t-1)^{d-r}\,{\mathsf{Q}}_{r}{\mathsf{P}}_{r}(\chi^{\prime}_{r}(\pi))\qquad\text{for}\qquad\chi^{\prime}_{r}(\pi)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}T\in\FSSYT(\pi,\varnothing)\\
T\text{ non-attacking}\end{subarray}}t^{\inv(T)}{\mathbf{x}}^{T}\,.

Note that
our definition of
\inv
differs from that of
[
10
, §2.4]
for equal letters but this does not arise here because of the non-attacking condition.
The proof of
[
10
, Theorem 4.4]
gives the following result.

Theorem 5.5.3

(
[
10
]
)
.

For
\pi\in{\mathbb{D}}_{r}(d)
,
\chi_{r}(\pi)
is
the result of applying the same sequence of operators to
1
as in
Definition
5.5.1
with
\Sigma=\varnothing
, except with
d_{-}^{CM},d_{+}^{CM}
in place
of
d_{-}^{\mathsf{P}},-d_{+}^{\mathsf{P}}
in (ii) and (iii).

We now prove an identity for
\chi_{r}^{P}(\pi,\Sigma)
, with any marking
\Sigma
, framed in terms of signed rather than non-attacking fillings.
This formulation will allow us to apply the powerful tool of nonsymmetric plethysm
to transform these objects to ones on the modified side (i.e. the right side of Figure
2
),
where there is visible potential for positivity and connections to representation theory.

Theorem 5.5.4
.

For
\pi\in{\mathbb{D}}_{r}(d)
and a marking
\Sigma
of
\pi
,

(84)

\displaystyle\mathsf{G}^{\pm}_{r}(\pi,\Sigma)=\chi^{\mathsf{P}}_{r}(\pi,\Sigma).

Proof.

We first establish the case
\Sigma=\varnothing
.
By Lemma
5.4.3
,

(85)

\displaystyle\mathsf{G}^{\pm}_{r}(\pi,\varnothing)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}T\in\FSSYT^{\pm}(\pi,\varnothing)\\
T\text{ non-attacking}\end{subarray}}t^{\inv(T)}(-t)^{m(T)}{\mathbf{x}}^{|T|}.

For
T
in this sum, the non-attacking and flagging conditions force
T(1)=1,\,\dots,\,T(r)=r
. Moreover, because of the non-attacking condition,
the set of words in (
85
) can be built from the set in (
83
) by freely choosing the signs on
T(r+1),\dots,T(d)
. It follows that

(86)

\displaystyle\mathsf{G}^{\pm}_{r}(\pi,\varnothing)=(1-t)^{d-r}\chi^{\prime}_{r}(\pi)\,.

Combining this with (
83
) gives

(87)

\displaystyle\mathsf{G}^{\pm}_{r}(\pi,\varnothing)=(-1)^{d-r}\hskip 0.85358pt{\mathsf{Q}}_{r}^{-1}{\mathsf{P}}_{r}^{-1}(\chi_{r}(\pi)).

On the other hand, Theorem
5.5.3
and (
39
)–(
40
) give

(88)

\displaystyle\chi^{\mathsf{P}}_{r}(\pi,\varnothing)=(-1)^{d-r}\hskip 0.85358pt{\mathsf{Q}}_{r}^{-1}{\mathsf{P}}_{r}^{-1}(\chi_{r}(\pi)).

Combining the last two equations proves the desired identity
\mathsf{G}^{\pm}_{r}(\pi,\varnothing)=\chi^{\mathsf{P}}_{r}(\pi,\varnothing)
.

We now address the case
\Sigma\neq\varnothing
, using an argument similar to the one going from Theorem 4.4 to 4.6 in
[
10
]
.
Consider a fixed
(i,j)\in\Sigma
.
Let
\pi^{*}\in{\mathbb{D}}_{r}(d)
be the path obtained by flipping the corner
(i,j)
inside out, i.e., by replacing the
\mathsf{E}\mathsf{N}
steps bordering the box
(i,j)
to
\mathsf{N}\mathsf{E}
,
so that box
(i,j)
now lies below
\pi^{*}
.
Set
\Sigma^{*}=\Sigma\setminus\{(i,j)\}
.
Directly from Definition
5.5.1
, we have

(89)

(t-1)\chi^{\mathsf{P}}_{r}(\pi,\Sigma)=\chi^{\mathsf{P}}_{r}(\pi^{*},\Sigma^{*})-\chi^{\mathsf{P}}_{r}(\pi,\Sigma^{*})\,.

Repeatedly applying identities of this form over the different elements of
\Sigma
allows
\chi^{\mathsf{P}}_{r}(\pi,\Sigma)
to be
written as an alternating sum of
2^{|\Sigma|}
terms from the
\Sigma=\varnothing
case.
Thus, it suffices to show that
\mathsf{G}^{\pm}_{r}(\pi,\Sigma)
satisfies the same recursion.

First note that
\FSSYT^{\pm}(\pi,\Sigma^{*})
and
\FSSYT^{\pm}(\pi^{*},\Sigma^{*})
are equal as sets, though inversions are counted differently; let
S
denote either of these sets.

Say
a word

T\in S

is
row-like
(resp.
column-like
) if
T(j)T(i)
is row-increasing (resp. column-decreasing).
For column-like
T\in S
,
(i,j)
is never an attacking inversion,
hence the contributions from the column-like words in

\FSSYT^{\pm}(\pi,\Sigma^{*})
and
\FSSYT^{\pm}(\pi^{*},\Sigma^{*})
cancel in

(90)

\mathsf{G}^{\pm}_{r}(\pi^{*},\Sigma^{*})-\mathsf{G}^{\pm}_{r}(\pi,\Sigma^{*})\,.

On the other hand, since
(i,j)\notin\Area(\pi)
, for row-like
T\in S
,

(i,j)
is never an attacking inversion of
T
regarded as an element of

\FSSYT^{\pm}(\pi,\Sigma^{*})
but always one when
T
is regarded as an element of

\FSSYT^{\pm}(\pi^{*},\Sigma^{*})
.
Thus, the contribution of the row-like words of

\FSSYT^{\pm}(\pi^{*},\Sigma^{*})

to
\mathsf{G}^{\pm}_{r}(\pi^{*},\Sigma^{*})
is
t
times
the contribution of the row-like words of

\FSSYT^{\pm}(\pi,\Sigma^{*})

to
\mathsf{G}^{\pm}_{r}(\pi,\Sigma^{*})
, and
it follows that (
90
) is equal to

(t-1)\mathsf{G}^{\pm}_{r}(\pi,\Sigma)
. Hence,
\mathsf{G}^{\pm}_{r}(\pi,\Sigma)
satisfies the same recursion as
\chi^{\mathsf{P}}_{r}(\pi,\Sigma)
, completing the proof.
∎

Remark 5.5.5
.

The non-attacking description of
\mathsf{G}^{\pm}_{r}(\pi,\Sigma)({\mathbf{x}};t)
is particularly nice in the unicellular case (
\Sigma=\varnothing
) because it reduces to a sum over positive fillings times a power of
(1-t)
.
Outside this case, we have not found a similar simplification for the non-attacking description, and we instead believe that the signed filling description is more natural.

5.6.
(Signed) flagged column LLT polynomials

We will also need a variant of the results above which correspond to the flagged LLT polynomials of

[
7
]
for a tuple of single-column skew shapes.

A
(\pi,\Sigma)
-column semistandard word
is a map

T\colon[d]\rightarrow{\mathbb{Z}}_{+}
such that for every
(i,j)\in\Sigma
,

T(i)<T(j)
.
More generally, a
(\pi,\Sigma)
-column super word
is a map

T\colon[d]\rightarrow{\mathcal{A}}
such that for every
(i,j)\in\Sigma
,

T(j)T(i)
is column-decreasing.
We say
T
is
flagged
if
T(1)\leq 1,\dots,\,T(r)\leq r
.
Denote the set of
flagged
(\pi,\Sigma)
-column semistandard words
(resp. flagged
(\pi,\Sigma)
-column super words) by

\FCSSYT(\pi,\Sigma)
(resp.
\FCSSYTpm(\pi,\Sigma)
).
Define

(91)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\inv}(T)=\#\big\{(i,j)\in\Area(\pi):\text{$T(j)T(i)$ is column-decreasing}\big\}.

Definition 5.6.1
.

For
\pi\in{\mathbb{D}}_{r}(d)
and a marking
\Sigma
of
\pi
,
the associated
flagged column LLT polynomial
is given by

(92)

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathsf{G}}_{r}(\pi,\Sigma)({\mathbf{x}};t)=\sum_{T\in\FCSSYT(\pi,\Sigma)}t^{\widetilde{\inv}(T)}{\mathbf{x}}^{T},

and the
signed flagged column LLT polynomial
by

(93)

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathsf{G}}^{\pm}_{r}(\pi,\Sigma)({\mathbf{x}};t)=\sum_{T\in\FCSSYTpm(\pi,\Sigma)}t^{\widetilde{\inv}(T)}(-t)^{-m(T)}{\mathbf{x}}^{|T|}.

Remark 5.6.2
.

Similar to §
5.3
, the (signed) flagged column LLT polynomials are special cases of those in
[
7
]
.
Let
\hat{\pi}^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{D}}_{r}(d)
and
\Sigma
be a marking of
\hat{\pi}^{\prime}
.
As discussed in §
5.3
,
there exists a tuple of single-row skew shapes
{\boldsymbol{\nu}}=(\nu^{(1)},\dots,\nu^{(d)})
so that (
73
) holds.
Now let

{\boldsymbol{\eta}}=(\eta^{(1)},\dots,\eta^{(d)})

be the tuple of single-column skew shapes
such that the contents occupied by the boxes of
\eta^{(i)}
are the same as those of
\nu^{(i)}
.
We have

(94)

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathsf{G}}_{r}(\hat{\pi}^{\prime},\Sigma)({\mathbf{x}};t)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{G}_{r,{\boldsymbol{\eta}}}({\mathbf{x}};t),

(95)

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathsf{G}}^{\pm}_{r}(\hat{\pi}^{\prime},\Sigma)({\mathbf{x}};t)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{G}^{\pm}_{r,{\boldsymbol{\eta}}}({\mathbf{x}};t).

Then (
76
) implies

(96)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{r}\big(\tilde{\mathsf{G}}^{\pm}_{r}(\hat{\pi}^{\prime},\Sigma)({\mathbf{x}};t^{-1})\big)=\tilde{\mathsf{G}}_{r}(\hat{\pi}^{\prime},\Sigma)({\mathbf{x}};t^{-1}).

We have the following variant of
\chi^{\mathsf{P}}_{r}(\pi,\Sigma)
for the signed flagged column LLT polynomials.

Definition 5.6.3
.

For
\pi\in{\mathbb{D}}_{r}(d)
and a marking
\Sigma
of
\pi
,
let
\tilde{\chi}^{\mathsf{P}}_{r}(\pi,\Sigma)
be the result of applying the following
sequence of operators to
1\in\mathcal{P}(0)
, determined
by traveling along
\pi
and
\Sigma

starting from the northeast endpoint of
\pi
:

(i)
