\frac{1}{t^{-1}-1}\big(d_{-}^{\mathsf{P}}(-t^{-r}d_{+}^{\mathsf{P}})-(-t^{-r+1}d_{+}^{\mathsf{P}})d_{-}^{\mathsf{P}}\big)
for each marked corner in
\Sigma
,

(ii)

d_{-}^{\mathsf{P}}
for each vertical step not part of a marked corner,

(iii)

-t^{-r}d_{+}^{\mathsf{P}}
for each horizontal step not part of a marked corner,

where
r=h-v
when we have so far visited
h
horizontal steps and
v
vertical steps.

Theorem 5.6.4
.

For
\pi\in{\mathbb{D}}_{r}(d)
and a marking
\Sigma
of
\pi
,

(97)

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathsf{G}}^{\pm}_{r}(\pi,\Sigma)(x;t^{-1})=\tilde{\chi}^{\mathsf{P}}_{r}(\pi,\Sigma)(x;t).

Proof.

First we address the case
\Sigma=\varnothing
.
Then (i) never arises in Definition
5.6.3
and we have

t^{|\Area(\pi)|}\tilde{\chi}^{\mathsf{P}}_{r}(\pi,\varnothing)=\chi^{\mathsf{P}}_{r}(\pi,\varnothing)
.
Hence, by Theorem
5.5.4
, (
74
), and (
95
), we have

(98)

\displaystyle t^{|\Area(\pi)|}\tilde{\chi}^{\mathsf{P}}_{r}(\pi,\varnothing)=\chi^{\mathsf{P}}_{r}(\pi,\varnothing)=\mathsf{G}^{\pm}_{r}(\pi,\varnothing)(x;t)=t^{|\Area(\pi)|}\tilde{\mathsf{G}}^{\pm}_{r}(\pi,\varnothing)(x;t^{-1}),

thus establishing (
97
) for
\Sigma=\varnothing
.
The case
\Sigma\neq\varnothing
now follows by an argument similar
to the analogous step in the proof of Theorem
5.5.4
.
∎

5.7.
Symmetrization

Here we relate flagged row and column LLT polynomials to ordinary symmetric LLT polynomials.

Let
{\mathcal{G}}_{{\boldsymbol{\nu}}}({\mathbf{x}};t)
denote the ordinary symmetric LLT polynomial in the sense of

[
15
]
indexed by the tuple of skew shapes
{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
and defined in terms attacking inversions.
The definition of these is exactly the
r=0
special case of the
\mathcal{G}_{r,{\boldsymbol{\nu}}}({\mathbf{x}};t)
defined in (
69
), i.e.,

\mathcal{G}_{0,{\boldsymbol{\nu}}}({\mathbf{x}};t)={\mathcal{G}}_{{\boldsymbol{\nu}}}({\mathbf{x}};t)
.

Remark 5.7.1
.

Let
\hat{\pi}^{\prime}
,
\Sigma
,
{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
, and
{\boldsymbol{\eta}}
be as in
Remark
5.6.2
, now with
r=0
(so
\hat{\pi}^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{D}}_{0}(d)
and we set
\pi^{\prime}=\hat{\pi}^{\prime}
as this is consistent with the notation in §
5.3
).

(i) The flagged row LLT polynomials are symmetric LLT polynomials when
r=0
.
Specifically,
(
75
) followed by
[
6
, Proposition 4.1.6]
yields

(99)

\displaystyle\mathsf{G}_{0}(\pi^{\prime},\Sigma)({\mathbf{x}};t)=t^{|\Area(\pi^{\prime})|}{\mathcal{G}}_{{\boldsymbol{\nu}}}({\mathbf{x}};t^{-1})=\omega\,{\mathcal{G}}_{{\boldsymbol{\eta}}}({\mathbf{x}};t).

(ii) Similarly, flagged column LLT polynomials are symmetric LLT polynomials: by (
94
),

(100)

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathsf{G}}_{0}(\pi^{\prime},\Sigma)({\mathbf{x}};t)={\mathcal{G}}_{{\boldsymbol{\eta}}}({\mathbf{x}};t).

(iii)
The symmetric LLT polynomials in (
100
) also match
the version of symmetric LLT polynomials
\chi(\pi^{\prime},\Sigma)
defined in
[
29
, §3.5]
;
the definition of
\chi(\pi^{\prime},\Sigma)
is the same as
\tilde{\mathsf{G}}_{0}(\pi^{\prime},\Sigma)({\mathbf{x}};t)

except that
in
\chi(\pi^{\prime},\Sigma)

the condition

T(i)<T(j)
for
(i,j)\in\Sigma
is replaced with
T(i)>T(j)
, and the
attacking inversion condition

T(i)<T(j)
for
(i,j)\in\Area(\pi^{\prime})
is replaced with
T(i)>T(j)
.
But since
\chi(\pi^{\prime},\Sigma)
is symmetric, reversing the order of the variables does not change the function, so
we indeed have

(101)

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathsf{G}}_{0}(\pi^{\prime},\Sigma)({\mathbf{x}};t)={\mathcal{G}}_{{\boldsymbol{\eta}}}({\mathbf{x}};t)=\chi(\pi^{\prime},\Sigma).

Proposition 5.7.2
.

(i) (Signed) flagged row LLT polynomials match up as follows under Hecke and Weyl symmetrization:
for
\theta\in{\mathbb{D}}_{r}(d)
and a marking
\Sigma
of
\theta
,

(102)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{G}^{\pm}_{r}(\theta,\Sigma)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{G}^{\pm}_{r-1}(\mathsf{N}\theta,\Sigma)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{G}_{r}(\theta,\Sigma)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{G}_{r-1}(\mathsf{N}\theta,\Sigma)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{{\mathcal{S}}}_{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{r-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\boldsymbol{\pi}}_{r}}

where
\mathsf{N}\theta\in{\mathbb{D}}_{r-1}(d)
is the result of adding a north step to the southwest end of
\theta
.
In particular, in the notation of Remark
5.7.1
with
\pi^{\prime}=\mathsf{N}^{r}\theta
,

(103)

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\pi}}_{1}\cdots{\boldsymbol{\pi}}_{r}(\mathsf{G}_{r}(\theta,\Sigma))=\mathsf{G}_{0}(\mathsf{N}^{r}\theta,\Sigma)=\omega\,{\mathcal{G}}_{{\boldsymbol{\eta}}}({\mathbf{x}};t).

(ii) Similarly, (signed) flagged column LLT polynomials match up as follows under Hecke and Weyl symmetrization:

(104)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\mathsf{G}}^{\pm}_{r}(\theta,\Sigma)({\mathbf{x}};t^{-1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\mathsf{G}}^{\pm}_{r-1}(\mathsf{N}\theta,\Sigma)({\mathbf{x}};t^{-1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\mathsf{G}}_{r}(\theta,\Sigma)({\mathbf{x}};t^{-1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\mathsf{G}}_{r-1}(\mathsf{N}\theta,\Sigma)({\mathbf{x}};t^{-1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{{\mathcal{S}}}_{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{r-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\boldsymbol{\pi}}_{r}}

In particular,

(105)

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\pi}}_{1}\cdots{\boldsymbol{\pi}}_{r}(\tilde{\mathsf{G}}_{r}(\theta,\Sigma))=\tilde{\mathsf{G}}_{0}(\mathsf{N}^{r}\theta,\Sigma)={\mathcal{G}}_{{\boldsymbol{\eta}}}({\mathbf{x}};t).

Proof.

The
\widehat{{\mathcal{S}}}_{r}
arrow of (
102
) is immediate from Theorem
5.5.4
and Definition
5.5.1

(keeping in mind Remark
4.1.2
). The
{\boldsymbol{\pi}}_{r}
arrow then follows from Proposition
4.2.3
.
Statement (ii) follows similarly using Theorem
5.6.4
in place of Theorem
5.5.4
.
∎

5.8.
Nonsymmetric chromatic polynomials and flagged unicellular LLT polynomials

Here we briefly mention a connection with related work, which will not be used elsewhere in the paper.
By
[
10
, Propositions 3.4–3.5]
, chromatic symmetric functions of incomparability graphs of natural unit interval orders
and unicellular LLT polynomials are related by the plethystic
transformation
f[{\mathbf{x}}]\mapsto f[{\mathbf{x}}/(1-t)]
.
We will now see that a similar relation holds between our
flagged row LLT polynomials
\mathsf{G}_{r}(\pi,\Sigma)({\mathbf{x}};t)
in the unicellular case (i.e.
\Sigma=\varnothing
)
and nonsymmetric versions of
chromatic symmetric functions introduced by Haglund-Wilson
[
18
]
and further studied by
Tewari-Wilson-Zhang
[
33
]
.

The functions
\boldsymbol{\chi}_{\mathbf{F},\pi}
from
[
33
]
, which depend on
d,r,N\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{+}
, flag bounds
\mathbf{F}=(0\leq F_{1}\leq\cdots\leq F_{r}\leq N)
, and
\pi\in{\mathbb{D}}_{r}(d)
, are readily written in the notation of this section:

(106)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\chi}_{\mathbf{F},\pi}=\sum_{T\text{ non-attacking}}t^{\inv(T)}{\mathbf{x}}^{T},

where the sum is just as in (
83
) except that now the letters of
T
must be
\leq N
and
the flagging condition
T(i)\leq i
for
i\in[r]
is replaced by the more general
T(i)\leq F_{i}
for
i\in[r]
;
\inv(T)
is also just as in (
83
),
as defined in §
5.2
.
Hence, in particular, by (
86
), these functions for flag bounds
(1,\dots,r)
are essentially the same as our signed flagged row LLT polynomials up to a power of
(1-t)
:

(107)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\chi}_{(1,\dots,r),\pi}=\chi^{\prime}_{r}(\pi)[{\mathbf{x}}_{N};t]=(1-t)^{r-d}\mathsf{G}^{\pm}_{r}(\pi,\varnothing)[{\mathbf{x}}_{N};t],

where we have used the notation
f[{\mathbf{x}}_{N};t]=f(x_{1},\dots,x_{N},0,\dots;t)
.

It was initially conjectured the
\boldsymbol{\chi}_{\mathbf{F},\pi}
were Demazure character positive but
this turned out to be false. Our computations support the conjecture that they are Demazure character positive in the case

\mathbf{F}=(1,\dots,r)
.
However, even supposing this is true,
this situation differs in an
important way from our atom positivity conjectures on unsigned flagged row LLT polynomials and related polynomials
(see §
8.5
):
we showed in §
5.7
that
\mathsf{G}_{r}(\pi,\Sigma)
Weyl symmetrizes to the corresponding symmetric LLT polynomial

\mathsf{G}_{0}(\mathsf{N}^{r}\pi,\Sigma)

and therefore atom positivity of
\mathsf{G}_{r}(\pi,\Sigma)[{\mathbf{x}}_{N};t]
implies the Schur positivity of
\mathsf{G}_{0}(\mathsf{N}^{r}\pi,\Sigma)[{\mathbf{x}}_{N};t]
via (
11
).
In contrast, the
\boldsymbol{\chi}_{\mathbf{F},\pi}

do
not have the property that they Weyl symmetrize to the corresponding
chromatic symmetric function
, i.e.,
\pi_{w_{0}}(\boldsymbol{\chi}_{\mathbf{F},\pi})
is typically not equal to
\boldsymbol{\chi}_{(N,\dots,N),\pi}
.
Therefore, Demazure character or atom positivity of
\boldsymbol{\chi}_{\mathbf{F},\pi}
does
not imply the Schur positivity of
\boldsymbol{\chi}_{(N,\dots,N),\pi}

via (
11
).

6.
The nonsymmetric compositional
(km,kn)
-shuffle theorem

Here we prove a nonsymmetric compositional
(km,kn)
-shuffle theorem.
Our approach is to first establish a signed version of it and to then apply the
r
-nonsymmetric plethsym map
{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{r}
.
The former is proven by interpreting the last step in Mellit’s proof of the (symmetric) compositional
(km,kn)
-shuffle theorem
in terms of the signed flagged row LLT polynomials developed in §
5
.

6.1.
Double Dyck path algebra endomorphisms

The algebraic side of the nonsymmetric compositional
(km,kn)
-shuffle theorem involves
ingredients from Mellit’s version.
In particular, it involves endomorphisms
N
and
S
of
{\mathbb{A}}_{t,q}
,
which are variants of well-known automorphisms of the DAHA.

Proposition 6.1.1

(
[
29
, Proposition 3.16]
)
.

There is a unique
{\mathbb{Q}}(q,t)
-algebra endomorphism

N\colon{\mathbb{A}}_{t,q}\to{\mathbb{A}}_{t,q}
determined by

(108)

\displaystyle e_{r}\mapsto e_{r},\ \ T_{i}\mapsto T_{i},\ \ d_{-}\mapsto d_{-},\ \ d_{+}^{*}\mapsto d_{+}^{*},\ \ d_{+}\mapsto-(qt)^{-1}z_{1}d_{+},

and a unique
{\mathbb{Q}}(q,t)
-algebra endomorphism

S\colon{\mathbb{A}}_{t,q}\to{\mathbb{A}}_{t,q}
determined by

(109)

\displaystyle e_{r}\mapsto e_{r},\ \ T_{i}\mapsto T_{i},\ \ d_{-}\mapsto d_{-},\ \ d_{+}\mapsto d_{+},\ \ d_{+}^{*}\mapsto-y_{1}d_{+}^{*}.

Define the following elements of
\SL_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})

(110)

\displaystyle N^{\prime}=\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\
0&1\end{pmatrix},\quad S^{\prime}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
1&1\end{pmatrix}.

Proposition-Definition 6.1.2

(
[
29
, Theorem 3.19]
)
.

Let
m\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{+}
,
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}
with
\gcd(m,n)=1
.
Let
A^{\prime}
be a word in the alphabet
\{N^{\prime},S^{\prime}\}
such that the product of these matrices has the form

\begin{pmatrix}m&*\\
n&*\end{pmatrix}
and let

A
be the corresponding composition of the endomorphisms
N,S
.
Such a word exists and can be constructed using Farey sequences (see
[
29
, Remark 3.20]
).
Let
\rho^{*}_{m,n}\colon{\mathbb{A}}_{t}\to{\mathbb{A}}_{t,q}
be the composition
A\circ\rho^{*}
, where

\rho^{*}\colon{\mathbb{A}}_{t}\to{\mathbb{A}}_{t,q}
is as in Remark
3.1.3
.
This is independent of the choice of word
A^{\prime}
.

Example 6.1.3
.

For
(m,n)=(1,1)
, we could choose
A^{\prime}=S^{\prime}N^{\prime k}
for any
k\geq 0
and

(111)

\displaystyle\rho^{*}_{1,1}=S\circ\rho^{*}.

As another example, for any
a,b\geq 0
,

(112)

\displaystyle\rho^{*}_{ab+1,b}=N^{a}S^{b}\circ\rho^{*}.

6.2.
Signed, nonsymmetric compositional
(km,kn)
-shuffle theorem

Let
m,n
be relatively prime positive integers and

\alpha=(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{r})\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{+}^{r}
be a strict composition of size
k
.
Let
s=\frac{n}{m}
and
s_{-}=s-\epsilon
for small
\epsilon>0
.
Set

(113)

\displaystyle\mathsf{C}_{m,n}^{\alpha}\overset{\text{{def}}}{=}(-1)^{k(m+n+1)}t^{r-k}{\mathsf{P}}_{r}^{-1}\rho^{*}_{m,n}\big(y_{1}^{\alpha_{1}-1}\cdots y_{r}^{\alpha_{r}-1}d_{+}^{r}\big)\cdot 1\ \in\mathcal{P}(r).

For comparison, the expression
(-1)^{kn}d_{-}^{r}{\mathsf{P}}_{r}\mathsf{C}_{m,n}^{\alpha}\in V_{0}=\Lambda(W)

is the form of the algebraic side of the compositional
(km,kn)
-shuffle theorem that Mellit uses in his proof (equivalent to the original by
[
29
, §3.8]
).

The signed, nonsymmetric compositional
(km,kn)
-shuffle theorem we prove below
expresses (
113
) as a sum of signed flagged row LLT polynomials.
To describe the combinatorial side, we need to expand on the notation on Dyck paths from §
5.1
.

Let
{\mathbb{D}}^{\alpha}_{km,kn}
denote the set of
(km,kn)
-Dyck paths which touch the diagonal
exactly at the points

(k_{j}m,k_{j}n)
, where
k_{j}=\sum_{i<j}\alpha_{i}
for
j=1,\ldots,r+1
.

For any
\pi\in{\mathbb{D}}^{\alpha}_{km,kn}
, the
r
north steps of
\pi
touching the diagonal are the first
r
north steps in sweeping reading order and are mutually attacking. This means that the associated attacking Dyck path
\pi^{\prime}

begins with
r
north steps, and we let
\hat{\pi}^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{D}}_{r}(kn)
denote the partial Dyck path obtained by removing these steps.
Define a distinguished marking of
\pi^{\prime}
, denoted

\Sigma_{\pi}
, to be the set of pairs of consecutive north steps along vertical runs of
\pi
.
Since the corners of
\hat{\pi}^{\prime}
are the same as
those of
\pi^{\prime}
, the marking

\Sigma_{\pi}
of
\pi^{\prime}
may also be regarded
as a marking of
\hat{\pi}^{\prime}
.

Following
[
29
]
,
for
\pi\in{\mathbb{D}}^{\alpha}_{km,kn}
,
define
\dinv(\pi)
to be the number of pairs
(e,f)
where
e
is an east step of
\pi

and
f
is a north step of
\pi
, such that
e
is to the left of
f
and there is a
line of slope
s_{-}
which intersects
e
and
f
.
Also define

(114)

\displaystyle\maxtdinv(\pi)\ =\ \big(\text{\# of attacking pairs of north steps of $\pi$}\big)\ =\ \area(\pi^{\prime}).

Theorem 6.2.1
.

For relatively prime positive integers
m,n
and
a strict composition
\alpha=(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{r})
of size
k
,

(115)

\displaystyle\mathsf{C}_{m,n}^{\alpha}=\sum_{\pi\in{\mathbb{D}}^{\alpha}_{km,kn}}q^{\area(\pi)}t^{\dinv(\pi)-\maxtdinv(\pi)}\mathsf{G}^{\pm}_{r}(\hat{\pi}^{\prime},\Sigma_{\pi})\,,

where
\area(\pi)
and
\mathsf{G}^{\pm}_{r}(\hat{\pi}^{\prime},\Sigma_{\pi})
are defined in §
5.1
and §
5.2
.

Proof.

For
\pi\in{\mathbb{D}}^{\alpha}_{km,kn}
, define

\chi_{r}(\hat{\pi}^{\prime},\Sigma_{\pi})\in V_{r}
just as
\chi^{\mathsf{P}}_{r}(\hat{\pi}^{\prime},\Sigma_{\pi})
in
Definition
5.5.1
but with
d_{+},d_{-}
in place of
-d_{+}^{\mathsf{P}},d_{-}^{\mathsf{P}}
.
Thus, by Theorem
5.5.4
,

(116)

\mathsf{G}^{\pm}_{r}(\hat{\pi}^{\prime},\Sigma_{\pi})=\chi^{\mathsf{P}}_{r}(\hat{\pi}^{\prime},\Sigma_{\pi})=(-1)^{kn}{\mathsf{P}}_{r}^{-1}\chi_{r}(\hat{\pi}^{\prime},\Sigma_{\pi}).

Hence (
115
)
is equivalent to the following identity of
[
29
]
:

(117)

(-1)^{k(m+1)}\,t^{r-k}\,\rho^{*}_{m,n}\big(y_{1}^{\alpha_{1}-1}\cdots y_{r}^{\alpha_{r}-1}d_{+}^{r}\big)\cdot 1\\
=\sum_{\pi\in{\mathbb{D}}^{\alpha}_{km,kn}}q^{\area(\pi)}t^{\dinv(\pi)-\maxtdinv(\pi)}\chi_{r}(\hat{\pi}^{\prime},\Sigma_{\pi}).

To explain how this follows from
[
29
]
, we first give an overview of Mellit’s proof of the
(km,kn)
-compositional shuffle conjecture, and then
focus on the specific details needed for (
117
).

For
h\in{\mathbb{R}}
, let
\ell_{h}
be the line

\{(x,y):y=s_{-}x+h\}
of slope
s_{-}
.
Following
[
29
, §4.1]
, for
\pi\in{\mathbb{D}}_{km,kn}^{\alpha}
and
h\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
set
D_{\pi}^{h}=O_{p_{j}}\cdots O_{p_{1}}\cdot 1
for certain operators
O_{p_{i}}\in{\mathbb{A}}_{t,q}
indexed by the lattice points
p_{1},\dots,p_{j}
weakly below

\pi
and above
\ell_{h}
,
ordered by their distance to
\ell_{-\epsilon}
.
We do not need the full description of the
O_{p_{i}}
, but to get a
flavor, a few examples of
D_{\pi}^{h}
are given below with
\ell_{h}

depicted as a dashed line.
\cdot
\cdot
\cdot
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{\pi}^{3}=1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{\pi}^{1.3}=t^{4}d_{-}d_{+}^{4}(1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{\pi}^{-\epsilon}=q^{6}t^{3}d_{-}^{2}\frac{[d_{-},d_{+}]}{q-1}d_{-}\frac{[d_{-},d_{+}]}{q-1}d_{-}d_{+}^{4}(1)}

In general,
O_{p_{i}}
is a scalar multiple of
id
,
d_{+}
,
d_{-}
, or
[d_{-},d_{+}]
, determined by the relative position of
\pi
and
p_{i}
.

An
admissible coloring of
\ell_{h}
is a subset of line segments of
\ell_{h}
, each of which has its left endpoint on a vertical lattice line and its right endpoint on a horizontal lattice line
in the rectangle
[0,km]\times[0,kn]
.
As in
[
29
, pg. 27]
, define, for an admissible coloring
c
of
\ell_{h}
,

(118)

\displaystyle D_{s,c}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{$(km,kn)$-Dyck paths $\pi$}\\
(\text{region below }\pi)\cap\ell_{h}=c.\end{subarray}}D^{h}_{\pi}.

The core of Mellit’s proof is to inductively establish an
identity equating the sum
D_{s,c}
with a single product of the operators
T_{i},y_{i},z_{i},d_{+}
applied to 1, determined from the lattice lines intersecting
c
.

Now to specifically address (
117
),
consider
h_{1}=kn-kms_{-}+\epsilon
so that
\ell_{h_{1}}

intersects
(km,kn+\epsilon)
and let
c_{\alpha}
be the admissible coloring of
\ell_{h_{1}}
given by the union of
r
line segments such that the
j
-th segment
has left endpoint with
x
-coordinate
m\sum_{i<j}\alpha_{i}
and
right endpoint with
y
-coordinate
n\sum_{i\leq j}\alpha_{i}
.
For example, for
n=m=1
and
\alpha=(2,2,1)
,
c_{\alpha}
consists of the three line segments below.

For the admissible coloring
c=c_{\alpha}
, the abovementioned identity for
D_{s,c_{\alpha}}
is the second to last displayed equation in
[
29
]
, which states

(119)

D_{s,c_{\alpha}}=(-1)^{k(m+1)}\,(qt)^{r-k}\,\rho^{*}_{m,n}\big(y_{1}^{\alpha_{1}-1}\cdots y_{r}^{\alpha_{r}-1}d_{+}^{r}\big)\cdot 1.

Note that for a
(km,kn)
-Dyck path
\pi
, we have
(\text{region below }\pi)\cap\ell_{h_{1}}=c_{\alpha}
if and only if
\pi\in{\mathbb{D}}_{km,kn}^{\alpha}
,
so
D_{s,c_{\alpha}}=\sum_{\pi\in{\mathbb{D}}_{km,kn}^{\alpha}}D^{h_{1}}_{\pi}
.
To connect this to the right hand side of (
117
), it is shown in
[
29
, §4.1]
that

(120)

\displaystyle D^{-\epsilon}_{\pi}=q^{\area(\pi)}t^{\dinv(\pi)-\maxtdinv(\pi)}d_{-}^{r}\,\chi_{r}(\hat{\pi}^{\prime},\Sigma_{\pi}),

which is close to what we want. What we actually want is

(121)

D^{h_{1}}_{\pi}=q^{\area(\pi)+r-k}t^{\dinv(\pi)-\maxtdinv(\pi)}\chi_{r}(\hat{\pi}^{\prime},\Sigma_{\pi}),

which can be deduced from the proof of (
120
).
To see this, observe that the operator sequence to go from
D^{h_{1}}_{\pi}
to
D^{-\epsilon}_{\pi}
corresponds to
the lattice points between the
lines
\ell_{h_{1}}
and
\ell_{-\epsilon}
, which are the
k+1

lattice points on the diagonal.
According to
[
29
, §4.1]
(see also
[
29
, Remark 4.6]
),
each non-touchpoint
lattice point registers a
q
factor, while each of the
r+1

touchpoint lattice points registers a
d_{-}
, except the top-right one, which registers a factor of
t^{0}
.

We conclude from (
121
) that the right hand side of (
117
) is equal to

q^{k-r}D_{s,c_{\alpha}}
. Combining this with (
119
) proves (
117
).
∎

Remark 6.2.2
.

Our notation
d_{-}^{r}\chi_{r}(\hat{\pi}^{\prime},\Sigma_{\pi})
in the proof above is equal to

\chi(\pi^{\prime},\Sigma_{\pi})
from
[
29
, §§3.5, 4.1]
,
which was also previously mentioned in Remark
5.7.1
(iii).

6.3.
The nonsymmetric compositional shuffle theorem

The virtue of a signed, compositional shuffle theorem, expressed as a sum of signed flagged LLT polynomials, is that its image under the

r
-nonsymmetric plethysm map
{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{r}
yields a positive, combinatorial sum of flagged LLT polynomials.
Precisely, applying

{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{r}

to Theorem
6.2.1
and using Theorem
5.4.1
yields the following combinatorial expression for
{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{r}\,\mathsf{C}_{m,n}^{\alpha}

which is manifestly monomial positive.

Theorem 6.3.1
.

With the notation from the beginning of
§
6.2
and in §§
5.1
–
5.2
,

(122)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{r}\,\mathsf{C}_{m,n}^{\alpha}=\sum_{\pi\in{\mathbb{D}}^{\alpha}_{km,kn}}q^{\area(\pi)}t^{\dinv(\pi)-\maxtdinv(\pi)}\,\mathsf{G}_{r}(\hat{\pi}^{\prime},\Sigma_{\pi}).

Theorem
6.3.1
can also be written in a form closer to the classical statement of the shuffle theorem.
Define a
flagged weak word parking function

w
on
\pi\in{\mathbb{D}}^{\alpha}_{km,kn}

to be a map from the north steps of
\pi
to
{\mathbb{Z}}_{+}
that is weakly decreasing going north along vertical runs and such that the entry on the north step from the point

(m\sum_{i<j}\alpha_{i},n\sum_{i<j}\alpha_{i})
is
\leq j
, for
j=1,\dots,r
.
Denote the set of these by
\FWPF^{\prime}_{\alpha}(\pi)
.

For
w\in\FWPF^{\prime}_{\alpha}(\pi)
, define the
weak temporary diagonal inversion statistic
by

(123)

\displaystyle\rm{tdinv}^{\prime}(\pi,w)=\big|\big\{\text{pairs of north steps $i$, $j$ of $\pi$}:\text{ $i$ attacks $j$ and $w(i)\geq w(j)$}\big\}\big|.

Corollary 6.3.2
.

With the notation above,

(124)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{r}\,\mathsf{C}_{m,n}^{\alpha}=\sum_{\pi\in{\mathbb{D}}^{\alpha}_{km,kn}}\sum_{w\in\FWPF^{\prime}_{\alpha}(\pi)}q^{\area(\pi)}t^{\dinv(\pi)-\maxtdinv(\pi)+\rm{tdinv}^{\prime}(\pi,w)}\,{\mathbf{x}}^{\content(w)},

where
{\mathbf{x}}^{\content(w)}=\prod_{i\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{+}}x_{i}^{\#\text{ of $i$'s in $w$}}
.

Remark 6.3.3
.

Two other variants of the
\rm{tdinv}^{\prime}
statistic appear in the literature and elsewhere in this paper.
Replacing
w(i)\geq w(j)
with
w(i)>w(j)
in (
123
) yields the temporary dinv statistic
\tdinv(\pi,w)
used in
[
4
,
29
]
.
Replacing
w(i)\geq w(j)
with
w(i)<w(j)
yields the dinv statistic
\dinv(\pi,w)
used in

[
16
]
and in Theorem
1.1.1
and (
192
) of this paper.

As touched on in the introduction and as will be further discussed in §
8.5
, two advantages to working on the modified side are that
the nonsymmetric shuffle theorems have a conjectural positive combinatorial interpretation,
and that the ordinary shuffle theorem is recovered simply by Weyl symmetrizing.

To see this symmetrization property,
first note that

(125)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{0}{\mathsf{P}}_{0}^{-1}(f[W])=f[-W]=(-1)^{d}\omega f[W],

for any symmetric function
f
of degree
d
.

By (
103
) and (
101
),

{\boldsymbol{\pi}}_{1}\cdots{\boldsymbol{\pi}}_{r}\mathsf{G}_{r}(\hat{\pi}^{\prime},\Sigma_{\pi})=\omega\hskip 0.85358pt\chi(\pi^{\prime},\Sigma_{\pi})
, where

\chi(\pi^{\prime},\Sigma_{\pi})
is the LLT polynomial with the notation of
[
29
, §3.5]
,
discussed in Remark
5.7.1
(iii).
Hence, applying
{\boldsymbol{\pi}}_{1}\cdots{\boldsymbol{\pi}}_{r}
to both sides of
(
122
) yields the first equality below

(126)

\sum_{\pi\in{\mathbb{D}}^{\alpha}_{km,kn}}q^{\area(\pi)}t^{\dinv(\pi)-\maxtdinv(\pi)}\,\omega\,\chi(\pi^{\prime},\Sigma_{\pi})={\boldsymbol{\pi}}_{1}\cdots{\boldsymbol{\pi}}_{r}\,{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{r}\,\mathsf{C}_{m,n}^{\alpha}\\
={\mathsf{\Pi}}_{0}\,\widehat{{\mathcal{S}}}_{1}\cdots\widehat{{\mathcal{S}}}_{r}\,\mathsf{C}_{m,n}^{\alpha}={\mathsf{\Pi}}_{0}{\mathsf{P}}_{0}^{-1}d_{-}^{r}{\mathsf{P}}_{r}\mathsf{C}_{m,n}^{\alpha}=\omega(-1)^{k(m+1)}t^{r-k}\,d_{-}^{r}\,\rho^{*}_{m,n}\big(y_{1}^{\alpha_{1}-1}\cdots y_{r}^{\alpha_{r}-1}d_{+}^{r}\big)\cdot 1;

the second equality is by Proposition
4.2.3
, the third is by
Proposition
4.1.1
, and the last is by (
125
).
This is exactly
\omega
of Mellit’s statement of the compositional
(km,kn)
-shuffle theorem.

7.
Modified nonsymmetric Macdonald polynomials

The (compositional) shuffle theorems involve the

\nabla
operator, which acts diagonally on modified Macdonald polynomials.
To prove our nonsymmetric version of this result (Theorem
1.1.1
), we need some
background on stable and modified nonsymmetric Macdonald polynomials
[
2
,
7
]
.

7.1.
Stable nonsymmetric Macdonald polynomials

We follow the conventions of
[
7
]
for nonsymmetric Macdonald polynomials
E_{\beta}
and their integral forms
{\mathcal{E}}_{\beta}
.
For
\beta\in{\mathbb{N}}^{N}
, let
E_{\beta}({\mathbf{x}}_{N};q,t)=E_{\beta}(x_{1},\dots,x_{N};q,t)

denote the monically normalized nonsymmetric Macdonald polynomial; it satisfies the
triangularity condition

E_{\beta}({\mathbf{x}}_{N};q,t)={\mathbf{x}}^{\beta}+\sum_{\alpha<\beta}c_{\alpha}(q,t){\mathbf{x}}^{\alpha},

for a certain order
<
on
{\mathbb{Z}}^{N}
defined via Bruhat order on the extended affine Weyl group
(see, e.g.,
[
7
, §5.4]
or
[
14
, §2.1]
).
Knop’s integral form nonsymmetric Macdonald polynomials
[
24
]
are then given by

(127)

{\mathcal{E}}_{\beta}({\mathbf{x}}_{N};q,t)=\bigl(\prod\nolimits_{\,u\in\dg(\beta)}(1-q^{a(u)+1}\,t^{l(u)+1})\bigr)\,E_{\beta}({\mathbf{x}}_{N};q,t),

where
\dg(\beta)=\big\{(i,j)\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{+}^{2}:1\leq j\leq N,\,-\beta_{N+1-j}+1\leq i\leq 0\big\}

and the arm
a(u)
and leg
l(u)
are nonnegative integer statistics on the boxes of
\dg(\beta)
(see, e.g.,
[
7
, §7.1]
).

Remark 7.1.1
.

The
E_{\beta}
here agree with
[
7
,
14
,
20
]
, and in terms of the notation here, the nonsymmetric Macdonald polynomials
in
[
2
]
are
E_{\beta}({\mathbf{x}}_{N};q^{-1},t)
and those in
[
24
]
are
E_{\beta_{N},\dots,\beta_{1}}(x_{N},\dots,x_{1};q,t)
.
The
{\mathcal{E}}_{\beta}

here agree with
[
7
,
14
]
, and in terms of the notation here, those in
[
24
]
are
{\mathcal{E}}_{\beta_{N},\dots,\beta_{1}}(x_{N},\dots,x_{1};q,t)
.

Denote a variant of
{\mathcal{E}}_{\beta}
by

(128)

\displaystyle\check{\mathcal{E}}_{\beta}({\mathbf{x}}_{N};q,t)=q^{\mathsf{n}((\beta_{+})^{*})}{\mathcal{E}}_{\beta}({\mathbf{x}}_{N};q^{-1},t),

where
\beta_{+}
is the partition rearrangement of
\beta
,
(\beta_{+})^{*}
denotes its conjugate partition,
and
\mathsf{n}(\mu)=\sum_{i}(i-1)\mu_{i}.

Below we will freely use notation on convergence and Weyl and Hecke symmetrization from
§
2.2
and §
4.1
.

Definition 7.1.2
.

For
(\eta|\lambda)\in{\mathbb{N}}^{r}\times\Par
,
the
integral form stable
r
-nonsymmetric Macdonald polynomial

\check{J}_{\eta|\lambda}({\mathbf{x}};q,t)\in\mathcal{P}(r)
is given by

(129)

\displaystyle\check{J}_{\eta|\lambda}({\mathbf{x}};q,t)=\lim_{N\to\infty}\widehat{{\mathcal{S}}}_{r+1}^{N}\,\check{\mathcal{E}}_{(\eta;\lambda;0^{N-r-\ell(\lambda)})}({\mathbf{x}}_{N};q,t),

where the sequence converges
t
-adically to a well-defined limit by

[
7
, Proposition 7.4.5]
.

Remark 7.1.3
.

(i) Bechtloff Weising introduced stable versions
\widetilde{E}_{(\eta|\lambda)}
of
E_{\beta}({\mathbf{x}}_{N};q^{-1},t)
and showed that
they have a description using partial Hecke symmetrization
[
2
, Corollary 4.26]
,
and we have closely followed this description for the definition above;
our versions are related by

(130)

\displaystyle\check{J}_{\eta|\lambda}=q^{\mathsf{n}(((\eta;\lambda)_{+})^{*})}\bigl(\prod\nolimits_{\,u}(1-q^{-a(u)-1}\,t^{l(u)+1})\bigr)\,\widetilde{E}_{(\eta|\lambda)}

where the product is over
u=(i,j)\in\dg((\eta;\lambda))
with
i<0
;
see
[
7
, Corollary 7.4.6 and Remark 7.4.9(ii)]
.
Below we will recall several results of
[
2
]
and will
state them in terms of the
\check{J}_{\eta|\lambda}
’s rather than
\widetilde{E}_{(\eta|\lambda)}
’s;
no adjustment is required as the results only depend on the functions in (
130
) up to a scalar factor.

(ii) Up to a
q
-flip, the integral form version defined above was introduced in
[
7
]
;
more precisely,

J_{\eta|\lambda}(x_{1},\dots,x_{r},Y;q,t)
with
Y=x_{r+1},x_{r+2},\ldots
from
[
7
, §7.4]

is equal to
q^{\mathsf{n}(((\eta;\lambda)_{+})^{*})}\check{J}_{\eta|\lambda}({\mathbf{x}};q^{-1},t)
here.
It was established in
[
7
]
that
J_{\eta|\lambda}(x_{1},\dots,x_{r},Y;q,t)
has coefficients in
{\mathbb{Z}}[q,t]
;
this implies that the same holds for the
\check{J}_{\eta|\lambda}({\mathbf{x}};q,t)
because the highest
q
power of
J_{\eta|\lambda}
is

q^{\mathsf{n}(((\eta;\lambda)_{+})^{*})}
,
as follows from our formula for
J_{\eta|\lambda}
in terms of signed flagged LLT polynomials
[
7
, Proposition 7.5.2]
.

(iii) Although
{\mathcal{E}}_{\beta}({\mathbf{x}}_{N};q,t)\in{\mathbb{Z}}[q,t][{\mathbf{x}}_{N}]
by
[
24
]
, this is typically not true for the
\check{\mathcal{E}}_{\beta}({\mathbf{x}};q,t)

since the highest
q
power of
{\mathcal{E}}_{\beta}({\mathbf{x}}_{N};q,t)
is typically larger than
q^{\mathsf{n}((\beta_{+})^{*})}
.

While
{\mathbb{N}}^{r}\times\Par
is the natural index set for a basis of
\mathcal{P}(r)
, we also want an index set for a basis of
\mathcal{P}_{\rm as}
.
Accordingly, we define

\pairs=\bigsqcup_{r\geq 0}\,\big\{(\eta|\lambda)\in{\mathbb{N}}^{r}\times\Par\hskip 0.85358pt:\hskip 0.85358pt\eta_{r}>0\big\}\,,

where, by convention, when
r=0
, there is the single possible
\eta=\varnothing
.

The
{\mathcal{E}}_{\alpha}
(
\alpha\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}
)
are joint eigenfunctions of the Cherednik operators
Y_{1},\dots,Y_{n}\in\mathcal{H}_{n}
.
The following result of Bechtloff Weising establishes the analogous result
in the stable limit.

Theorem 7.1.4

(
[
2
, Corollary 4.15]
)
.

For
(\eta|\lambda)\in{\mathbb{N}}^{r}\times\Par
, and any
j\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{+}
,

(131)

\displaystyle Y_{j}\check{J}_{\eta|\lambda}=\begin{cases}q^{\eta_{j}}\hskip 0.85358ptt^{r+\ell(\lambda)+1-\st((\eta;\lambda))_{j}}\,\check{J}_{\eta|\lambda}&\text{$j\leq r$ and $\eta_{j}>0$},\\
0&\text{otherwise},\end{cases}

where

\st((\eta;\lambda))
is the word obtained from
(\eta;\lambda)
by relabeling its smallest letter with
1,\dots,k

from left to right, the next smallest by
k+1,\dots
, and so on.

The
\check{J}_{\eta|\lambda}
are compatible for different
r
in the sense that

(132)

\displaystyle\check{J}_{\eta|\lambda}=\check{J}_{(\eta,0)|\lambda}.

This will be proved in the next subsection.

Theorem 7.1.5

(
[
2
, Theorem 4.30]
)
.

The set
\big\{\check{J}_{\eta|\lambda}:(\eta|\lambda)\in\pairs\big\}

forms an eigenbasis of
\mathcal{P}_{\rm as}
for the commuting operators
Y_{i}
.
For each
r\geq 0
, the set
\big\{\check{J}_{\eta|\lambda}:(\eta|\lambda)\in{\mathbb{N}}^{r}\times\Par\big\}
forms an eigenbasis of the subspace
\mathcal{P}(r)
.

Proposition 7.1.6

(
[
7
, Proposition 7.8.6]
)
.

Partial Hecke symmetrization acts in the following simple way on integral form stable
r
-nonsymmetric Macdonald polynomials.
For any
(\eta|\lambda)\in{\mathbb{N}}^{r}\times\Par
,

(133)

\displaystyle\widehat{{\mathcal{S}}}_{r}\,\check{J}_{\eta|\lambda}=\check{J}_{(\eta_{1},\dots,\eta_{r-1})|(\eta_{r},\lambda)_{+}}.

Full Hecke symmetrization yields

(134)

\displaystyle\widehat{{\mathcal{S}}}_{1}\cdots\widehat{{\mathcal{S}}}_{r}\,\check{J}_{\eta|\lambda}({\mathbf{x}};q,t)=\check{J}_{\varnothing|(\eta;\lambda)_{+}}({\mathbf{x}};q,t)=q^{\mathsf{n}(((\eta;\lambda)_{+})^{*})}J_{(\eta;\lambda)_{+}}({\mathbf{x}};q^{-1},t),

where
J_{\mu}({\mathbf{x}};q,t)
is the integral form symmetric Macdonald polynomial.

Bechtloff Weising
uses Theorem
7.1.4
to deduce an
explicit formula for
T_{i}\check{J}_{\eta|\lambda}
, for
i\in[r-1]
, in terms of
\check{J}_{\eta|\lambda}
and
\check{J}_{s_{i}(\eta)|\lambda}
,
an analog of the Knop-Sahi intertwiner formula for the
E_{\beta}
’s. For later use, we record the following result, which is mostly a consequence of this formula.

Proposition 7.1.7
.

For any
(\eta|\lambda)\in{\mathbb{N}}^{r}\times\Par
and
i\in[r-1]
,

(135)

\displaystyle T_{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\check{J}_{\eta|\lambda}\in\spn_{{\mathbb{Q}}(q,t)}\{\check{J}_{\eta|\lambda},\check{J}_{s_{i}(\eta)|\lambda}\}.

Proof.

For
\eta_{i}\neq\eta_{i+1}
, the result follows from the formula of Bechtloff Weising mentioned just above.
Now suppose
\eta_{i}=\eta_{i+1}
.
Then
\check{\mathcal{E}}_{(\eta;\lambda;0^{N-r-\ell(\lambda)})}({\mathbf{x}}_{N};q,t)
is symmetric in
x_{i},x_{i+1}
(see
[
27
, eq. (5.4.4)]
),
and so

T_{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\check{\mathcal{E}}_{(\eta;\lambda;0^{N-r-\ell(\lambda)})}=\check{\mathcal{E}}_{(\eta;\lambda;0^{N-r-\ell(\lambda)})}
.
We deduce
T_{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\check{J}_{\eta|\lambda}=\check{J}_{\eta|\lambda}
,
as follows from Definition
7.1.2
, the commutation relation
T_{i}\widehat{{\mathcal{S}}}_{r+1}^{N}=\widehat{{\mathcal{S}}}_{r+1}^{N}T_{i}
for
i<r
, and the fact that
T_{i}\hskip 0.85358pt({\mathbb{Q}}(q,t)[{\mathbf{x}}_{N}])_{e}\subseteq({\mathbb{Q}}(q,t)[{\mathbf{x}}_{N}])_{e}
.
∎

7.2.
Modified nonsymmetric Macdonald polynomials

Definition 7.2.1

(
[
7
, §7.5]
)
.

For
(\eta|\lambda)\in{\mathbb{N}}^{r}\times\Par
, define the
modified
r
-nonsymmetric Macdonald polynomial
,
in terms of the
r
-nonsymmetric plethysm map
{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{r}
from (
6
), by

(136)

\displaystyle\check{\mathsf{H}}_{\eta|\lambda}={\mathsf{\Pi}}_{r}\,\check{J}_{\eta|\lambda}.

Proposition 7.2.2
.

The map
{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{r}
and the elements
\check{J}_{\eta|\lambda}
and
\check{\mathsf{H}}_{\eta|\lambda}

are compatible for different
r
in the following sense:

(137)

the restriction of
{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{r}
to
\mathcal{P}(r-1)\subseteq\mathcal{P}(r)
agrees with
{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{r-1}
,

(138)

\displaystyle\check{J}_{\eta|\lambda}=\check{J}_{(\eta,0)|\lambda},

(139)

\displaystyle\check{\mathsf{H}}_{\eta|\lambda}=\check{\mathsf{H}}_{(\eta,0)|\lambda}.

Proof.

By
[
7
, Remark 4.1.2 (i) and Proposition 4.3.5]
,
if
g({\mathbf{x}}_{r})\in{\mathbb{Q}}(q,t)[{\mathbf{x}}_{r}]
is independent of
x_{r}
,
then

\pol_{{\mathbf{x}}_{r}}\!\!\big(\frac{g({\mathbf{x}}_{r})}{\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq r}(1-t\hskip 0.85358ptx_{i}/x_{j})}\big)=\pol_{{\mathbf{x}}_{r-1}}\!\!\big(\frac{g({\mathbf{x}}_{r})}{\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq r-1}(1-t\hskip 0.85358ptx_{i}/x_{j})}\big)
,
where
\pol_{{\mathbf{x}}_{N}}
is the polynomial part operator from (
4
)
taken with respect to the basis
\{{\mathcal{D}}_{\alpha}:\alpha\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{N}\}
of
{\mathbb{Q}}(q,t)[x_{1}^{\pm 1},\cdots,x_{N}^{\pm 1}]
.
The identity in (
137
) then follows by computing
{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{r}
on the basis

\big\{x_{1}^{\eta_{1}}\cdots x_{r}^{\eta_{r}}s_{\lambda}({\mathbf{x}}):(\eta|\lambda)\in{\mathbb{N}}^{r}\times\Par\big\}
of
\mathcal{P}(r)
, which has the convenient property that
the subset with
\eta_{r}=0
is a basis for
\mathcal{P}(r-1)
.

The identity (
139
)
follows from the formula for
\check{\mathsf{H}}_{\eta|\lambda}
as a sum of flagged LLT polynomials
[
7
, Theorem 7.6.6 (b)]
and

[
7
, Remark 7.6.8]
which gives an explicit combinatorial formula for these flagged LLT terms. Adding the extra 0 to go from
\eta|\lambda
to

(\eta,0)|\lambda
adds an extra empty row to the tableaux in this formula, but the remark shows that this does not change the flagging conditions
or the
\inv
statistic.
Identity (
138
) then follows from (
137
) and (
139
).
∎

The
\check{\mathsf{H}}_{\eta|\lambda}
, as
(\eta|\lambda)
ranges over
\pairs
, form a basis for
\mathcal{P}_{\rm as}
.
For each
r\geq 0
, the subset indexed by
{\mathbb{N}}^{r}\times\Par
forms a basis of
\mathcal{P}(r)
.

Proposition 7.2.3

(
[
7
, Proposition 7.8.3]
)
.

Weyl symmetrization acts in the following simple way on modified
r
-nonsymmetric Macdonald polynomials.
For any
(\eta|\lambda)\in{\mathbb{N}}^{r}\times\Par
,

(140)

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\pi}}_{r}\,\check{\mathsf{H}}_{\eta|\lambda}=\check{\mathsf{H}}_{(\eta_{1},\dots,\eta_{r-1})|(\eta_{r},\lambda)_{+}}.

Full Weyl symmetrization yields

(141)

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\pi}}_{1}\cdots{\boldsymbol{\pi}}_{r}\hskip 0.85358pt\check{\mathsf{H}}_{\eta|\lambda}({\mathbf{x}};q,t)=\check{\mathsf{H}}_{\varnothing|(\eta;\lambda)_{+}}({\mathbf{x}};q,t)=\omega\tilde{H}_{(\eta;\lambda)_{+}}({\mathbf{x}};q,t).

Propositions
7.1.6
and
7.2.3
are nicely summarized by the following commutative diagram,
which holds for any
(\eta|\lambda)\in{\mathbb{N}}^{r}\times\Par
and
\mu=(\eta;\lambda)_{+}
.

(142)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{\mathsf{n}(\mu^{*})}J_{\mu}({\mathbf{x}};q^{-1},t)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega\tilde{H}_{\mu}({\mathbf{x}};q,t)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\check{J}_{\eta|\lambda}({\mathbf{x}};q,t)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\check{\mathsf{H}}_{\eta|\lambda}({\mathbf{x}};q,t)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f[{\mathbf{x}}]\mapsto f[{\mathbf{x}}/(1-t)]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{{\mathcal{S}}}_{1}\cdots\widehat{{\mathcal{S}}}_{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{r}}

\scriptstyle{\boldsymbol{\pi}}_{1}\cdots{\boldsymbol{\pi}}_{r}

8.
Nonsymmetric
\nabla

formulation of the nonsymmetric shuffle theorem

The operator
\nabla
, introduced in
[
3
]
, is the linear
operator on symmetric functions which acts diagonally on the basis of
modified Macdonald polynomials
\tilde{H}_{\mu}({\mathbf{x}};q,t)
by

\nabla\tilde{H}_{\mu}=q^{\mathsf{n}(\mu^{*})}t^{\mathsf{n}(\mu)}\tilde{H}_{\mu}
.
We now introduce two nonsymmetric versions of
\nabla
: an operator
\bar{\nabla}
which acts diagonally on the
\check{J}_{\eta|\lambda}
’s and
\boldsymbol{\nabla}
which acts diagonally on the
\check{\mathsf{H}}_{\eta|\lambda}
’s. Then, in Theorem
8.3.1
, we show that the
{\mathsf{P}}_{r}
conjugate of
\bar{\nabla}
is equal, up to a sign, to the operator on
V_{*}
induced by the endomorphism
N
of
\mathbb{A}_{t,q}
. From there, we deduce the nonsymmetric compositional shuffle theorem stated in terms of
\boldsymbol{\nabla}
(Theorem
1.1.1
).

8.1.
Nonsymmetric
\nabla
defined via nonsymmetric Macdonald polynomials

Define the
signed nonsymmetric nabla operator

\bar{\nabla}\colon\mathcal{P}_{\rm as}\to\mathcal{P}_{\rm as}
to be the
{\mathbb{Q}}(q,t)
-linear map
determined by

(143)

\displaystyle\bar{\nabla}\check{J}_{\eta|\lambda}({\mathbf{x}};q,t)=q^{\mathsf{n}(\mu^{*})}t^{\mathsf{n}(\mu)}\check{J}_{\eta|\lambda}({\mathbf{x}};q,t),\qquad\text{where $\mu=(\eta;\lambda)_{+}$},

as
(\eta|\lambda)
ranges over
\pairs
.
Note that by (
138
), (
143
) then holds for any
(\eta|\lambda)\in{\mathbb{N}}^{r}\times\Par
for any
r\geq 0
.

Recall that in (
3
),
we defined the
nonsymmetric nabla operator

\boldsymbol{\nabla}\colon\mathcal{P}_{\rm as}\to\mathcal{P}_{\rm as}
to be the
{\mathbb{Q}}(q,t)
-linear map
determined by

(144)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\nabla}\check{\mathsf{H}}_{\eta|\lambda}({\mathbf{x}};q,t)=q^{\mathsf{n}(\mu^{*})}t^{\mathsf{n}(\mu)}\check{\mathsf{H}}_{\eta|\lambda}({\mathbf{x}};q,t),\qquad\text{where $\mu=(\eta;\lambda)_{+}$},

as
(\eta|\lambda)
ranges over
\pairs
.
Note that by (
139
), (
144
) then holds for any
(\eta|\lambda)\in{\mathbb{N}}^{r}\times\Par
for any
r\geq 0
.

Note that since the set
\{\check{J}_{\eta|\lambda}:(\eta|\lambda)\in{\mathbb{N}}^{r}\times\Par\}

spans
\mathcal{P}(r)
,
\bar{\nabla}
fixes the subspace
\mathcal{P}(r)\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{\rm as}
.
The same goes for
\boldsymbol{\nabla}
.

The operator
\boldsymbol{\nabla}
is a nonsymmetric version of
\omega\nabla\omega
in the sense that

(145)

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\pi}}_{1}\cdots{\boldsymbol{\pi}}_{r}\hskip 0.85358pt\boldsymbol{\nabla}\hskip 0.85358ptf=\omega\hskip 0.85358pt\nabla\hskip 0.85358pt\omega\hskip 0.85358pt{\boldsymbol{\pi}}_{1}\cdots{\boldsymbol{\pi}}_{r}\hskip 0.85358ptf

for any
f\in\mathcal{P}(r)
.
This holds by Proposition
7.2.3
.
This implies that
\boldsymbol{\nabla}
restricted to
the space of symmetric functions agrees with
\omega\nabla\omega
, but is also a significantly stronger notion.

Also note that by Definition
7.2.1
,

(146)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{r}\bar{\nabla}{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{r}^{-1}=\boldsymbol{\nabla}\quad\text{as operators on $\mathcal{P}(r)$}.

8.2.
An operator identity for
\bar{\nabla}

The goal of this subsection is to prove the following operator identity, which will allow us to relate
\bar{\nabla}
to the endomorphism
N
of
{\mathbb{A}}_{t,q}
.

Lemma 8.2.1
.

We have the following identity of operators on
\mathcal{P}_{\rm as}
:

(147)

\displaystyle\bar{\nabla}X_{1}=(qt)^{-1}Y_{1}X_{1}\bar{\nabla}.

To prove this, we need to analyze which
\check{J}_{\gamma|\mu}
’s appear in the expansion of

x_{1}\check{J}_{\eta|\lambda}
.
For this, we use a ‘Monk rule’ for nonsymmetric Macdonald polynomials,
which is an explicit formula for
x_{j}\hskip 0.85358ptE_{\alpha}
in the basis
\{E_{\beta}:\beta\in{\mathbb{N}}^{N}\}
.
We use the version given by Halverson-Ram
[
20
]
(see also
[
1
,
25
]
).
Note that
their rule is expressed in terms of the
E_{\beta}({\mathbf{x}}_{N};q,t)
’s rather than the
\check{\mathcal{E}}_{\beta}({\mathbf{x}}_{N};q,t)
’s,
but we do not need to make an adjustment for this since we only care about which coefficients are nonzero.

Proposition 8.2.2
.

For any
\alpha\in{\mathbb{N}}^{N}
,

(148)

\displaystyle x_{1}\hskip 0.85358pt\check{\mathcal{E}}_{\alpha}\in\spn_{{\mathbb{Q}}(q,t)}\big\{\check{\mathcal{E}}_{\beta}:(\beta_{1}-1,\beta_{2},\dots,\beta_{N})_{+}=\alpha_{+}\big\}.

Proof.

By
[
20
, Theorem 3.1 (a)]
,
the
\beta
for which
\check{\mathcal{E}}_{\beta}
appears with nonzero coefficient in
x_{j}\hskip 0.85358pt\check{\mathcal{E}}_{\alpha}
are of the form
\rot_{C}(\alpha)
for
j\in C
, where
\rot_{C}(\alpha)
is defined as follows:
let
C=\{i_{1}<\cdots<i_{k}\}\subseteq\{1,\dots,N\}
. Define

\rot_{C}(\alpha)
to be the result of replacing the subsequence
\alpha_{i_{1}},\dots,\alpha_{i_{k}}
of
\alpha
with
\alpha_{i_{k}}+1,\alpha_{i_{1}},\dots,\alpha_{i_{k-1}}
, and leaving the rest of
\alpha
as is.
For example, for

(149)

\displaystyle\alpha=\mathbf{2}\hskip 0.85358pt1\hskip 0.85358pt1\hskip 0.85358pt\mathbf{7}\hskip 0.85358pt\mathbf{4}\hskip 0.85358pt3,\,C=\{1,4,5\},\quad\quad\text{we have }\rot_{C}(\alpha)=\mathbf{5}\hskip 0.85358pt1\hskip 0.85358pt1\hskip 0.85358pt\mathbf{2}\hskip 0.85358pt\mathbf{7}\hskip 0.85358pt3,

where the subsequence with indices
C
is shown in bold.

Thus, when
j=1
,
\beta=\rot_{C}(\alpha)
for some
C\subseteq\{1,\dots,N\}
containing 1,
and so
(\beta_{1}-1,\beta_{2},\dots,\beta_{N})
is a rearrangement of
\alpha
.
∎

We will use this to prove a similar result for the integral form stable
r
-nonsymmetric Macdonald polynomials
\check{J}_{\eta|\lambda}
after two preparatory results.
Recall from §
2.2
that
({\mathbb{Q}}(q,t)[{\mathbf{x}}_{N}])_{e}=\{\sum_{\beta\in{\mathbb{N}}^{N}}c_{\beta}{\mathbf{x}}^{\beta}:\ord(c_{\beta})\geq e\}
.

Lemma 8.2.3
.

(i) For
\beta\in{\mathbb{N}}^{N}
,

(150)

\displaystyle\widehat{{\mathcal{S}}}_{r+1}^{N}\check{\mathcal{E}}_{\beta}\in\spn_{{\mathbb{Q}}(q,t)}\{\check{\mathcal{E}}_{\alpha}:\alpha\in S_{(1^{r};N-r)}\beta\},

where
S_{(1^{r};N-r)}\beta
denotes the orbit of
\beta
under the symmetric group action permuting the last
N-r
indices.

(ii) For
\beta\in{\mathbb{N}}^{N}
,
\widehat{{\mathcal{S}}}_{r+1}^{N}\check{\mathcal{E}}_{\beta}\notin({\mathbb{Q}}(q,t)[{\mathbf{x}}_{N}])_{1}
.

(iii)
({\mathbb{Q}}(q,t)[{\mathbf{x}}_{N}])_{e}=\{\sum_{\beta}c_{\beta}\check{\mathcal{E}}_{\beta}:\ord(c_{\beta})\geq e\}
.

Proof.

Part (i) follows from the Knop-Sahi recurrence,
which expresses
T_{i}E_{\beta}
as a
{\mathbb{Q}}(q,t)
-linear combination of
E_{\beta}
and
E_{s_{i}\beta}
(see, e.g.,
[
7
, §5.4]
).

For part (ii), first, for the monically normalized
E_{\beta}
when
\beta_{r+1}\geq\cdots\geq\beta_{N}
, from eq. (178)
and the paragraph surrounding
eq. (316) in
[
7
]
, we have that the coefficient of

{\mathbf{x}}^{\beta}
in
\widehat{{\mathcal{S}}}_{r+1}^{N}E_{\beta}
is equal to
\frac{\prod_{i}[m_{i}]_{t}!}{[N-r]_{t}!}
, where
m_{i}
is the number of
i
’s in
(\beta_{r+1},\dots,\beta_{N})
.
This constant has order of vanishing 0, and so
\widehat{{\mathcal{S}}}_{r+1}^{N}E_{\beta}\notin({\mathbb{Q}}(q,t)[{\mathbf{x}}_{N}])_{1}
.
Since
E_{\beta}
and
{\mathcal{E}}_{\beta}
differ by a scalar factor which has order of vanishing 0 (see (
127
)),
(ii) follows for
\beta
with
\beta_{r+1}\geq\cdots\geq\beta_{N}
. The general case then follows from the fact that
\widehat{{\mathcal{S}}}_{r+1}^{N}{\mathcal{E}}_{\beta}=\widehat{{\mathcal{S}}}_{r+1}^{N}{\mathcal{E}}_{\alpha}
for any
\alpha\in S_{(1^{r};N-r)}\beta
, by
[
7
, Proposition 7.1.6]
.

For (iii), the inclusion
\supseteq
follows from
\check{\mathcal{E}}_{\beta}\in{\mathbb{Z}}[q,q^{-1},t][{\mathbf{x}}_{N}]
.
For the reverse inclusion, first note that the matrix
A
expressing the
\check{\mathcal{E}}_{\beta}
’s in terms of monomials has coefficients in
{\mathbb{Z}}[q,q^{-1},t]

and is triangular with diagonal entries
q^{\mathsf{n}((\beta_{+})^{*})}\bigl(\prod\nolimits_{\,u\in\dg(\beta)}(1-q^{-a(u)-1}\,t^{l(u)+1})\bigr)
.
Hence, restricting to degree
d
and using the adjugate formula for
A^{-1}
,
we see that
A^{-1}
has entries in
\frac{1}{\det(A)}{\mathbb{Z}}[q,q^{-1},t]
. Since
\ord(\det(A))=0
, the inclusion
\subseteq
follows.
∎

Corollary 8.2.4
.

Suppose that

(151)

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha}d_{\alpha}\,\widehat{{\mathcal{S}}}_{r+1}^{N}(\check{\mathcal{E}}_{\alpha})\equiv\sum_{\beta\in{\mathbb{N}}^{N}}c_{\beta}\,\widehat{{\mathcal{S}}}_{r+1}^{N}(\check{\mathcal{E}}_{\beta})\quad\bmod({\mathbb{Q}}(q,t)[{\mathbf{x}}_{N}])_{e},

for coefficients
d_{\alpha},c_{\beta}\in{\mathbb{Q}}(q,t)
,
with the sum on the left containing at most one
\alpha
from each
S_{(1^{r};N-r)}
orbit of
{\mathbb{N}}^{N}
.
Then
\ord(d_{\alpha})<e
implies that for at least one
\beta
in the orbit
S_{(1^{r};N-r)}\alpha
,
c_{\beta}
is nonzero.

Proof.

Expand both sides of (
151
) into the
\check{\mathcal{E}}_{\alpha}
basis of
{\mathbb{Q}}(q,t)[{\mathbf{x}}_{N}]
, and denote
the expansion on the left by
\sum_{\tau\in{\mathbb{N}}^{N}}a_{\tau}\,\check{\mathcal{E}}_{\tau}
.
Given
\ord(d_{\alpha})<e
, Lemma
8.2.3
(ii) implies
d_{\alpha}\,\widehat{{\mathcal{S}}}_{r+1}^{N}(\check{\mathcal{E}}_{\alpha})\notin({\mathbb{Q}}(q,t)[{\mathbf{x}}_{N}])_{e}
.
Then Lemma
8.2.3
(i) and the assumption on the left sum imply
that for some
\tau\in S_{(1^{r};N-r)}\alpha
,

a_{\tau}\,\check{\mathcal{E}}_{\tau}\notin({\mathbb{Q}}(q,t)[{\mathbf{x}}_{N}])_{e}
.
From Lemma
8.2.3
(i) and (iii), it follows that at least one
c_{\beta}
among
\beta\in S_{(1^{r};N-r)}\alpha
is nonzero.
∎

For the next two results, it is convenient to denote elements of
{\mathbb{N}}^{r}\times\Par
by a single symbol,
and we define the following accompanying notation: for
{\boldsymbol{\theta}}=(\eta|\lambda)\in{\mathbb{N}}^{r}\times\Par
, we let
{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
also stand for the concatenation
(\eta;\lambda)
,
so that
{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{+}=(\eta;\lambda)_{+}
,
{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{1}=\eta_{1}
when
r\geq 1
,
{\boldsymbol{\theta}}-\varepsilon_{1}=(\eta_{1}-1,\dots,\eta_{r};\lambda)
, etc.
Also set
\ell({\boldsymbol{\theta}})=r+\ell(\lambda)
.

Proposition 8.2.5
.

For any
{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}\in\pairs
,

(152)

\displaystyle x_{1}\hskip 0.85358pt\check{J}_{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}\in\spn_{{\mathbb{Q}}(q,t)}\big\{\check{J}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}:{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{r}\times\Par\text{ with $r\geq 1$ and }({\boldsymbol{\theta}}-\varepsilon_{1})_{+}={\boldsymbol{\kappa}}_{+}\big\}.

Proof.

By (
138
),

\check{J}_{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}=\check{J}_{\eta|\lambda}
for some

(\eta|\lambda)\in{\mathbb{N}}^{r}\times\Par
where
r\geq 1
. Hence,

x_{1}\check{J}_{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}\in\mathcal{P}(r)
and we can write

(153)

\displaystyle x_{1}\check{J}_{{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}}=\sum_{{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{r}\times\Par}d_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\,\check{J}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}},

with coefficients
d_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\in{\mathbb{Q}}(q,t)
.
We need to show that
d_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\neq 0
only when
({\boldsymbol{\theta}}-\varepsilon_{1})_{+}={\boldsymbol{\kappa}}_{+}
.

Define
D_{{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}^{\prime}}^{N}=\check{J}_{{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}^{\prime}}[{\mathbf{x}}_{N};q,t]-\widehat{{\mathcal{S}}}_{r+1}^{N}(\check{\mathcal{E}}_{({\boldsymbol{\kappa}}^{\prime};0^{N-\ell({\boldsymbol{\kappa}}^{\prime})})})
for
{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{r}\times\Par
and
N\geq r
,
so
that by Definition
7.1.2
, for every
e>0
,
D_{{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}^{\prime}}^{N}\in({\mathbb{Q}}(q,t)[{\mathbf{x}}_{N}])_{e}
for sufficiently large
N
.
For any
N\geq r
, setting the variables
x_{N+1},x_{N+2},\dots
to 0 in (
153
), we obtain

(154)

x_{1}\widehat{{\mathcal{S}}}_{r+1}^{N}(\check{\mathcal{E}}_{({\boldsymbol{\kappa}};0^{N-\ell({\boldsymbol{\kappa}})})})+x_{1}D_{{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}}^{N}=x_{1}\check{J}_{{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}}[{\mathbf{x}}_{N};q,t]\\
=\sum_{{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{r}\times\Par}d_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\,\check{J}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}[{\mathbf{x}}_{N};q,t]=\sum_{{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{r}\times\Par}d_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\big(\widehat{{\mathcal{S}}}_{r+1}^{N}(\check{\mathcal{E}}_{({\boldsymbol{\theta}};0^{N-\ell({\boldsymbol{\theta}})})})+D_{{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}^{N}\big),

By Proposition
8.2.2
and the fact that multiplication by
x_{1}
commutes with
\widehat{{\mathcal{S}}}_{r+1}^{N}
, we have

(155)

\displaystyle x_{1}\hskip 0.85358pt\widehat{{\mathcal{S}}}_{r+1}^{N}(\check{\mathcal{E}}_{({\boldsymbol{\kappa}};0^{N-\ell({\boldsymbol{\kappa}})})})=\widehat{{\mathcal{S}}}_{r+1}^{N}\bigg(\sum_{\beta\in{\mathbb{N}}^{N}\hskip 0.85358pt:\hskip 0.85358pt(\beta-\varepsilon_{1})_{+}={\boldsymbol{\kappa}}_{+}}c_{\beta}\,\check{\mathcal{E}}_{\beta}\bigg),

for coefficients
c_{\beta}\in{\mathbb{Q}}(q,t)
.
Putting (
154
) and (
155
) together we have

(156)

\displaystyle\sum_{\beta\in{\mathbb{N}}^{N}\hskip 0.85358pt:\hskip 0.85358pt(\beta-\varepsilon_{1})_{+}={\boldsymbol{\kappa}}_{+}}\!\!\!c_{\beta}\,\widehat{{\mathcal{S}}}_{r+1}^{N}(\check{\mathcal{E}}_{\beta})=\sum_{{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{r}\times\Par}\!\!d_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\,\widehat{{\mathcal{S}}}_{r+1}^{N}(\check{\mathcal{E}}_{({\boldsymbol{\theta}};0^{N-\ell({\boldsymbol{\theta}})})})+\sum_{{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{r}\times\Par}\!\!d_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\hskip 0.85358ptD_{{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}^{N}-x_{1}\hskip 0.85358ptD_{{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}}^{N}.

Note that there are finitely many nonzero
d_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
and they are independent of
N
, while the
c_{\beta}
depend on
N
.
Choose any
e\geq 0
which is greater than
\max\{\ord(d_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}):d_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\neq 0\}
.
Set
e^{\prime}=\min\{\ord(d_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}):d_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\neq 0\}

(we need to allow for the possibility some
\ord(d_{\boldsymbol{\theta}})
are negative).
Then for sufficiently large
N
, we have
D_{{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}^{N}\in({\mathbb{Q}}(q,t)[{\mathbf{x}}_{N}])_{e-e^{\prime}}
for all nonzero
d_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}

and
D_{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}^{N}\in({\mathbb{Q}}(q,t)[{\mathbf{x}}_{N}])_{e}
.
Hence, for sufficiently large
N
,

(157)

\displaystyle\sum_{\beta\in{\mathbb{N}}^{N}\hskip 0.85358pt:\hskip 0.85358pt(\beta-\varepsilon_{1})_{+}={\boldsymbol{\kappa}}_{+}}c_{\beta}\,\widehat{{\mathcal{S}}}_{r+1}^{N}(\check{\mathcal{E}}_{\beta})\equiv\sum_{{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{r}\times\Par}d_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\,\widehat{{\mathcal{S}}}_{r+1}^{N}(\check{\mathcal{E}}_{({\boldsymbol{\theta}};0^{N-\ell({\boldsymbol{\theta}})})})\quad\bmod({\mathbb{Q}}(q,t)[{\mathbf{x}}_{N}])_{e}

and the assumptions of Corollary
8.2.4
are satisfied. Applying the corollary, we have that for each nonzero
d_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
, some

\beta
in the orbit
S_{(1^{r};N-r)}({\boldsymbol{\theta}};0^{N-\ell({\boldsymbol{\theta}})})
has
c_{\beta}\neq 0
, and therefore
({\boldsymbol{\theta}}-\varepsilon_{1})_{+}=(\beta-\varepsilon_{1})_{+}={\boldsymbol{\kappa}}_{+}
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
8.2.1
.

We will compare the actions of
Y_{1}X_{1}
and
\bar{\nabla}X_{1}\bar{\nabla}^{-1}

on the basis of integral form stable
r
-nonsymmetric Macdonald polynomials.
Let
{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}\in\pairs
. According to Proposition
8.2.5
, we can write
x_{1}\check{J}_{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}=\sum_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}d_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\check{J}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
for
some coefficients
d_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\in{\mathbb{Q}}(q,t)
, over
{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{r}\times\Par
with
r\geq 1
and such that
({\boldsymbol{\theta}}-\varepsilon_{1})_{+}={\boldsymbol{\kappa}}_{+}
.
Hence,

(158)

\displaystyle\bar{\nabla}X_{1}\bar{\nabla}^{-1}\check{J}_{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}=\sum_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}d_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\,q^{\mathsf{n}(({\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{+})^{*})-\mathsf{n}(({\boldsymbol{\kappa}}_{+})^{*})}\,t^{\mathsf{n}({\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{+})-\mathsf{n}({\boldsymbol{\kappa}}_{+})}\,\check{J}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}.

On the other hand, applying Theorem
7.1.4
and noting that since

({\boldsymbol{\theta}}-\varepsilon_{1})_{+}={\boldsymbol{\kappa}}_{+}
, the top case of (
131
) always applies, we have

(159)

\displaystyle Y_{1}X_{1}\,\check{J}_{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}=Y_{1}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}d_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\,\check{J}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}=\sum_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}d_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\,q^{{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{1}}t^{\ell({\boldsymbol{\theta}})+1-\st({\boldsymbol{\theta}})_{1}}\,\check{J}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}.

For
{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
appearing in the sum,
the skew shape
{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{+}/{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}_{+}
is a single box
lying in some row
a
and column
b
.
Still using
({\boldsymbol{\theta}}-\varepsilon_{1})_{+}={\boldsymbol{\kappa}}_{+}
, and given that there are
\big(\ell({\boldsymbol{\theta}})+1-\st({\boldsymbol{\theta}})_{1}\big)
parts of
{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{+}
which
are
\geq{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{1}
,
it follows that
b={\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{1}
and
a=\ell({\boldsymbol{\theta}})+1-\st({\boldsymbol{\theta}})_{1}
.
Moreover,
b-1=\mathsf{n}(({\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{+})^{*})-\mathsf{n}(({\boldsymbol{\kappa}}_{+})^{*})
and
a-1=\mathsf{n}({\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{+})-\mathsf{n}({\boldsymbol{\kappa}}_{+})
directly from definition of the statistic
\mathsf{n}
.
Thus, the right hand side of (
159
) is
qt
times the right hand side of (
158
).
∎

8.3.
Two versions of nonsymmetric nabla agree

By
[
29
, Theorem 3.13]
, the left
{\mathbb{A}}_{t,q}
-module homomorphism
{\mathbb{A}}_{t,q}e_{0}\to V_{*}
,
e_{0}\mapsto 1\in V_{0}
is surjective; let
I
denote the kernel.
On
[
29
, pg. 19]
it is noted that
the map
{\mathbb{A}}_{t,q}e_{0}\to{\mathbb{A}}_{t,q}e_{0}
induced by the endomorphism
N
defined in (
108
) preserves
I
and therefore yields
endomorphisms
\widecheck{\nabla}\colon V_{r}\to V_{r}

for all
r\geq 0
. This collection of endomorphisms satisfies

\widecheck{\nabla}(1)=1
for
1\in V_{0}
, and

(160)

\displaystyle\widecheck{\nabla}\circ L=N(L)\circ\widecheck{\nabla}\quad\text{for all }L\in{\mathbb{A}}_{t,q}.

In fact,
\widecheck{\nabla}
is determined by these two properties.

Mellit shows
[
29
, pg. 19]
that
\widecheck{\nabla}
restricted to
V_{0}=\Lambda(W)
is the same as
the operator
\nabla
up to a sign.
The following result generalizes this to the nonsymmetric setting, in the sense that it relates

\widecheck{\nabla}
, acting now on any
V_{r}
, to a nonsymmetric version of
\nabla
defined to act diagonally on a Macdonald basis.
To see that it is indeed a generalization of Mellit’s result, observe that when
r=0
, by (
125
) and (
145
), both sides of (
162
) become
\omega\nabla\omega f
.

Theorem 8.3.1
.

For any
f\in\mathcal{P}(r)
of degree
d
,

(161)

\displaystyle\bar{\nabla}f

\displaystyle=(-1)^{d}\widecheck{\nabla}^{{\mathsf{P}}}f,

(162)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\nabla}f

\displaystyle=(-1)^{d}{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{r}\widecheck{\nabla}^{{\mathsf{P}}}{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{r}^{-1}f,

where
\widecheck{\nabla}^{{\mathsf{P}}}
is the endomorphism of
\mathcal{P}(r)
given by
{\mathsf{P}}_{r}^{-1}\widecheck{\nabla}{\mathsf{P}}_{r}
.

Remark 8.3.2
.

The operator
\bar{\nabla}
is clearly an invertible operator on
\mathcal{P}_{\rm as}
.
Thus, Theorem
8.3.1
implies that
\widecheck{\nabla}
is invertible, which is not clear from the definition.

We will prove Theorem
8.3.1
with the help of the following lemma.

Lemma 8.3.3
.

The endomorphism
N
satisfies

(163)

\displaystyle N(T_{i})=T_{i},\ \ N(z_{i})=z_{i},\ \ N(y_{1})=-(qt)^{-1}\hskip 0.85358ptz_{1}y_{1}.

Proof.

The first two identities are immediate from (
108
).
For the last, we compute, for the loop
y_{1}\in{\mathbb{A}}_{t,q}
at quiver vertex
r\geq 1
,

\displaystyle N(y_{1})

\displaystyle=N\left(\frac{1}{t^{r-1}(t-1)}[d_{+},d_{-}]T_{r-1}\cdots T_{1}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{-1}{t^{r-1}(t-1)}(qt)^{-1}(z_{1}d_{+}d_{-}-d_{-}z_{1}d_{+})T_{r-1}\cdots T_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{-1}{t^{r-1}(t-1)}(qt)^{-1}(z_{1}d_{+}d_{-}-z_{1}d_{-}d_{+})T_{r-1}\cdots T_{1}

\displaystyle=-(qt)^{-1}z_{1}y_{1}\,,

where in the third equality, we have used the relation
d_{-}z_{i}=z_{i}d_{-}
of
{\mathbb{A}}_{t,q}

[
21
, eq. 7.3(d)]
, which holds for paths starting from quiver vertex
r
when
1\leq i<r
;
this applies since precomposing with
d_{+}
yields the relation
d_{-}z_{i}d_{+}=z_{i}d_{-}d_{+}
for
1\leq i\leq r
, as an identity of paths starting at quiver vertex
r
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
8.3.1
.

Note that second identity (
162
) is immediate from (
161
)
and (
146
).

We now prove (
161
).
First, by Theorem
3.3.2
, the definition of
N
in
(
108
), the relationship between
N
and
\widecheck{\nabla}

in (
160
), and Lemma
8.3.3
,

(164)

\displaystyle\widecheck{\nabla}^{\mathsf{P}}\,T_{i}

\displaystyle=T_{i}\,\widecheck{\nabla}^{\mathsf{P}},

(165)

\displaystyle\widecheck{\nabla}^{\mathsf{P}}\,d_{-}^{\mathsf{P}}

\displaystyle=d_{-}^{\mathsf{P}}\,\widecheck{\nabla}^{\mathsf{P}},

(166)

\displaystyle\widecheck{\nabla}^{\mathsf{P}}\,X_{1}

\displaystyle=-(qt)^{-1}Y_{1}X_{1}\,\widecheck{\nabla}^{\mathsf{P}},

where (
164
) is an identity of operators from
\mathcal{P}(r)\to\mathcal{P}(r)
and
i<r
,
(
165
) is an identity of operators from
\mathcal{P}(r)\to\mathcal{P}(r-1)
,
and (
166
) is an identity of operators from
\mathcal{P}(r)\to\mathcal{P}(r)
for
r\geq 1
.

Next, we see that the corresponding identities hold for
\bar{\nabla}
:
by Proposition
7.1.7
,

(167)

\displaystyle\bar{\nabla}\,T_{i}

\displaystyle=T_{i}\,\bar{\nabla},

and (
133
) (keeping in mind Proposition
4.1.1
) gives

(168)

\displaystyle\bar{\nabla}\,d_{-}^{\mathsf{P}}

\displaystyle=d_{-}^{\mathsf{P}}\,\bar{\nabla}.

Finally, Lemma
8.2.1
is the identity corresponding to
(
166
), up to a sign.

Let
1_{k}
denote the element 1 in
\mathcal{P}(k)
or
V_{k}
.
To complete the proof it suffices to show that
any element of
\mathcal{P}(r)
can be generated using the operators
X_{1}
,
T_{i}
, and
d_{-}^{\mathsf{P}}
, starting from
1_{r^{\prime}}
for
r^{\prime}\geq r
, and that both
\bar{\nabla}
and
\widecheck{\nabla}^{\mathsf{P}}
act as the identity on
1_{r^{\prime}}
.
Indeed, by
[
29
, eq. (16)]
, the following elements form a basis for
V_{r}
:

(169)

\displaystyle d_{-}^{\ell}\,y_{1}^{\eta_{1}}\cdots y_{r}^{\eta_{r}}y_{r+1}^{\lambda_{1}}\cdots y_{r+\ell}^{\lambda_{\ell}}\cdot 1_{r+\ell},\quad\text{ for }(\eta|\lambda)\in{\mathbb{N}}^{r}\times\Par,

where
\ell=\ell(\lambda)
.
Hence, we have the corresponding basis of
\mathcal{P}(r)

(170)

\displaystyle(d_{-}^{\mathsf{P}})^{\ell}\,X_{1}^{\eta_{1}}\cdots X_{r}^{\eta_{r}}X_{r+1}^{\lambda_{1}}\cdots X_{r+\ell}^{\lambda_{\ell}}\cdot 1_{r+\ell},\quad\text{ for }(\eta|\lambda)\in{\mathbb{N}}^{r}\times\Par.

The operators in (
170
) are generated by
X_{1}
,
T_{i}
, and
d_{-}^{\mathsf{P}}
since
X_{j}=t^{j-1}\,T_{j-1}^{-1}\cdots T_{1}^{-1}X_{1}T_{1}^{-1}\cdots T_{j-1}^{-1}
.

Finally, we have
\bar{\nabla}(1_{k})=1_{k}
as
1_{k}=\check{J}_{\eta|\lambda}
for
(\eta|\lambda)
the empty pair
(\varnothing|\varnothing)
, and

(171)

\widecheck{\nabla}(1_{k})=\widecheck{\nabla}(d_{+}^{*})^{k}\cdot 1_{0}=N((d_{+}^{*})^{k})\widecheck{\nabla}(1_{0})=(d_{+}^{*})^{k}\widecheck{\nabla}(1_{0})=1_{k}.\qed

8.4.
The
\boldsymbol{\nabla}
formulation of the nonsymmetric compositional shuffle theorem

Say that a map
\varphi\colon M\to N
of
{\mathbb{Q}}(q,t)
-vector spaces is
{\mathbb{Q}}(q,t)
-antilinear
if it is
{\mathbb{Q}}
-linear and
\varphi(c(q,t)g)=c(q^{-1},t^{-1})\varphi(g)

for any
c(q,t)\in{\mathbb{Q}}(q,t)
and
g\in M
.

Proposition 8.4.1

(
[
29
, Proposition 3.15]
)
.

There is a
{\mathbb{Q}}(q,t)
-antilinear algebra involution
on the Dyck path algebra
\bar{w}\colon{\mathbb{A}}_{t}\to{\mathbb{A}}_{t}
determined by

(172)

\displaystyle e_{r}\mapsto e_{r},\ \ T_{i}\mapsto T_{i}^{-1},\ \ d_{-}\mapsto d_{-},\ \ d_{+}\mapsto-t^{-r}d_{+},

where the
d_{+}
’s are arrows starting at quiver vertex
r
.

Theorem 8.4.2

(
[
29
, Theorem 3.12]
)
.

We have the following isomorphism of left
{\mathbb{A}}_{t}
-modules:

(173)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{A}}_{t}e_{0}\xrightarrow{\cong}\bigoplus_{r=0}^{\infty}y_{1}\cdots y_{r}V_{r},\quad\text{determined by }e_{0}\mapsto 1\in V_{0}.

The involution
\bar{w}
preserves the left ideal
{\mathbb{A}}_{t}e_{0}
, i.e., it
restricts to a
{\mathbb{Q}}
-linear involution
{\mathbb{A}}_{t}e_{0}\to{\mathbb{A}}_{t}e_{0}
(not an
{\mathbb{A}}_{t}
-module homomorphism).
We let
\widecheck{w}\colon\bigoplus_{r=0}^{\infty}y_{1}\cdots y_{r}V_{r}\to\bigoplus_{r=0}^{\infty}y_{1}\cdots y_{r}V_{r}
denote the corresponding involution obtained via Theorem
8.4.2
. It takes
1\in V_{0}
to itself and

(174)

\displaystyle\widecheck{w}\circ L=\bar{w}(L)\circ\widecheck{w}\quad\text{for all }L\in{\mathbb{A}}_{t}\,.

It is determined by these two properties.

The next two results require the notation
d^{CM}_{-}
, etc.
from §
3.2
for the Carlsson-Mellit
[
10
]
action of
{\mathbb{A}}_{t,q}
on
V_{*}
,
and the isomorphism
{\mathsf{Q}}_{r}\colon y_{1}\cdots y_{r}V_{r}\to V_{r},\,y_{1}\cdots y_{r}v\mapsto v.

Theorem 8.4.3

(
[
10
, Theorem 7.4]
)
.

There are
{\mathbb{Q}}(q,t)
-antilinear maps
\mathcal{N}\colon V_{r}\to V_{r}
for
r\geq 0

such that
\mathcal{N}(1)=1
for
1\in V_{0}
, and which satisfy

(175)

\displaystyle\ \ \mathcal{N}\hskip 0.85358ptT^{CM}_{i}=(T^{CM}_{i})^{-1}\hskip 0.85358pt\mathcal{N},\ \ \mathcal{N}\hskip 0.85358ptd^{CM}_{-}=d^{CM}_{-}\hskip 0.85358pt\mathcal{N},\ \ \mathcal{N}\hskip 0.85358ptd^{CM}_{+}=d^{*CM}_{+}\hskip 0.85358pt\mathcal{N}.

Moreover,
\mathcal{N}\colon V_{r}\to V_{r}
is an involution, i.e.,
\mathcal{N}^{2}=\text{\rm id}
.

Let
\mathcal{N}^{\mathsf{Q}}
denote the collection of maps
{\mathsf{Q}}_{r}^{-1}\mathcal{N}{\mathsf{Q}}_{r}\colon y_{1}\cdots y_{r}V_{r}\to y_{1}\cdots y_{r}V_{r}
for
r\geq 0
.

Lemma 8.4.4
.

We have the following identities of operators on
(y_{1}\cdots y_{r}V_{r})_{r\geq 0}
:

(176)

\displaystyle\widecheck{\nabla}\widecheck{w}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{N}^{\mathsf{Q}},

(177)

\displaystyle\widecheck{w}\widecheck{\nabla}^{-1}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{N}^{\mathsf{Q}}.

Proof.

Note that it is not yet clear that
\widecheck{\nabla}\widecheck{w}
preserves the space
(y_{1}\cdots y_{r}V_{r})_{r\geq 0}
,
but since
\mathcal{N}^{\mathsf{Q}}
does, this will follow once we prove (
176
)
as an identity of maps from
y_{1}\cdots y_{r}V_{r}\to V_{r}
.
To prove this, we will compare how
\mathcal{N}^{\mathsf{Q}}
and
\widecheck{\nabla}\widecheck{w}
intertwine the generators of
{\mathbb{A}}_{t}
.

By ‘conjugating’ (
175
) by the maps
{\mathsf{Q}}_{r}
, we have

\mathcal{N}^{\mathsf{Q}}{\mathsf{Q}}_{r}^{-1}T^{CM}_{i}{\mathsf{Q}}_{r}={\mathsf{Q}}_{r}^{-1}(T^{CM}_{i})^{-1}{\mathsf{Q}}_{r}\hskip 0.85358pt\mathcal{N}^{\mathsf{Q}}
,

\mathcal{N}^{\mathsf{Q}}\hskip 0.85358pt{\mathsf{Q}}_{r-1}^{-1}d^{CM}_{-}{\mathsf{Q}}_{r}={\mathsf{Q}}_{r-1}^{-1}d^{CM}_{-}{\mathsf{Q}}_{r}\hskip 0.85358pt\mathcal{N}^{\mathsf{Q}}
, and

\mathcal{N}^{\mathsf{Q}}\hskip 0.85358pt{\mathsf{Q}}_{r+1}^{-1}d^{CM}_{+}{\mathsf{Q}}_{r}={\mathsf{Q}}_{r+1}^{-1}d^{*CM}_{+}{\mathsf{Q}}_{r}^{-1}\hskip 0.85358pt\mathcal{N}^{\mathsf{Q}}.

Proposition
3.2.2
then gives

(178)

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}^{\mathsf{Q}}\,T_{i}=T_{i}^{-1}\mathcal{N}^{\mathsf{Q}},

(179)

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}^{\mathsf{Q}}\,d_{-}=d_{-}\,\mathcal{N}^{\mathsf{Q}},

(180)

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}^{\mathsf{Q}}\,(-d_{+})=y_{1}d_{+}^{*}\,\mathcal{N}^{\mathsf{Q}}.

Now to address
\widecheck{\nabla}\widecheck{w}
, (
108
) and (
172
) directly give

(181)

\displaystyle\widecheck{\nabla}\widecheck{w}\,T_{i}=T_{i}^{-1}\widecheck{\nabla}\widecheck{w},

(182)

\displaystyle\widecheck{\nabla}\widecheck{w}\,d_{-}=d_{-}\,\widecheck{\nabla}\widecheck{w}.

Further, computing with (
174
) and (
160
), we have

(183)

\displaystyle\widecheck{\nabla}\widecheck{w}\,d_{+}=-t^{-r}\widecheck{\nabla}d_{+}\widecheck{w}=-t^{-r}N(d_{+})\widecheck{\nabla}\widecheck{w}=t^{-r}(qt)^{-1}z_{1}d_{+}\widecheck{\nabla}\widecheck{w},

where these are identities of operators on
V_{r}
.
Using the relation (
26
) of
{\mathbb{A}}_{t,q}
,
which says that
z_{1}d_{+}=-qt^{r+1}y_{1}d_{+}^{*}
, we obtain

(184)

\displaystyle\widecheck{\nabla}\widecheck{w}(-d_{+})=y_{1}d_{+}^{*}\widecheck{\nabla}\widecheck{w}.

Since the
d_{+}
’s,
d_{-}
’s, and
T_{i}
’s generate

{\mathbb{A}}_{t}
, they also generate
(y_{1}\cdots y_{r}V_{r})_{r\geq 0}
from
1\in V_{0}
by Theorem
8.4.2
.
Hence, (
176
) follows from the matching intertwining relations
(
178
)–(
180
) and (
181
)–(
184
).

Now that we know
\widecheck{\nabla}\widecheck{w}=\mathcal{N}^{\mathsf{Q}}
as operators on
y_{1}\cdots y_{r}V_{r}
,
using that
\widecheck{w}
is an involution on
y_{1}\cdots y_{r}V_{r}
, we find

\widecheck{\nabla}=\mathcal{N}^{\mathsf{Q}}\widecheck{w}
as operators on
y_{1}\cdots y_{r}V_{r}
.
Then since
\mathcal{N}^{\mathsf{Q}}
is also an involution on
y_{1}\cdots y_{r}V_{r}
,
\widecheck{\nabla}
is invertible as an operator on
y_{1}\cdots y_{r}V_{r}
with inverse

\widecheck{\nabla}^{-1}=\widecheck{w}\mathcal{N}^{Q}
. The identity (
177
) follows.
∎

Recall that
\rho_{m,n}^{*}
,
\rho
, and
\rho^{*}
are the algebra homomorphisms from
{\mathbb{A}}_{t}\to{\mathbb{A}}_{t,q}
from Proposition-Definition
6.1.2
and
Remark
3.1.3
.

Proposition 8.4.5
.

For any
L\in{\mathbb{A}}_{t}
,

(185)

\displaystyle\rho_{m,1}^{*}(L)=\widecheck{w}\widecheck{\nabla}^{-m}\,L\,\widecheck{\nabla}^{m}\widecheck{w}

as operators on
(y_{1}\cdots y_{r}V_{r})_{r\geq 0}
.

Note that it is not clear that

\rho_{m,1}^{*}(L)

preserves
(y_{1}\cdots y_{r}V_{r})_{r\geq 0}
, but this will follow from the proof since the right hand side does.

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle\widecheck{w}\widecheck{\nabla}^{-m}\,L\,\widecheck{\nabla}^{m}\widecheck{w}

\displaystyle=\widecheck{\nabla}^{m}\widecheck{w}\,L\,\widecheck{w}\widecheck{\nabla}^{-m}

by Lemma
8.4.4

\displaystyle=\widecheck{\nabla}^{m}\bar{w}(L)\widecheck{\nabla}^{-m}

by (
174
)

\displaystyle=N^{m}(\rho(\bar{w}(L)))

by (
160
)

\displaystyle=(N^{m-1}\circ S\circ\rho^{*})(L)

by
[
29
, eq. (19)]

\displaystyle=\rho_{m,1}^{*}(L).

Theorem 8.4.6
.

The algebraic side of the signed nonsymmetric compositional
(km,kn)
-shuffle theorem for
n=1

(defined in (
113
))
can be expressed in terms of
\bar{\nabla}
and
\widecheck{w}^{\mathsf{P}}\overset{\text{{def}}}{=}{\mathsf{P}}_{r}^{-1}\widecheck{w}{\mathsf{P}}_{r}
as follows:

(186)

\displaystyle\mathsf{C}_{m,1}^{\alpha}=(-1)^{k}(-t)^{r-k}\widecheck{w}^{\mathsf{P}}\bar{\nabla}^{-m}(x_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}\cdots x_{r}^{\alpha_{r}}).

Proof.

Using Proposition
8.4.5
followed by the
easily verified fact
d_{+}^{r}\cdot 1=(-1)^{r}y_{1}\cdots y_{r}
,

(187)

\rho_{m,1}^{*}(y_{1}^{\alpha_{1}-1}\cdots y_{r}^{\alpha_{r}-1}d_{+}^{r})\cdot 1=\widecheck{w}\widecheck{\nabla}^{-m}\,y_{1}^{\alpha_{1}-1}\cdots y_{r}^{\alpha_{r}-1}d_{+}^{r}\widecheck{\nabla}^{m}\widecheck{w}\cdot 1\\
=(-1)^{r}\widecheck{w}\widecheck{\nabla}^{-m}\,y_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}\cdots y_{r}^{\alpha_{r}}=(-1)^{r}\widecheck{w}\widecheck{\nabla}^{-m}{\mathsf{P}}_{r}\,x_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}\cdots x_{r}^{\alpha_{r}}.

Hence,

(188)

\mathsf{C}_{m,1}^{\alpha}=(-1)^{k(m+2)}t^{r-k}(-1)^{r}{\mathsf{P}}_{r}^{-1}\widecheck{w}\widecheck{\nabla}^{-m}{\mathsf{P}}_{r}\,x_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}\cdots x_{r}^{\alpha_{r}}\\
=(-1)^{k(m+2)+r}t^{r-k}\widecheck{w}^{\mathsf{P}}(\widecheck{\nabla}^{\mathsf{P}})^{-m}\,x_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}\cdots x_{r}^{\alpha_{r}}=(-1)^{k}(-t)^{r-k}\widecheck{w}^{\mathsf{P}}\bar{\nabla}^{-m}(x_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}\cdots x_{r}^{\alpha_{r}}),

where we have used Theorem
8.3.1
for the last equality.
∎

We prefer to have a nonsymmetric shuffle statement without the
\widecheck{w}^{\mathsf{P}}
,
and the following result makes this possible.

Proposition 8.4.7
.

The operator
\widecheck{w}^{\mathsf{P}}
takes signed flagged row LLT polynomials to signed flagged column LLT polynomials:
for
\pi\in{\mathbb{D}}_{r}(d)
and a marking
\Sigma
of
\pi
,

(189)

\displaystyle\widecheck{w}^{\mathsf{P}}\mathsf{G}^{\pm}_{r}(\pi,\Sigma)({\mathbf{x}};t)=(-1)^{d}\tilde{\mathsf{G}}^{\pm}_{r}(\pi,\Sigma)({\mathbf{x}};t^{-1}).

Proof.

Comparing the definitions of
\chi^{\mathsf{P}}_{r}(\pi,\Sigma)
and
\tilde{\chi}^{\mathsf{P}}_{r}(\pi,\Sigma)

(Definitions
5.5.1
and
5.6.3
), we see

\widecheck{w}^{\mathsf{P}}\chi^{\mathsf{P}}_{r}(\pi,\Sigma)=(-1)^{d}\tilde{\chi}^{\mathsf{P}}_{r}(\pi,\Sigma)
.
The result then follows from Theorems
5.5.4
and
5.6.4
.
∎

Using Theorem
8.4.6
and Proposition
8.4.7
to rewrite
Theorem
6.2.1
, we obtain another version of the
signed nonsymmetric compositional
(km,kn)
-shuffle theorem for
n=1
, which expresses
\bar{\nabla}^{-m}
of a monomial
in terms of signed flagged column LLT polynomials.

Corollary 8.4.8
.

Maintain the notation from the beginning of
§
6.2
, §
5.1
, and §
5.6
.
Then for a positive integer
m
and a strict composition
\alpha=(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{r})

of size
k
,

(190)

\displaystyle(-t)^{r-k}\vartheta\bar{\nabla}^{-m}(x_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}\cdots x_{r}^{\alpha_{r}})=\sum_{\pi\in{\mathbb{D}}^{\alpha}_{km,k}}q^{\area(\pi)}t^{\dinv(\pi)-\maxtdinv(\pi)}\tilde{\mathsf{G}}^{\pm}_{r}(\hat{\pi}^{\prime},\Sigma_{\pi}),

where
\vartheta\colon\mathcal{P}_{\rm as}\to\mathcal{P}_{\rm as}
is the
{\mathbb{Q}}(q,t)
-antilinear map
given by
f({\mathbf{x}};q,t)\mapsto f({\mathbf{x}};q^{-1},t^{-1})
.

We are now ready to prove Theorem
1.1.1
from the introduction.
Recall that the nonsymmetric compositional Hall-Littlewood polynomial
\mathsf{C}_{\alpha}
is given by

(191)

\displaystyle\mathsf{C}_{\alpha}=(-t)^{|\alpha|-r}{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{r}(x_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}\cdots x_{r}^{\alpha_{r}})=(-t)^{|\alpha|-r}\pol\Big(\frac{x_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}\cdots x_{r}^{\alpha_{r}}}{\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq r}(1-tx_{i}/x_{j})}\Big)\ \in{\mathbb{Q}}(t)[{\mathbf{x}}_{r}]\subseteq\mathcal{P}(r).

It is natural to allow
\alpha\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{r}
in the definition, but we will only use the case
\alpha
is a strict composition.
These are related to the compositional Hall-Littlewood polynomials by Weyl symmetrization:

{\boldsymbol{\pi}}_{1}\cdots{\boldsymbol{\pi}}_{r}\mathsf{C}_{\alpha}=C_{\alpha}({\mathbf{x}};t^{-1})
. This follows from, e.g.,
[
8
, Proposition 4.6]
.

Define a
flagged word parking function

w
on
\pi\in{\mathbb{D}}^{\alpha}_{km,kn}

to be a map from the north steps of
\pi
to
{\mathbb{Z}}_{+}
which is strictly increasing going north along vertical runs and such that the entry on the north step from the point

(m\sum_{i<j}\alpha_{i},n\sum_{i<j}\alpha_{i})
is
\leq j
, for
j=1,\dots,r
.
Denote the set of these by
\FWPF_{\alpha}(\pi)
.

For
w\in\FWPF_{\alpha}(\pi)
, define the
diagonal inversion statistic
by

(192)

\displaystyle\dinv(\pi,w)=\big|\big\{\text{pairs of north steps $i$, $j$ of $\pi$}:\text{ $i$ attacks $j$ and $w(i)<w(j)$}\big\}\big|.

Corollary 8.4.9
.

With notation as in Corollary
8.4.8
and just above,

(193)

\displaystyle\vartheta\boldsymbol{\nabla}^{-m}(\mathsf{C}_{\alpha})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\pi\in{\mathbb{D}}^{\alpha}_{km,k}}q^{\area(\pi)}t^{\dinv(\pi)-\maxtdinv(\pi)}\tilde{\mathsf{G}}_{r}(\hat{\pi}^{\prime},\Sigma_{\pi})

(194)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\pi\in{\mathbb{D}}^{\alpha}_{km,k}}\sum_{w\in\FWPF_{\alpha}(\pi)}q^{\area(\pi)}t^{\dinv(\pi)-\maxtdinv(\pi)+\dinv(\pi,w)}{\mathbf{x}}^{\content(w)}.

Proof.

Applying
\vartheta\hskip 0.85358pt{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{r}\hskip 0.85358pt\vartheta
to both sides of (
190
) and using
(
96
) gives (
193
).
The second identity (
194
) then follows from

\tilde{\mathsf{G}}_{r}(\hat{\pi}^{\prime},\Sigma_{\pi})=\sum_{w\in\FWPF_{\alpha}(\pi)}t^{\dinv(\pi,w)}{\mathbf{x}}^{\content(w)}
,
which comes from
converting the
definition of
\tilde{\mathsf{G}}_{r}(\hat{\pi}^{\prime},\Sigma_{\pi})
to the language of word parking functions, using the correspondence between attacking pairs of north steps of
\pi
and
\Area(\pi^{\prime})
from §
5.1
.
∎

Setting
m=1
, we obtain Theorem
1.1.1
once we check that the statistics simplify in this case.

Proposition 8.4.10
.

For a
(k,k)
-Dyck path
\pi
,
\dinv(\pi)=\maxtdinv(\pi)
.

Proof.

For each east step
e
of
\pi
, let
\theta(e)
be the first north step of
\pi
which is hit when following a line of slope 1 down from the midpoint of
e
. One checks that for a pair
(e,f)
with

e
an east step of
\pi
and
f
a north step of
\pi
,
(e,f)
counts towards
\dinv(\pi)
if and only if
\theta(e)
attacks
f
(as in §
5.1
).
The result then follows from (
114
).
∎

It is instructive to see how Corollary
8.4.9
with
m=1
Weyl symmetrizes
to the compositional shuffle theorem
conjectured by Haglund-Morse-Zabrocki
[
16
]
and proven by Carlsson-Mellit
[
10
]
.

First note that we have the following identity of symmetric function operators:

(195)

\displaystyle\vartheta\hskip 0.85358pt\omega\hskip 0.85358pt\nabla^{-1}\hskip 0.85358pt\omega\hskip 0.85358pt\vartheta=\nabla,

which follows from
\omega\tilde{H}_{\mu}({\mathbf{x}};q,t)=q^{\mathsf{n}(\mu^{*})}t^{\mathsf{n}(\mu)}\tilde{H}_{\mu}({\mathbf{x}};q^{-1},t^{-1})
.
Then using (
145
),
{\boldsymbol{\pi}}_{1}\cdots{\boldsymbol{\pi}}_{r}\mathsf{C}_{\alpha}=C_{\alpha}({\mathbf{x}};t^{-1})
, and (
195
), we have

(196)

{\boldsymbol{\pi}}_{1}\cdots{\boldsymbol{\pi}}_{r}\hskip 0.85358pt\vartheta\hskip 0.85358pt\boldsymbol{\nabla}^{-m}(\mathsf{C}_{\alpha})=\vartheta\hskip 0.85358pt{\boldsymbol{\pi}}_{1}\cdots{\boldsymbol{\pi}}_{r}\hskip 0.85358pt\boldsymbol{\nabla}^{-m}(\mathsf{C}_{\alpha})=\vartheta\hskip 0.85358pt\omega\nabla^{-m}\omega\hskip 0.85358pt{\boldsymbol{\pi}}_{1}\cdots{\boldsymbol{\pi}}_{r}\mathsf{C}_{\alpha}\\
=\vartheta\hskip 0.85358pt\omega\nabla^{-m}\omega\hskip 0.85358ptC_{\alpha}({\mathbf{x}};t^{-1})=\nabla^{m}\vartheta\hskip 0.85358ptC_{\alpha}({\mathbf{x}};t^{-1})=\nabla^{m}C_{\alpha}({\mathbf{x}};t),

while by (
105
),

(197)

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\pi}}_{1}\cdots{\boldsymbol{\pi}}_{r}\,\tilde{\mathsf{G}}_{r}(\hat{\pi}^{\prime},\Sigma_{\pi})=\tilde{\mathsf{G}}_{0}(\pi^{\prime},\Sigma_{\pi})={\mathcal{G}}_{{\boldsymbol{\eta}}}({\mathbf{x}};t),

where
{\mathcal{G}}_{{\boldsymbol{\eta}}}({\mathbf{x}};t)
is an ordinary LLT polynomial indexed by the tuple of single-column skew shapes
{\boldsymbol{\eta}}
,
determined from
\hat{\pi}^{\prime},\Sigma_{\pi}
as in Remark
5.6.2
.
Thus applying
{\boldsymbol{\pi}}_{1}\cdots{\boldsymbol{\pi}}_{r}
to both sides of
(
193
) yields

(198)

\displaystyle\nabla^{m}C_{\alpha}({\mathbf{x}};t)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\pi\in{\mathbb{D}}^{\alpha}_{km,k}}\!q^{\area(\pi)}t^{\dinv(\pi)-\maxtdinv(\pi)}\tilde{\mathsf{G}}_{0}(\pi^{\prime},\Sigma_{\pi})

(199)

\displaystyle=\!\sum_{\pi\in{\mathbb{D}}^{\alpha}_{km,k}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{word parking}\\
\text{functions $w$ of $\pi$}\end{subarray}}\!\!q^{\area(\pi)}t^{\dinv(\pi)-\maxtdinv(\pi)+\dinv(\pi,w)}{\mathbf{x}}^{\content(w)},\!

where the word parking functions on
\pi
are as in (
1
) (the same as the flagged word parking functions defined above but without the flag bounds).
After swapping
q
and
t
, this statement for
m=1
is the same as the statement of the compositional shuffle conjecture in
[
16
, Conjecture 4.5]
.

8.5.
Atom positivity conjectures

We discuss atom positivity conjectures for several objects in this paper.
This ties into the discussion in §
1.2
and provides context and motivation for
several objects on the modified side of Figure
2
including
\boldsymbol{\nabla}^{-1}\mathsf{C}_{\alpha}
and the modified
r
-nonsymmetric Macdonald polynomials
\check{\mathsf{H}}_{\eta|\lambda}
.

Recall that
{\mathbf{x}}=x_{1},x_{2},\dots,
and
{\mathbf{x}}_{N}=x_{1},\dots,x_{N}
, and that
for
f({\mathbf{x}})\in\mathcal{P}(r)
, we write
f[{\mathbf{x}}_{N}]
for
f({\mathbf{x}})|_{x_{N+1}=x_{N+2}=\cdots=0}
.

A typical element of
\mathcal{P}(r)
is an infinite sum of monomials and does not lie in
the span of the Demazure atoms, so we need stable versions of Demazure atoms which span
\mathcal{P}(r)
.
For
(\eta|\lambda)\in{\mathbb{N}}^{r}\times\Par
with
k=\ell(\lambda)
, define the
stable atom
by

(200)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{A}}_{\eta|\lambda}={\mathcal{A}}_{\eta|\lambda}({\mathbf{x}})={\boldsymbol{\pi}}_{r+1}\cdots{\boldsymbol{\pi}}_{r+k}\,{\mathcal{A}}_{(\eta;\lambda)}({\mathbf{x}}_{r+k})\,\in\mathcal{P}(r),

where
{\mathcal{A}}_{(\eta;\lambda)}
is the Demazure atom (defined in §
1.2
) indexed by the concatenation of
\eta
and
\lambda
.
The stable atoms form a basis for
\mathcal{P}(r)
as
(\eta|\lambda)
ranges over
{\mathbb{N}}^{r}\times\Par
.

The evaluations of stable atoms in a finite number of variables are related to Demazure atoms as follows.
For
(\eta|\lambda)\in{\mathbb{N}}^{r}\times\Par
and
N\geq r+\ell(\lambda)
, we have

(201)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{A}}_{\eta|\lambda}[{\mathbf{x}}_{N}]=\pi_{x_{r+1},\dots,x_{N}}{\mathcal{A}}_{(\eta;\lambda;0^{d})}({\mathbf{x}}_{N})=\sum_{w\in S_{(1^{r};N-r)}}{\mathcal{A}}_{w(\eta;\lambda;0^{d})}({\mathbf{x}}_{N}),

where
\pi_{x_{r+1},\dots,x_{N}}
is the operator
\pi_{w}
(see §
1.2
)
for
w
the longest permutation in
S_{(1^{r};N-r)}
,
and
d=N-r-\ell(\lambda)
.

Remark 8.5.1
.

By the first equality of (
201
), the stable atom
{\mathcal{A}}_{\eta|\lambda}({\mathbf{x}})
is also equal to the strongly convergent limit

\lim_{N\to\infty}\pi_{x_{r+1},\dots,x_{N}}{\mathcal{A}}_{(\eta;\lambda;0^{d})}({\mathbf{x}}_{N})
. This is the definition of stable atom used in

[
7
, §7.6]
, and so our notions of stable atoms agree.

Remark 8.5.2
.

(i)
Any
f({\mathbf{x}})\in\mathcal{P}(r)
of degree
d
can be recovered from

f[{\mathbf{x}}_{N}]
for
N\geq d+r
via
f({\mathbf{x}})={\boldsymbol{\pi}}_{r+1}\cdots{\boldsymbol{\pi}}_{N}f[{\mathbf{x}}_{N}]
.
This follows, for instance, by writing
f
in terms of the basis

\big\{x_{1}^{\eta_{1}}\cdots x_{r}^{\eta_{r}}s_{\lambda}(x_{r+1},x_{r+2},\dots):(\eta|\lambda)\in{\mathbb{N}}^{r}\times\Par\big\}

of
\mathcal{P}(r)
.

(ii) Stable atom positivity essentially reduces to the more familiar notion of atom positivity:
for any
f({\mathbf{x}})\in\mathcal{P}(r)
of degree
d
and
N\geq d+r
,

f({\mathbf{x}})
is stable atom positive if and only if

f[{\mathbf{x}}_{N}]
is atom positive.
This follows from (i), (
201
), and the fact that any
g\in{\mathbb{Q}}(q,t)[{\mathbf{x}}_{N}]
which is symmetric in

x_{r+1},\dots,x_{N}
has atom expansion
g=\sum_{\alpha}c_{\alpha}{\mathcal{A}}_{\alpha}
with the coefficients
c_{\alpha}
constant
on
S_{(1^{r};N-r)}
orbits.

Consider the full Weyl symmetrization operator
{\boldsymbol{\pi}}_{1}\cdots{\boldsymbol{\pi}}_{r}
from
\mathcal{P}(r)
to
\mathcal{P}(0)
.
By
[
7
, Lemma 7.8.2]
, it acts on stable atoms by

(202)

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\pi}}_{1}\cdots{\boldsymbol{\pi}}_{r}\,{\mathcal{A}}_{\eta|\lambda}({\mathbf{x}})=\begin{cases}s_{(\eta;\lambda)}({\mathbf{x}})&\text{if $(\eta;\lambda)$ is weakly decreasing},\\
0&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Hence, similar to (
11
),
we have for
f\in\mathcal{P}(r)
,

(203)

\displaystyle\text{$f$ is stable atom positive}\implies\text{${\boldsymbol{\pi}}_{1}\cdots{\boldsymbol{\pi}}_{r}f$ is Schur positive},

and so stable atom positivity is a natural strengthening of Schur positivity to the space
\mathcal{P}(r)
.

We conjecture that the following elements of
\mathcal{P}(r)
on the modified side have coefficients in
{\mathbb{N}}[q,t]
when expanded in the basis of stable atoms.
Although part (i) of the conjecture implies (ii)–(iv), we have stated these separately in order to emphasize that each could potentially have its
own representation-theoretic significance.

Conjecture 8.5.3
.

(i) The flagged LLT polynomials
\mathcal{G}_{r,{\boldsymbol{\nu}}}({\mathbf{x}};t)
from §
5.3
are stable atom positive.
This includes in particular
\mathsf{G}_{r}(\pi,\Sigma)({\mathbf{x}};t)
and

\tilde{\mathsf{G}}_{r}(\pi,\Sigma)({\mathbf{x}};t)
from
Definitions
5.2.1
and
5.6.1
.

(ii) The modified
r
-nonsymmetric Macdonald polynomials
\check{\mathsf{H}}_{\eta|\lambda}
are stable atom positive.

(iii) The quantity
\boldsymbol{\nabla}^{-1}\mathsf{C}_{\alpha}
from Theorem
1.1.1
is stable atom positive.

(iv) The quantity
{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{r}\,\mathsf{C}_{m,n}^{\alpha}
from Theorem
6.3.1
is stable atom positive.

Parts (i) and (ii) were formulated in
[
7
]
,
where we also showed that (i) implies (ii) by
giving a formula for
\check{\mathsf{H}}_{\eta|\lambda}

as a positive sum of flagged LLT polynomials for tuples of ribbon skew shapes.
Note that by (
203
) and Proposition
7.2.3
, (ii) is a conjectural strengthening of Macdonald positivity.
By Theorems
1.1.1
and
6.3.1
,
(i) implies (iii) and (iv).

8.6.
Examples

Remark 8.6.1
.

(i) The following improvement on Remark
8.5.2
(i)–(ii) is useful for explicit computations:
the degree
d
subspace of
\mathcal{P}(r)
has a filtration
(\mathcal{P}(r)_{d_{1},d_{2}})_{d_{1},d_{2}\in{\mathbb{N}},\,d_{1}+d_{2}=d}

given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}(r)_{d_{1},d_{2}}\overset{\text{{def}}}{=}

\displaystyle\,\spn_{{\mathbb{Q}}(q,t)}\big\{x_{1}^{\eta_{1}}\cdots x_{r}^{\eta_{r}}s_{\lambda}(x_{r+1},x_{r+2},\dots):(\eta|\lambda)\in{\mathbb{N}}^{r}\times\Par,\,|\eta|\geq d_{1},\,|\eta|+|\lambda|=d\big\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\spn_{{\mathbb{Q}}(q,t)}\big\{x_{1}^{\eta_{1}}\cdots x_{r}^{\eta_{r}}s_{\lambda}({\mathbf{x}}):(\eta|\lambda)\in{\mathbb{N}}^{r}\times\Par,\,|\eta|\geq d_{1},\,|\eta|+|\lambda|=d\big\}.

Any
f({\mathbf{x}})\in\mathcal{P}(r)_{d_{1},d_{2}}
can be recovered from

f[{\mathbf{x}}_{N}]
for
N\geq d_{2}+r
via

f({\mathbf{x}})={\boldsymbol{\pi}}_{r+1}\cdots{\boldsymbol{\pi}}_{N}f[{\mathbf{x}}_{N}]
.
The subspace
\mathcal{P}(r)_{d_{1},d_{2}}
is spanned by the subset of stable atoms indexed by
(\eta|\lambda)
with
|\eta|\geq d_{1}
and
|\eta|+|\lambda|=d_{1}+d_{2}
.
Hence, as in Remark
8.5.2
(ii),
for any
f({\mathbf{x}})\in\mathcal{P}(r)_{d_{1},d_{2}}
and
N\geq d_{2}+r
,

f({\mathbf{x}})
is stable atom positive if and only if

f[{\mathbf{x}}_{N}]
is atom positive.

(ii)
Given (i), the following additional fact is useful for explicit computations:
for any
\pi\in{\mathbb{D}}_{r}(k)
and marking
\Sigma
of
\pi
,

(204)

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathsf{G}}_{r}(\pi,\Sigma)({\mathbf{x}};t)\in\mathcal{P}(r)_{r,k-r}.

In particular, each LLT term in (
193
)
(and therefore
\boldsymbol{\nabla}^{-m}\mathsf{C}_{\alpha}
as well) is determined by its evaluation in only the variables
x_{1},\dots,x_{k}
(
k=|\alpha|
is the degree of all these terms).
To prove (
204
), first note that the signed flagged column LLT polynomials have a formula in terms of non-attacking fillings analogous
to Lemma
5.4.3
, by
[
7
, Proposition 6.7.9]
. Hence,
x_{1}\cdots x_{r}
divides

\tilde{\mathsf{G}}^{\pm}_{r}(\pi,\Sigma)({\mathbf{x}};t)
, and so
\tilde{\mathsf{G}}^{\pm}_{r}(\pi,\Sigma)({\mathbf{x}};t)\in\mathcal{P}(r)_{r,k-r}
.
Then apply
{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{r}
to obtain (
204
), noting that
{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{r}
restricts to an isomorphism from
\mathcal{P}(r)_{d_{1},d_{2}}
to itself,
which is straightforward to check from the definition (
6
).

\omega\nabla\omega

Figure 2.
This diagram summarizes most of the notation in the paper.
Restricting each table to a fixed row gives a commutative
diagram between families of almost symmetric polynomials, e.g., restricting to (c)
gives (
142
).
Automorphisms given by curved arrows are related via intertwining relations implied by the diagram, e.g.,

\boldsymbol{\nabla}
and
\omega\nabla\omega
are automorphisms of

\mathcal{P}(r)
and
\mathcal{P}(0)
, respectively, and

{\boldsymbol{\pi}}_{1}\cdots{\boldsymbol{\pi}}_{r}\boldsymbol{\nabla}=\omega\nabla\omega{\boldsymbol{\pi}}_{1}\cdots{\boldsymbol{\pi}}_{r}
(see (
145
)). Another example is
given by
{\mathsf{P}}_{r}\bar{\nabla}=(-1)^{d}\widecheck{\nabla}{\mathsf{P}}_{r}
(see (
161
)).
